Uncertainty and entropies of classical channels
Takla Nateeboon

Abstract

Abstract

In this thesis, I studied a mathematical development to define and quantify the uncertainty inherent in classical channels.
This thesis starts with the introduction and background on how to formally think about uncertainty in the domain of classical states. The concept of probability vector majorization and its variants, relative majorization and conditional majorization, are reviewed. This thesis introduces three conceptually distinct approaches to formalize the notion of uncertainty inherent in classical channels. These three approaches define the same preordering on the domain of classical channels, leading to characterizations from many perspectives. With the solid foundation of uncertainty comparison, classical channel entropy is then defined to be an additive monotone with respect to the majorization relation. The well-known entropies in the domain of classical states are uniquely extended to the domain of channels via the optimal extensions, providing not only a solid foundation but also the quantifiers of uncertainty inherent in classical channels.

[
1
]
. Some figures have previously appeared or have been adapted from the published manuscript.

Chapter 0
Introduction

Throughout this thesis, we shall concern ourselves with the study which stems from the development of information theory, where in this thesis we refer to it as the
classical
information theory. Unlike any physical theories, classical information theory distances itself from a specific physical system by abstracting the information as a currency of ability to perform tasks, for example, sending a message, encoding, and decoding. Centered on this field study is entropy, especially Shannon entropy, which is proposed to reflect how much information one can obtain from a random variable
[
2
]
. The quantity of entropy itself depends on the probability distribution of the random variable of interest. The theory has been successful so far in describing and giving insight into communication, cryptography, statistical testing, and more disciplines
[
3
,
4
]
.

Quantum information theory has emerged as a natural progression from classical information theory. Paradoxically, the development of the physical theory, known as quantum theory, has led to a paradigm shift in the physical-independent theory of information theory. Random variables and their probability distributions, once considered the most fundamental objects, have been replaced by quantum states, or in certain contexts, referred to as density operators. This shift has profound implications. A notable example is quantum entanglement, a property where two physical systems cannot be fully described individually, despite appearing to be physically separated. Entanglement enables the formation of correlations beyond the capabilities of any classical correlation, leading to the development of new capabilities.

Despite the powerful capability inherent in quantum properties, many implementations are hard to realize as there are physical constraints. In the notable example of entanglement, the creation and maintaining of such properties have been challenging. This frames entanglement as a valuable resource, which one cannot easily create, hence it requires optimally utilizing the available resource. Similar situations also appear in coherence
[
5
,
6
,
7
,
8
]
, purity
[
9
]
, and athermality
[
10
,
11
]
as well. These constraints lead to the study of multiple quantum resource theories.

Although there is a shift in the nature of information from classical to quantum, concepts in classical information theory extend to the quantum regime. The von Neumann entropy, which was previously devised to put quantum mechanics into a thermodynamic perspective
[
12
,
13
]
, turns out to be applicable in quantum information theory. The von Neumann entropy extends Shannon entropy from the domain of classical random variables, where we will refer to them as classical states, to the domain of quantum states. The entropy not only quantifies information contained in a quantum state but also provides a measure of resource in multiple resource theories, including quantum resource of entanglement
[
14
,
15
,
16
,
17
]
, purity
[
9
]
, and coherence
[
18
,
8
]
.

Shannon entropy and von Neumann entropy are said to be measures of uncertainty. But what makes entropy a measure of uncertainty? Can we have a more varied set of entropies? The more general approach is to define uncertainty by a preorder, namely, majorization. Independently of information theory and physics, the majorization itself is a concept developed to quantify how much the wealth distribution spread out
[
19
]
. The key characterization of majorization with a convertibility via
noisy
operation leads to the motivation to ground the definition of entropy with the majorization
[
20
]
. The development of the field in the past decades, 2010s, has shown that not only can we define entropy as an additive monotone (in more strict term, an additive antitone) of majorization, but also we can do with relative entropies and conditional entropies
[
21
,
22
,
23
]
, for both classical and quantum domains. Majorization also serves as a quintessential tool in quantum information theory. For example, majorization is used in describing the uncertainty relation between non-commuting observables
[
24
,
25
]
, classical and quantum thermodynamics
[
26
,
10
,
11
]
, and many quantum resource theories
[
27
,
8
]
. Most prominently, Nielsen’s majorization theorem
[
15
,
17
]
gives a sufficient and necessary condition of converting quantum state via local operation and classical communication by majorization relation.

The recognition of what constitutes a resource in quantum theories extends beyond static resources of quantum states. The operations on quantum states, quantum channels, have been recognized as resources
[
28
,
29
,
30
,
31
,
32
,
33
,
34
]
, leading to the development of dynamical resource theories
[
35
,
36
,
37
,
38
,
39
]
. Recently, a dynamical resource has been experimentally measured in
[
40
]
. Taking an example from the entanglement theories, one can consume a maximally entangled state, which is a static resource, to perform a teleportation
[
41
]
or dense coding protocols
[
42
]
. These operations can be viewed as a creation of a teleportation channel or a dense coding channel with an entanglement pair, rendering such channels as a resourceful object. On the contrary, if one has access a bipartite channel that can perform non-LOCC operation, then one can use such a channel instead of consuming an entanglement pair in every operation. This is an example that put quantum channels as a resourceful object in its own right.

Similar questions occurred in the dynamical resource theories. Can quantum channels have entropy? The assumption was that they can. The earlier attempts
[
43
,
44
,
45
]
to define quantum channel entropy are done with divergences or relative entropies of quantum channels, which measure a degree of distinguishability between any two quantum channels. The quantum channel entropy is then defined to be a measure of the distance of a quantum channel from the totally mixed channel, a channel that always produces a maximally mixed state regardless of the input. Questions still remain. What does it mean for one quantum channel to have more entropy compared to another channel? What is the uncertainty of a quantum channel? What is the more fundamental rationale for using such a definition as the entropy of a quantum channel?

To answer these questions, this thesis approaches from and focuses on the classical channel domain, a domain of quantum channels where the output is incoherent and completely independent of the coherent part of the quantum state. We will attempt to define the uncertainty of classical channels via a preorder. This preorder should be devised from convertibility using
noisy
operations, which will provide a foundational and operational interpretation of what uncertainty is in the channel domain. Then, we will define an entropy, similar to quantum and classical states’ entropies, to be an additive monotone of classical channel majorization.

To do this, we firstly begin with the review of necessary mathematical concepts and tools in Chapter 1. Starting with the framework of quantum information theory: Hilbert spaces, operators, quantum states, quantum channels, and quantum superchannels.
In Chapter 2, we provide a review of entropy measures on classical states and its foundational preorder, including majorization, relative majorization, and the conditional majorization.
Our original contribution begins in Chapter 3, where we provide our answer to the main question of this thesis: What is the uncertainty of a classical channel? Along this, we characterize the uncertainty via multiple mathematical perspectives. In Chapter 4, we define the entropy of a classical channel as a function reflecting the uncertainty of a classical channel. We provide examples of entropy functions and provide a discussion on the uniqueness of functions extending entropies. Lastly, we conclude our answer and provide a look ahead in Chapter 5.

Organization

Throughout this thesis, we display the important definitions and propositions with color boxes:
orange
boxes are for theorems, lemmas, or propositions and
pink
boxes are for definitions. The complementary definitions and propositions are presented in the usual definition, lemma, or theorem environment without a box.

Chapter 1
Mathematical framework for quantum information theory

1
Mathematical path to quantum theory

Quantum theory is a physical theory that describes a physical system. In quantum theory, a physical system, denoted by
A
, is described by a
Hilbert space

\mathscr{H}_{A}
. One of the fundamental objects of interest is a quantum state, which is analogue to position and momentum in classical mechanics. A quantum state is built on a mathematical object called
wavefunction
. A wavefunction
\psi
belongs to the Hilbert space
\mathscr{H}_{A}
. For convenience, we will use
A
to denote both the physical system and the associated Hilbert space. We denote a set of complex or real numbers with
\mathbb{F}
.

To begin with, a Hilbert space is a complete inner product space. An inner product space is defined as follows.

Definition 1.1

(Inner Product Space)
.

An inner product space is a vector space
V
over the field
\mathbb{F}
equipped with a binary map
\innerproduct{\parm}{\parm}:V\crossproduct V\to\mathbb{F}
such that
\forall\phi,\psi,\chi\in V
and
a,b\in\mathbb{F}
the followings holds.

1.

Conjugate Symmetry:
\innerproduct{\phi}{\psi}=\overline{\innerproduct{\psi}{\phi}}

2.

Linearity in the second argument:
\innerproduct{\phi}{a\psi+b\chi}=a\innerproduct{\phi}{\psi}+b\innerproduct{\phi}{\chi}

3.

Positive-definiteness:
\innerproduct{\phi}{\phi}>0
except at
\phi=0
,
\innerproduct{\phi}{\phi}=0

An inner product space
(V,\innerproduct{\parm}{\parm})
is said to be complete if any Cauchy sequence in
V
converges to an element in
V
. The Cauchy sequence is defined with respect to metric
d
, which is induced from a norm,
d(x,y)=\norm{x-y}
.

Definition 1.2

(Norm)
.

A norm is a mapping from a vector space
V
to a real number, denoted by
\norm{\parm}:V\to\mathbb{R}
, that satisfies the following properties for all
a\in\mathbb{F}
and
\psi,\phi\in V
.

1.

\norm{\psi}>0
except when
\psi=0
we have
\norm{\psi}=0
.

2.

\norm{a\psi}=\absolutevalue{a}\norm{\psi}
.

3.

Triangle inequality:
\norm{\psi+\phi}\leq\norm{\psi}+\norm{\phi}
.

Now, we can define Hilbert space

Definition 1.3
.

A Hilbert space is a vector space
\mathscr{H}
over a field
\mathbb{F}
endowed with an inner product which is complete as a metric space.

In this thesis, we work exclusively on the finite dimensional Hilbert space. A dimension of a Hilbert space is defined from the cardinality of its basis.

1
Dirac notation

Definition 1.4
.

Given a vector space
\mathscr{H}
over a field
\mathbb{F}
. Its dual space
\mathscr{H}^{*}
is a set of all linear functionals
f:V\to\mathbb{F}
,

\mathscr{H}^{*}=\quantity{f:\scr{H}\to\bb{F}~\text{linear}}

In our particular case of quantum theory, we are interested in finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces. These spaces come with a special property that all linear functionals are representable with matrices, hence bounded and continuous. By the Riesz representation theorem, any elements in the dual
f\in\mathscr{H}^{*}
can be identified uniquely with an element
\psi_{f}\in\mathscr{H}
, in exactly the following form

f(\phi)=\innerproduct{\psi_f}{\phi}.

Indeed, any
\psi\in\mathscr{H}
can be identified with a linear function
f_{\psi}(\phi)=\innerproduct{\psi}{\phi}
, establishing an isomorphism between the original and the dual. This motivates the bra-ket notation to be used. For a wavefunction
\phi\in A
, the ket of
\phi
,
\ket{\phi}
, denotes
\phi\in A
, which is the wavefunction itself. On the other hand, the bra of
\phi
,
\bra{\phi}
, denotes the dual of
\phi
.

Within the dirac notation, since
\ket{\phi}
denotes an element in the original function, the expression
\innerproduct{\phi}{M\psi}
is often expressed as
\bra{\phi}M\ket{\psi}
, and equivalently
\innerproduct{M^* \phi}{\psi}
. Note that in
\innerproduct{\phi}{M\psi}
and
\bra{\phi}M\ket{\psi}
the inner product are between two elements in
B
while
\innerproduct{M^* \phi}{\psi}
is between two elements in
A
.

2
Multipartite systems

One of the key tasks in quantum information theory is to include or discard physical systems from consideration. The inclusion of a physical system into consideration is represented by taking the tensor product of two Hilbert spaces, e.g. a Hilbert space
A
and a Hilbert space
B
. The product space is denoted by
A\otimes B
or simply by their concatenation
AB
.

In the finite dimensional case, which this thesis is based on, we have that the Kronecker product is the corresponding product between vectors from two Hilbert spaces.

On the other hand, the discarding of a physical system is represented by taking a trace or partial trace. Formally, it is defined as

\Tr_{A}\bqty{\rho^{AB}}=\sum_{x\in[n]}(\bra{x}\otimes I^{B})\rho^{AB}(\ket{x}\otimes I^{B}).

3
Operators on Hilbert space

A set of all linear operators from
A
to
B
is denoted with
\mathfrak{L}(A,B)
. A set of all linear operators onto itself, e.g., from
A
to
A
is denoted with
\mathfrak{L}(A)
. In quantum theory, there are two important classes of operators: Unitary operators and Hermitian operators. Both are defined on their relations with their adjoint.

Definition 1.5

(Adjoint of an operator)
.

Suppose
M\in\mathfrak{L}(A,B)
. An operator
M^{*}\in\mathfrak{L}(B,A)
is an adjoint of
M
if
\innerproduct{\phi}{M \psi}=\innerproduct{M^* \phi}{\psi}
for all
\phi\in B
and all
\psi\in A
.

Lemma 1.6
.

Given an operator
M\in\mathfrak{L}(A,B)
, its adjoint is unique.

Proof.

Suppose
N
and
M^{*}
are adjoints of
M
, not necessarily distinct. We have the following equalities for all
\phi\in B
and
\psi\in A
,

\innerproduct{\phi}{M\psi}=\innerproduct{M^* \phi}{\psi}=\innerproduct{N \phi}{\psi}.

That is
\innerproduct{M^* \phi- N \phi}{\psi}=0
for all
\psi\in A
. This implies
M^{*}\phi-N\phi=0
. Since this is for all
\phi\in B
, we have that
N=M^{*}
, proving the uniqueness.
∎

Suppose
A
is a finite-dimensional Hilbert space, and each operator
V\in\mathfrak{L}(A,B)
has a matrix representation. To create a matrix representation, we prescribe bases in which we would like to write this matrix in. For example, suppose
\quantity{\phi_x}
be an orthonormal basis of
A
and
\quantity{\psi_x}
be an orthonormal basis of
B
. A matrix
M
represents
V
in this basis is

M=\sum_{x,x^{\prime}}\bra{\psi_x}V\ket{\phi_{x'}}\outerproduct{\psi_x}{\phi_{x'}}.

(1)

4
Important classes of operators on Hilbert space and their properties

Now, we define the three important classes of operators.

Definition 1.7

(Unitary operator)
.

An operator
U\in\mathfrak{L}(A)
is unitary if its adjoint is its inverse. In symbol,
U
is unitary if

U^{*}=U^{-1}.

Every unitary matrix can be written as

U=\sum_{x\in[n]}\outerproduct{\phi_x}{\psi_x}

where
\quantity{\phi_x:x\in[n]}
and
\quantity{\psi_x : x \in[n]}
each is an orthonormal basis of
A
and
B
respectively.

A class of unitary operators is contained within a class of isometry operators. An isometry is an operator that preserves an inner product between two vectors.

Definition 1.8
.

An operator
V\in\mathfrak{L}(A,B)
is an isometry if for all
\phi,\psi\in A

\innerproduct{ V \phi}{V \psi}=\innerproduct{\phi}{\psi}.

Moreover, if
V
is an isometry, we have that
V^{*}V=I
. This follows from
\innerproduct{\phi}{\psi}=\innerproduct{V\phi}{V\psi}=\bra{\phi}V^{*}V\ket{\psi}
for all
\phi,\psi\in A
. Applying a matrix representation in the Equation (
1
) will show that an identity matrix is a matrix representing
V^{*}V
.

Similar to unitary operator, for any isometry
V\in\mathfrak{L}\pqty{A,B}
,

V=\sum_{x\in[n]}\outerproduct{\phi_x}{\psi_x}

where
\quantity{\phi_x}
is an orthonormal set, need not span
B
, i.e. may not be a basis.

Definition 1.9

(Hermitian matrix)
.

An operator
H\in\mathfrak{L}\pqty{A,B}
is Hermitian, or in more modern terms self-adjoint, if its adjoint is itself. In symbols,
H
is Hermitian if

H^{*}=H.

For a Hermitian matrix, we have that
H
has a spectral decomposition

H=\sum_{x\in[n]}\lambda\outerproduct{\psi_\lambda}{\psi_\lambda}

where
\quantity{\psi_\lambda}
form an orthonormal basis of
\mathscr{H}
. That is, every Hermitian matrix is diagonalizable with an orthonormal basis.

2
Quantum state

A quantum state is represented by a density matrix. It describes the property of a quantum object, e.g. an electron and possible energy levels, positions, or momenta.

Definition 2.1
.

A
quantum state

\rho
on the Hilbert space
A
is a Hermitian operator with the following properties,

•

Positive semidefinite:
\forall\psi\in A
we have that
\bra{\psi}\rho\ket{\psi}\geq 0

•

Trace one:
\Tr[\rho]=1

We denote the set of all density matrices on a system
A
with
\mathfrak{D}(A)
.

We denote a quantum state
\rho
on system
A
as
\rho^{A}
. This is to signify its corresponding Hilbert space.

Any quantum state
\rho
has a spectral decomposition of the form

\rho=\sum_{i\in[m]}p_{i}\outerproduct{\psi_i}{\psi_i};\quad\text{$\sum_{i\in[m]}p_{i}=1$;$\quad\forall i\in[m]$, $p_{i}\geq 0$ }

(2)

where
m
is the dimension of the Hilbert space
A
. This leads to an interpretation that the quantum state is a probability mixture of wavefunctions. In particular, we call

\quantity{(p_x, \phi_x) : \phi_x \in\rm{PURE}(A), ~p_x \in[0,1], ~\sum_{x\in[m]} p_x = 1}

a probabilistic ensemble of wavefunctions. With the ensemble, there is a unique density matrix related to it. However, it is not true in general that given a density matrix, a wavefunction ensemble is unique.

1
Pure states, mixed states, and purifications

A pure state describes perfect knowledge of what quantum state the system is in. A mixed state, however, describes an ensemble of possible quantum states.

Definition 2.2
.

A quantum state
\rho\in\mathfrak{D}
is
pure
if
\Tr[\rho^{2}]=1
. Otherwise,
\Tr[\rho^{2}]<1
and the quantum state
\rho
is said to be
mixed
.

However, given a mixed state
\rho^{A}
one can always find a pure state
\psi^{AB}
such that its partial trace
\Tr_{B}[\psi^{AB}]=\rho^{A}
. Such a pure state is said to be a
purification
of
\rho^{A}
. An example of the purification of
\rho^{A}
is

\ket{\psi}^{\tilde{A}A}=\sqrt{\rho}^{\tilde{A}}\otimes I^{{A}}(\ket{\Phi}^{\tilde{A}A})

(3)

where
\ket{\Phi}^{\tilde{A}A}
is called maximally entangled state and has the form

\ket{\Phi}^{\tilde{A}A}=\sum_{x\in[\absolutevalue{A}]}\ket{x}^{\tilde{A}}\otimes\ket{x}^{A}.

In fact, any two purifications of the same quantum state are related through an isometry channel, a type of linear map between quantum states which we will introduce in the next section. To see that this is a purification, tracing out
\tilde{A}
will lead to

\displaystyle\Tr_{\tilde{A}}[\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}^{\tilde{A}A}]

\displaystyle=\Tr_{\tilde{A}}\bqty{(\sqrt{\rho^T}^{\tilde{A}} \otimes I^{{A}})\ket{\Phi}^{\tilde{A}{A}} \bra{\Phi}^{\tilde{A}A}(\sqrt{\rho^T}^{\tilde{A}} \otimes I^{{A}})}

\displaystyle=\Tr_{\tilde{A}}\bqty{(\sqrt{\rho^T}^{\tilde{A}} \otimes I^{{A}})\pqty{\sum_{x,y\in[n]} \ketbra{x}{y}^{\tilde{A}} \otimes\ketbra{x}{y}^{A}}(\sqrt{\rho^T}^{\tilde{A}} \otimes I^{{A}})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{x,y\in[n]}\Tr\bqty{\sqrt{\rho^T}\ketbra{x}{y}\sqrt{\rho^T}~}\outerproduct{x}{y}^{A}

\displaystyle=\sum_{x,y\in[n]}\bra{y}\rho^{T}\ket{x}\outerproduct{x}{y}^{A}=\sum_{x,y\in[n]}\rho_{xy}\outerproduct{x}{y}^{A}=\rho^{A}.

Similar to Equation (
3
), any bipartite state
\ket{\psi}^{AB}
can be written as a product of the maximally entangled state
\ket{\Phi}^{\tilde{B}B}
with some matrix
M\otimes I
where
M\in\mathfrak{L}(\tilde{B},A)
. Suppose
\absolutevalue{A}=n
and
\absolutevalue{B}=m
. Suppose that

\ket{\psi}^{AB}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in[n]\\
y\in[m]\end{subarray}}c_{xy}\ket{x}^{A}\otimes\ket{y}^{B}.

Define
M
to be

M=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in[n]\\
y^{\prime}\in[m]\end{subarray}}c_{xy^{\prime}}\outerproduct{x}{y'}.

Then, it is straightforward to verify that

\ket{\psi}^{AB}=(M\otimes I)\ket{\Phi}^{\tilde{B}B}.

(4)

2
Probability distribution from quantum state

A probability distribution can be obtained from a quantum state via
measurement
. Mathematically, measurement is defined as a set of matrices satisfying a condition.

Definition 2.3

(Measurement)
.

A set of matrices
\quantity{M_x:x\in[n]}\subset\mathbb{C}^{\ell\times m}
is a
measurement
if

\sum_{x\in[n]}M^{*}_{x}M_{x}=I^{(m)}.

A measurement maps a quantum state
\rho
to the measurement outcome
\sigma_{x}

\sigma_{x}=\frac{1}{p_{x}}M_{x}\rho M^{*}_{x}\quad\mathrm{where}~p_{x}=\Tr[M_{x}\rho M^{*}_{x}].

Then, it is easy to see that
\sum_{x\in[n]}\Tr[M^{*}_{x}M_{x}\rho]=\Tr\bqty{\sum_{x\in[n]} M^* _x M_x \rho}=\Tr[\rho]=1
. Since
M^{*}_{x}M_{x}
is Hermitian, its eigenvalues are real numbers. Moreover, for any
\rho\in\mathfrak{D}(A)
,
\Tr[M^{*}M\rho]
is positive since it is a product of
M
and
M^{*}
. For each
x\in[n]
, we interpret
p_{x}
to be a probability in which a quantum state after the measurement is
\sigma_{x}
.

3
Classical state

A notion of quantum state extends a classical state. In terms of density operators, a classical state is a quantum state that is diagonal with respect to a particular orthonormal basis. This particular orthonormal basis is often referred to as the classical basis, which is picked depending on the physical systems and physical laws describing the system.

For example, a quantum communication using photonic qubit encodes information in 2 distinct states, says the states
\ket{0}
and
\ket{1}
. The measurement device in this setup measures a quantum state sent in the
\quantity{\ket{0},\ket{1}}
basis. Notice that a diagonal matrix in this basis may not be diagonal in other bases. For example,
\outerproduct{0}{0}
is diagonal in this basis but not in
\quantity{\ket{+},\ket{-}}
basis, where
\ket{\pm}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\pqty{\ket{0}\pm\ket{1}}
.

1
Probability vectors

Since any classical state
\rho\in\mathfrak{D}(X)
can be written as a diagonal matrix, it can be put in a probability vector of dimension equal to
\absolutevalue{X}
. For example, a classical state
\rho^{X}=\sum_{x\in[n]}p_{x}\outerproduct{x}{x}^{X}
where
n=\absolutevalue{X}
. Then, a probability vector associated with a classical state is

\mathbf{p}=\sum_{x\in[n]}p_{x}\mathbf{e}_{x}.

We denoted by
\mathrm{Prob}(n)
a set of all probabilities of dimension
n
. A probability vector is a non-negative vector with the sum of all entries equal to one.

4
Quantum channel

A quantum channel is a linear map with extra properties that always maps a quantum state to a quantum state. These properties will be introduced shortly in a formal definition. The linearity requirement comes from the preservation of the probabilistic interpretation of the quantum state. Suppose that one has an ensemble of pure quantum states,

\quantity{(p_x, \psi_x) :x\in[n], p_x \in[0,1],\sum_{x\in[n]} p_x = 1, \psi_x \in\rm{PURE}(A)}.

Mapping the quantum state
\rho=\sum_{x\in[n]}p_{x}\psi_{x}
associated with this ensemble, by a channel
\mathcal{E}
must result identically to mapping the pure quantum state in the ensemble individually. We denote
\mathfrak{L}(\mathfrak{L}(A),\mathfrak{L}(B))
with
\mathfrak{L}(A\to B)
.

Definition 4.1

(Quantum channel)
.

Quantum channel
\mathcal{E}^{A\to B}
is a linear map
\mathfrak{L}(A\to B)
, which is

•

completely-positive (CP): for any Hilbert space
R
, an action of the channel on a positive operator
\rho^{RA}\geq 0
results in a positive operator. That is

\mathsf{id}^{R}\otimes\mathcal{E}^{A\to B}(\rho^{RA})\geq 0.

•

trace-preserving (TP): for any operator
\sigma^{A}\in\mathfrak{L}(A)
, we have that

\Tr[\mathcal{E}^{A\to B}(\sigma^{A})]=\Tr[\sigma^{A}]

The set of all quantum channels in
\mathfrak{L}(\mathfrak{L}(A),\mathfrak{L}(B))
is denoted by
\mathrm{CPTP}(A\to B)
. The question is how one could verify these properties? One of the approaches is to characterize it with the Choi-Jamiołkowski isomorphism, which is an isomorphism between maps of quantum channel types and matrices.

1
Choi-Jamiołkowski isomorphism between channel and state

Definition 4.2

(A Choi matrix)
.

Suppose
\mathcal{E}\in\mathrm{CPTP}(A\to B)
is a quantum channel. Suppose
\absolutevalue{A}=n
and
\absolutevalue{B}=m
. A
Choi matrix
denoted with
J_{\mathcal{E}}\in\mathbb{C}^{mn\times mn}
is defined to be an application of the channel
\mathcal{E}
on the unnormalized maximally entangled state,
\Phi^{A\tilde{A}}=\sum_{i,j\in[n]}\outerproduct{i,i}{j,j}
.

J_{\mathcal{E}}^{AB}=\mathsf{id}^{A\to A}\otimes\mathcal{E}^{\tilde{A}\to B}(\Phi^{A\tilde{A}}),

where
\mathsf{id}^{A\to A}
is an identity channel.

One can compute an action of channel
\mathcal{E}^{A\to B}
using its Choi matrix
J_{\mathcal{E}}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{A\to B}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\Tr_{A}[J_{\mathcal{E}}(\rho^{T}\otimes I)].

To see this, consider the right side of the equation

\displaystyle\Tr_{A}[J_{\mathcal{E}}(\rho^{T}\otimes I)]

\displaystyle=\Tr_{A}\bqty{\id^{A} \otimes\cal{E}^{\tilde{A}\to B} (\Phi^{A\tilde{A}}) (\rho^T \otimes I)}

\displaystyle=\Tr_{A}\bqty{\sum_{x,y} \rho^{T} \ketbra{x}{y}^A \otimes\cal{E}^{\tilde{A}\to B}(\ketbra{x}{y}^{\tilde{A}})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{x,y}\Tr[\rho^{T}\outerproduct{x}{y}^{A}]\mathcal{E}^{\tilde{A}\to B}(\outerproduct{x}{y}^{\tilde{A}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{x,y}\rho_{xy}\mathcal{E}(\outerproduct{x}{y})=\mathcal{E}(\rho).

Characterizations of quantum channels

Theorem 4.3
.

A linear map
\mathcal{E}\in\mathfrak{L}(A\to B)
is completely positive if and only if its Choi matrix
J_{\mathcal{E}}^{AB}
is positive semidefinite.

Proof.

Suppose that
\mathcal{E}
is completely positive. Its Choi matrix is

\displaystyle J^{AB}_{\mathcal{E}}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{id}^{A\to A}\otimes\mathcal{E}^{\tilde{A}\to B}(\Phi^{A\tilde{A}})\geq 0

where the positive semidefiniteness follows directly from the definition of completely positive.

Conversely, if
J^{AB}_{\mathcal{E}}\geq 0
. Suppose
R
is an auxiliary system and
\ket{\psi}^{RA}
is a bipartite wavefunction on the product Hilbert space
RA
. Suppose
M\in\mathfrak{L}(\tilde{A},R)
is a matrix such that

\ket{\psi}^{RA}=(M\otimes I)\ket{\Phi}^{\tilde{A}A}.

Furthermore, we define a linear map
\mathcal{M}\in\mathfrak{L}(A\to R)
to be
\mathcal{M}(\rho^{A})=M\rho M^{*}
.
To check complete positivity, consider

\displaystyle\mathsf{id}^{R\to R}\otimes\mathcal{E}(\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}^{RA})

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{id}^{R\to R}\otimes\mathcal{E})\circ(\mathcal{M}\otimes\mathsf{id}^{A\to A})(\Phi^{\tilde{A}A})

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{M}\otimes\mathsf{id}^{B\to B})\circ(\mathsf{id}^{R\to R}\otimes\mathcal{E})(\Phi^{\tilde{A}A})

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{M}\otimes\mathsf{id}^{B\to B})J^{AB}_{\mathcal{E}}.

Next is to show that
(\mathcal{M}\otimes\mathsf{id}^{B\to B})J^{AB}_{\mathcal{E}}
is positive semidefinite. For any
\psi\in RB
consider

\displaystyle\bra{\psi}(\mathcal{M}\otimes\mathsf{id}^{B\to B})J^{AB}_{\mathcal{E}}\ket{\psi}

\displaystyle=\bra{\psi}(M\otimes I)J^{AB}_{\mathcal{E}}(M^{*}\otimes I)\ket{\psi}

\displaystyle=\bra{(M^*\otimes I) \psi}J^{AB}_{\mathcal{E}}\ket{(M^*\otimes I)\psi}\geq 0,

since
J^{AB}_{\mathcal{E}}\geq 0
and
(M^{*}\otimes I)\psi^{RA}\in AB
.
∎

Theorem 4.4
.

A linear map
\mathcal{E}\in\mathfrak{L}(A\to B)
is trace-preserving if and only if the marginal of its Choi matrix
J_{\mathcal{E}}^{A}=\Tr_{B}[J_{\mathcal{E}}]
is an identity matrix on
A
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\mathcal{E}
is trace-preserving. Then its Choi matrix has a partial trace on
B
as

\displaystyle\Tr_{B}[J^{AB}_{\mathcal{E}}]

\displaystyle=\Tr_{B}[\mathsf{id}^{A\to A}\otimes\mathcal{E}^{\tilde{A}\to B}(\Phi^{A\tilde{A}})]

\displaystyle=\Tr_{B}[J^{AB}_{\mathcal{E}}]=\sum_{x,y\in[n]}\Tr[\mathcal{E}^{\tilde{A}\to B}(\outerproduct{x}{y})]\outerproduct{x}{y}

\displaystyle=\sum_{x,y\in[n]}\Tr[\outerproduct{x}{y}]\outerproduct{x}{y}=\sum_{x,y\in[n]}\delta_{xy}\outerproduct{x}{y}=I^{A},

where
\delta_{xy}
is equal to one if
x=y
and is equal to zero otherwise and
\absolutevalue{A}=n
.

Conversely, suppose
J^{A}_{\mathcal{E}}=I^{A}
and
m=\absolutevalue{B}
. The trace of
\mathcal{E}(\rho)
is

\displaystyle\Tr[\mathcal{E}(\rho)]

\displaystyle=\Tr_{B}[\Tr_{A}[J^{AB}_{\mathcal{E}}(\rho^{T}\otimes I^{B})]]

\displaystyle=\Tr[\sum_{y\in[m]}(I^{A}\otimes\bra{y}^{B})J^{AB}_{\mathcal{E}}(I^{A}\otimes\ket{y}^{B})(\rho^{T})]

\displaystyle=\Tr[\Tr_{B}[J^{AB}_{\mathcal{E}}](\rho^{T})]=\Tr[I^{A}\rho^{T}]=\Tr[\rho],

showing trace-preserving property of
\mathcal{E}
.
∎

2
Notable examples of quantum channels

Isometry channel

Suppose
V:\mathfrak{L}(A,B)
is an isometry matrix. An isometry channel
\mathcal{V}
is defined as

\mathcal{V}(\rho^{A})=V\rho^{A}V^{*}.

The map
\mathcal{V}
is completely positive and trace-preserving. For complete positivity, notice that an isometry channel is a specific case of the channel
\mathcal{M}
defined in the proof of Theorem
4.3
.
For trace-preserving, it is straightforwardly

\displaystyle\Tr[\mathcal{V}(\rho)]=\Tr[V\rho V^{*}]=\Tr[V^{*}V\rho]=\Tr[\rho].

Discarding a system

Tracing a system out is completely positive and trace-preserving. An act of tracing out a specific system,
\Tr_{A}
is a linear map in
\mathfrak{L}(A\to\mathbb{C})
. Its Choi matrix is

J_{\mathrm{Tr}}^{A}=\Tr_{\tilde{A}}[\Phi^{A\tilde{A}}]=I^{A}\geq 0,

showing that
\Tr_{A}
is completely-positive. For trace-preserving, it is trivially followed from
\Tr[\rho]
giving exactly the trace of the operator
\rho
.

For the partial trace, its complete-positivity and trace-preserving follow directly from the complete-positivity and trace-preserving of the trace.

Any quantum channel
\mathcal{E}
can be implemented with an isometry and the discarding of a physical system. This is due to the well-known Stinespring dilation theorem
[
46
]
.

Theorem 4.5

(Stinespring dilation)
.

A channel
\mathcal{E}\in\mathrm{CPTP}(A\to B)
can be expressed as a composition of an isometry channel
\mathcal{V}\in\mathrm{CPTP}(A\to BR)
and the discarding of an auxiliary system
R
.

We will follow the proof that is laid out in
[
47
]
. This start with introducing an operator sum representation (Kraus representation) of a quantum channel
[
48
]
.

Theorem 4.6

(Krause representation)
.

A quantum channel
\mathcal{E}\in\mathrm{CPTP}(A\to B)
can be expressed as

\mathcal{E}(\rho)=\sum_{x\in[n]}M_{x}\rho M^{*}_{x}

where
n=\absolutevalue{AB}
and
\quantity{M_x :M_x \in\mathfrak{L}(A\to B), x\in[n]}
is a set of matrices.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{E}
is a quantum channel, its Choi matrix is positive semidefinite, hence Hermitian and diagonalizable,

J^{AB}_{\mathcal{E}}=\sum_{x\in[n]}\lambda_{x}\outerproduct{x}{x}

where
\quantity{x}
is an orthonormal basis of
AB
where the Choi matrix is diagonal. Absorbing the factor
\lambda_{x}
into both vector
\ket{x}
and
\bra{x}
, rename it
\ket{\phi_x}
we have,

J^{AB}_{\mathcal{E}}=\sum_{x\in[n]}\outerproduct{\phi_x}{\phi_x}.

Similarly to the case of a bipartite wavefunction, we can rewrite
\ket{\phi_x}
as

\ket{\phi_x}=M_{x}\otimes I\ket{\Phi}^{A\tilde{A}}.

The action of the channel
\mathcal{E}
is expressed in terms of the Choi matrix as

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in[n]}\Tr_{A}[(I^{A}\otimes M_{x})\Phi^{A\tilde{A}}(I^{A}\otimes M_{x}^{*})(\rho^{T}\otimes I)]

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in[n]}\Tr_{A}[(I^{A}\otimes M_{x})\Phi^{A\tilde{A}}(\rho^{T}\otimes I)(I^{A}\otimes M_{x}^{*})]

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in[n]}M_{x}\Tr_{A}[\Phi^{A\tilde{A}}(I\otimes\rho)]M^{*}_{x}=\sum_{x\in[n]}M_{x}\rho M_{x}^{*}.

∎

Corollary 4.7
.

A linear map
\mathcal{E}\in\mathfrak{L}(A\to B)
defined as

\mathcal{E}(\rho)=\sum_{x\in[n]}M_{x}\rho M^{*}_{x}

is trace-preserving if and only if
\sum_{x\in[n]}M_{x}^{*}M_{x}=I

Proof.

The map
\mathcal{E}
is trace-preserving if and only if its Choi has a marginal be
I^{A}
on
A
. That is,

\displaystyle I^{A}

\displaystyle=\Tr_{B}[J^{AB}_{\mathcal{E}}]=\Tr_{B}[\sum_{x\in[n]}(I\otimes M_{x})\Phi^{A\tilde{A}}(I\otimes M^{*}_{x})]

\displaystyle=\Tr_{B}[\sum_{x\in[n]}(M_{x}^{T}\otimes I)\Phi^{\tilde{B}B}((M^{*}_{x})^{T}\otimes I)]=\sum_{x\in[n]}M_{x}^{T}(M^{*}_{x})^{T}=\sum_{x\in[n]}M_{x}^{*}M_{x}.

∎

Proof of Theorem
4.5
.

Such isometry is

V=\sum_{x\in[n]}M_{x}\otimes\ket{x}^{R}

where
\quantity{\ket{x}^R}
is an orthonormal basis for
R
. Notice that
R
can be set to have dimension
\absolutevalue{AB}
. This is an isometry, because

\displaystyle V^{*}V=\sum_{x,y\in[n]}M^{*}_{y}M_{x}\innerproduct{y}{x}=\sum_{x\in[n]}M_{x}^{*}M_{x}=I.

To see that this representation emulates the channel
\mathcal{E}
, consider

\displaystyle\Tr_{R}

\displaystyle\bqty{\pqty{\sum_{x\in[n]} M_x \otimes\ket{x}^R} \rho\pqty{\sum_{y\in[n]} M_y \otimes\ket{y}^R}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in[n]}\Tr_{R}\bqty{M_x \rho M^* _y \otimes\ketbra{x}{y}^R}

\displaystyle=\sum_{x,y\in[n]}M_{x}\rho M^{*}_{y}\Tr[\outerproduct{x}{y}]=\sum_{x\in[n]}M_{x}\rho M^{*}_{x}=\mathcal{E}(\rho).

∎

Completely dephasing channel

A completely dephasing channel
\mathcal{D}\in\mathrm{CPTP}(A\to A)
is a channel such that for all
\rho\in\mathfrak{D}(A)
,

\mathcal{D}(\rho)=\sum_{x\in[n]}\outerproduct{x}{x}\bra{x}\rho\ket{x},

with respect to an orthogonal basis
\quantity{\ket{x}:x\in[n]}
and
n=\absolutevalue{A}
.
This channel, with respect to the basis, removes all off-diagonal elements of the input density matrix while keeping the diagonal elements intact.

Quantum measurement

A quantum measurement can be modeled with a quantum channel. Suppose that we have a physical system
A
and once we perform a measurement, it puts a quantum state in the physical system
B
and records the outcome in a classical memory
X
. For this process, a quantum channel performing this measurement is
\mathcal{E}\in\mathrm{CPTP}(A\to BX)
, which has the form,

\mathcal{E}^{A\to BX}(\rho)=\sum_{x\in[n]}\mathcal{N}_{x}^{A\to B}(\rho)\otimes\outerproduct{x}{x}^{X}

where
\quantity{\cal{N}_x ^A: x\in[n]}
is a set of completely positive trace-non-increasing operations that sum to a map in
\mathrm{CPTP}(A\to B)
.

5
Classical channel

A classical channel is a channel
\mathcal{N}
that always output a classical state and treat an input classically. Specifically, a classical channel is invariant under the pre- and post-compositions with a totally dephasing channel
\mathcal{D}
on a specific basis.

Classical channel

Definition 5.1
.

A channel
\mathcal{N}\in\mathrm{CPTP}(X\to Y)
is a
classical channel
if

\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{D}^{Y}\circ\mathcal{N}\circ\mathcal{D}^{X}

(5)

As this thesis discusses mainly classical channels, we use probability vectors to represent classical states and transition matrices to represent classical channels. A vector in the standard basis
\mathbf{e}_{x}=\outerproduct{x}{x}
represents a state of the definite outcome labeled with
x
. For example, a channel
\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}
is classical with respect to the choice of basis
\quantity{\ket{\psi_i}^X}_{i}
and
\quantity{\ket{\phi_j}^Y}_{j}
. This channel has an associated transition matrix
N
whose elements are defined from

N_{ij}=\Tr\bqty{\ketbra{\phi_j}{\phi_j} \cal{N}\pqty{\ketbra{\psi_i}{\psi_i}}}.

(6)

6
Quantum superchannel

A superchannel

Definition 6.1
.

A
superchannel
,
\Theta^{(A\to B)\to(A^{\prime}\to B^{\prime})}
, is a linear map between
\mathfrak{L}(\mathfrak{L}(A),\mathfrak{L}(B))
to
\mathfrak{L}(\mathfrak{L}(A^{\prime}),\mathfrak{L}(B^{\prime}))
, denoted as
\mathfrak{L}((A\to B)\to(A^{\prime}\to B^{\prime}))
, with the following properties

1.

Completely completely-positive preserving: given any complete positive map
\mathcal{E}^{AR_{0}\to BR_{1}}\in\mathrm{CP}(AR_{0}\to BR_{1})
, the map

\Theta^{(A\to B)\to(A^{\prime}\to B^{\prime})}\otimes\mathrm{1}^{(R_{0}\to R_{1})}\quantity[\cal{E}^{AR_0 \to BR_1}]

(7)

is completely positive, where
\mathrm{1}^{(R_{0}\to R_{1})}
is an identity supermap in
\mathfrak{L}((R_{0}\to R_{1}))
.

2.

Trace-preserving preserving: given any trace-preserving map
\mathcal{E}\in\mathrm{TP}(A\to B)
, the map

\Theta^{(A\to B)\to(A^{\prime}\to B^{\prime})}\bqty{\cal{E}^{AR_0 \to BR_1}}

(8)

is trace-preserving.

A superchannel can be realized with quantum channels and quantum memory
[
49
]
. To see this, we first have to introduce the Choi isomorphism between superchannel and quantum channel. The proof of the realization theorem follows the previously appeared in
[
50
]
.

1
Choi isomorphism and superchannel

A Choi map associated with
\Theta
is
\mathcal{J}_{\Theta}
defined as

\mathcal{J}_{\Theta}=\Theta^{(C\to D)\to(A^{\prime}\to B^{\prime})}[\mathcal{Q}^{AC\to BD}]

(9)

where
C\to D
is a replica of
A\to B
and
\mathcal{Q}
is a maximally entangled maps defined as follows.

\mathcal{Q}^{AC\to BD}=\sum_{\mu\in\mathfrak{I}}\mathcal{E}^{A\to B}_{\mu}\otimes\mathcal{E}^{C\to D}_{\mu}

(10)

where
\mathcal{E}_{\mu}^{A\to B}
is defined as

\mathcal{E}^{A\to B}_{\mu}\pqty{\rho^A}:=\Tr\bqty{\rho^{A} \ketbra{x'}{x}^{A}}\outerproduct{y}{y'}^{B}

(11)

and
\mu=(x,x^{\prime},y,y^{\prime})
.

Realization theorem

Theorem 6.2
.

Any superchannel
\Theta\in\mathrm{SUPER}((A\to B)\to(A^{\prime}\to B^{\prime}))
can be realized by pre-processing channel, post-processing channel, and a quantum memory
R
. That is for any
\mathcal{N}\in\mathrm{CPTP}(A\to B)
, the action of the superchannel
\Theta
is equal to

\Theta[\mathcal{N}]=\mathcal{E}^{BR\to B^{\prime}}\circ\mathcal{N}^{A\to B}\circ\mathcal{V}^{A^{\prime}\to AR}

(12)

where
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{F}
are quantum channels.

To prove this theorem, we make use of the lemma,

Lemma 6.3
.

A superchannel that has the form as in Equation (
12
). A map
\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{CPTP}(A^{\prime}B\to AB^{\prime})
defined as

\mathcal{F}^{A^{\prime}B\to AB^{\prime}}=\mathcal{E}^{RB\to B^{\prime}}\circ\mathcal{V}^{A^{\prime}\to RA}

(13)

has the same Choi matrix as the superchannel.

Proof.

A Choi map associated with
\Theta\in\mathrm{SUPER}((A\to B)\to(A^{\prime}\to B^{\prime}))
is
\mathcal{J}_{\Theta}\in\mathrm{CPTP}(AA^{\prime}\to BB^{\prime})
defined as

\Theta^{(C\to D)\to(A^{\prime}\to B^{\prime})}[\mathcal{Q}^{AC\to BD}]=\mathcal{E}^{DR\to B^{\prime}}\circ\mathcal{Q}^{AC\to BD}\circ\mathcal{V}^{A^{\prime}\to CR}.

(14)

Its Choi matrix is defined as

J_{\Theta}^{ABA^{\prime}B^{\prime}}=\mathcal{J}^{\tilde{A}\tilde{A^{\prime}}\to BB^{\prime}}_{\Theta}\pqty{\Omega^{A\tilde{A}}\otimes\Omega^{A'\tilde{A'}}}.

(15)

Consider the action of the isometry channel
\mathcal{V}^{A^{\prime}\to CR}
on the maximally entangled state,

\mathcal{V}^{\tilde{A}^{\prime}\to CR}\pqty{\Omega^{A\tilde{A}}\otimes\Omega^{A'\tilde{A'}}}=\mathcal{V}^{\tilde{C}\tilde{R}\to A^{\prime}}_{t}\pqty{\Omega^{A\tilde{A}}\otimes\Omega^{C\tilde{C}}\otimes\Omega^{R\tilde{R}}}.

(16)

Compose with
\mathcal{Q}^{\tilde{A}C\to BD}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{\tilde{A}C\to BD}\circ\mathcal{V}^{A^{\prime}\to CR}\pqty{\Omega^{A\tilde{A}}\otimes\Omega^{A'\tilde{A'}}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Q}^{\tilde{A}C\to BD}\circ\mathcal{V}^{\tilde{C}\tilde{R}\to A^{\prime}}_{t}\pqty{\Omega^{A\tilde{A}}\otimes\Omega^{C\tilde{C}}\otimes\Omega^{R\tilde{R}}}

(17)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\pqty{\text{Choi of } \cal{Q}}\longrightarrow}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{V}^{\tilde{C}\tilde{R}\to A^{\prime}}_{t}\pqty{\Omega^{BD}\otimes\Omega^{A\tilde{C}}\otimes\Omega^{R\tilde{R}}}

(18)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{V}^{\tilde{A^{\prime}}\to CR}\pqty{\Omega^{AC}\otimes\Omega^{A'\tilde{A'}}}

(19)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{RD\to B^{\prime}}\circ\mathcal{Q}^{\tilde{A}C\to BD}\circ\mathcal{V}^{A^{\prime}\to CR}\pqty{\Omega^{A\tilde{A}}\otimes\Omega^{A'\tilde{A'}}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}^{RD\to B^{\prime}}\circ\mathcal{V}^{\tilde{A^{\prime}}\to CR}\pqty{\Omega^{AC}\otimes\Omega^{A'\tilde{A'}}}

(20)

The last equality shows that the Choi of superchannel and
\mathcal{F}
are identical. Since Choi’s isomorphism is an isomorphism.
∎

Now we can prove the theorem.

Proof of the Theorem
6.2
.

Suppose that we have a Choi matrix
\mathbf{J}^{\mathbb{AB}}_{\Theta}
of the superchannel
\Theta
, we want to shows that we can define
\mathcal{E}\in\mathrm{CPTP}(RA_{1}\to B_{1})
and
\mathcal{V}\in\mathrm{CPTP}(B_{0}\to RA_{0})
such that
\mathcal{F}:=\mathcal{E}\circ\mathcal{V}
has the same Choi matrix as
\Theta
. This is so that Lemma
6.3
implies
\Theta
is in the form in Equation
12
.

To begin with, we define
\omega^{\mathbb{AB}}:=\mathbf{J}^{\mathbb{AB}}_{\Theta}/\absolutevalue{A_0 A_1 B_0 B_1}
to be a normalization of the Choi matrix of the superchannel. We pick the following

1.

a system
E
such that it allows us to define
\psi^{\mathbb{AB}E}
to be a purification of
\omega^{\mathbb{AB}}
.

2.

a system
R
such that it allows us to define
\phi^{A_{0}B_{0}R}
to be a purification of the marginal omega
\omega^{A_{0}B_{0}}
.

where
\omega^{A_{0}B_{0}}:=\Tr_{A_{1}B_{1}}[\omega^{\mathbb{AB}}]
is one of its marginals. With the condition that
\Theta
is a superchannel, then it is TP-preserving, which is equivalent to

\Tr_{B_{0}}[\mathbf{J}^{\mathbb{A}B_{0}}_{\Theta}]=\mathbf{J}^{A_{0}B_{0}}_{\Theta}\otimes\mathbf{u}^{A_{1}}\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\Tr_{A_{0}A_{1}B_{1}}[\mathbf{J}^{\mathbb{AB}}_{\Theta}]=\absolutevalue{A_1}I^{B_{0}}.

(21)

We make use of the first equality and use purification with
R
,

\displaystyle\omega^{\mathbb{A}B_{0}}=\omega^{A_{0}B_{0}}\otimes\mathbf{u}^{A_{1}}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{Purification}}\phi^{A_{0}B_{0}R}\otimes\Phi^{A_{1}\tilde{A}_{1}}.

(22)

Another purification of
\omega^{\mathbb{AB}}
is the state
\psi^{\mathbb{AB}E}
. The two purifications relate to one another by an isometry
\mathcal{U}
,

\psi^{\mathbb{AB}E}=~\mathcal{U}^{R\tilde{A}_{1}\to EB_{1}}\pqty{\phi^{A_0B_0R}\otimes\Phi^{A_1 \tilde{A}_1}}.

(23)

From the above equation, tracing out the auxiliary system
E
results in

\omega^{\mathbb{AB}}=\underbrace{\Tr_{E}\circ~\mathcal{U}^{R\tilde{A}_{1}\to EB_{1}}}_{=:\mathcal{E}^{R\tilde{A}_{1}\to B_{1}}}\pqty{\phi^{A_0B_0R}\otimes\Phi^{A_1 \tilde{A}_1}}

(24)

By the second equality in Equation (
21
), we have that tracing out
\phi^{A_{0}B_{0}R}
over
A_{0}R
results in a uniform vector
\mathbf{u}
. Purification of the state
\mathbf{u}
with a copy of
B_{0}
is a maximally entangled state, which related to
\phi^{A_{0}B_{0}R}
by an isometry
\mathcal{V}^{\tilde{B}_{0}\to A_{0}R}

\phi^{A_{0}B_{0}R}=\mathcal{V}^{\tilde{B}_{0}\to A_{0}R}(\Phi^{B_{0}\tilde{B}_{0}}).

(25)

All of these together, we have

\omega^{\mathbb{AB}}=\mathcal{E}^{R\tilde{A}_{1}\to B_{1}}\circ\mathcal{V}^{\tilde{B}_{0}\to A_{0}R}\pqty{\Phi^{B_0\tilde{B}_0}\otimes\Phi^{A_1 \tilde{A}_1}}.

(26)

The right-hand side is the Choi matrix of the superchannel of the form as in Equation (
12
), by Lemma
6.3
. As the Choi isomorphism is an isomorphism, the superchannel
\Theta
can be realized as such.
∎

Chapter 2
Uncertainty and majorizations of classical states

In this chapter, we provide a review of a mathematical conceptualization of the uncertainty inherence in a classical state, which later on will be applicable to a quantum state. The chapter begin with the main tool termed
Majorization
which developed initially in the early 20th century by Hardy, Littlewood and Pólya
[
19
]
to study inequality of wealth distribution. Later on, the concept found its way into the study of quantum resource theories as a comparison tools of uncertainty.

In this chapter, we provide an introduction to three variants of majorization relation on the domain of probability vectors: majorization, relative majorization, and conditional majorization. The majorization, or the standard relation, defines concretely what is an uncertainty inherent in a random variable
X
with a specific probability distribution. The conditional majorization compares and defines the notion of uncertainty of a random variable
X
similarly to the standard probability vector majorization, but with a conditioning on accessing information of
Y
, a potentially non-independent random variable. Lastly, the relative majorization is a majorization comparing distinguishability between probability distributions. That is, how likely one can tell two probability distributions apart by just learning their outcomes.

1
Majorization

Majorization compares two distributions, e.g. probability distributions or wealth distribution of two nations. We can represent a distribution among the finite number of parties, e.g. random variable outcomes or individuals in a nation, with a vector.
An intuitive way to compare the concentration of the distribution is to compare the sums of the first
k
the largest weight of each distribution. For example, in case of a nation wealth, it could be a sum of the top
k\%
. Mathematically, the sum of the first
k
absolutely the largest elements of a vector is a norm.

The Ky Fan
k
-norm for vectors

Definition 1.1
.

Given a vector
\mathbf{r}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
k
is a positive integer no greater than
n
. The
Ky Fan
k
-norm
of the vector
\mathbf{r}
is

\left\|\mathbf{r}\right\|_{(k)}=\sum_{i=0}^{k}\absolutevalue{r_i^\da},

(1)

where
\mathbf{r}^{\downarrow}=\pqty{r_1 ^\da, r_2 ^\da, \ldots, r_n ^\da}^{T}
is a rearrangement of the elements of
\mathbf{r}
such that
\absolutevalue{r_1 ^\da}\geq\absolutevalue{r_2 ^\da}\geq\ldots\geq\absolutevalue{r_n ^\da}
.

Theorem 1.2
.

The Ky Fan
k
-norm is a norm. For any
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
,
\mathbf{r},\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and
n
is a positive integer the followings hold.

1.

\left\|\mathbf{r}\right\|_{(k)}>0
except when
\mathbf{r}=0
,
\left\|\mathbf{r}\right\|_{(k)}=0
.

2.

\left\|\alpha\mathbf{r}\right\|_{(k)}=\absolutevalue{\alpha}\left\|\mathbf{r}\right\|_{(k)}
.

3.

\left\|\mathbf{r}+\mathbf{s}\right\|_{(k)}\leq\left\|\mathbf{r}\right\|_{(k)}+\left\|\mathbf{s}\right\|_{(k)}

Proof.

1. and 2. follow directly from the definition. To see the inequality in 3., consider

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{r}+\mathbf{s}\right\|_{(k)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in[k]}\absolutevalue{(\vctr+\s)^\da_x}=\max\quantity{\abs{\vctr+\s}\cdot\t~\big\vert~\t\in\qty{0,1}^n, ~ \norm{\*{t}}_1 = k}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\pqty{\abs{\vctr+\s}\leq\abs{\vctr}+\abs{\s}}\rightarrow}

\displaystyle\leq\max\quantity{\abs{\vctr}\cdot\t_1+\abs{\s}\cdot\t_2~\big\vert~\t_1=\t_2 \in\qty{0,1}^n, ~ \norm{\t_1}_1=\norm{\t_2}_1 = k}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\pqty{\text{being~superset}}\rightarrow}

\displaystyle\leq\max\quantity{\abs{\vctr}\cdot\t_1+\abs{\s}\cdot\t_2~\big\vert~\t_1,\t_2\in\qty{0,1}^n, ~ \norm{\t_1}_1=\norm{\t_2}_1 = k}

\displaystyle=\max\quantity{\abs{\vctr}\cdot\t_1 ~\big\vert~\t_1 \in\qty{0,1}^n, ~ \norm{\t_1}_1=k}

\displaystyle\qquad+\max\quantity{\abs{\s}\cdot\t_2 ~\big\vert~\t_2 \in\qty{0,1}^n, ~ \norm{\t_2}_1=k}

\displaystyle=\left\|\mathbf{r}\right\|_{(k)}+\left\|\mathbf{s}\right\|_{(k)},

showing that we have triangle inequality.
∎

Majorization
(\succ)
is a preorder on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
defined from the
k^{\mathrm{th}}
Ky Fan norm of the vector.

Majorization

Definition 1.3
.

Suppose
\mathbf{r},\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. we say
\mathbf{r}

majorizes

\mathbf{s}
and write
\mathbf{r}\succ\mathbf{s}
if

\norm{\*{r}}_{(k)}\geq\norm{\*{s}}_{(k)}\quad\forall k\in[n-1]

(2)

with the equality at
k=n
. Moreover, if
\mathbf{r}\succ\mathbf{s}
and
\mathbf{s}\succ\mathbf{r}
we said that both vectors are equivalent under majorization relation and write
\mathbf{r}\sim\mathbf{s}
.

Theorem 1.4
.

Majorization,
\succ
, is a preorder. It satisfies

•

Transitivity: for any
\mathbf{r},\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we have
\mathbf{r}\succ\mathbf{s}
and
\mathbf{s}\succ\mathbf{t}
imply
\mathbf{r}\succ\mathbf{t}
.

•

Reflexivity:
\mathbf{r}\succ\mathbf{r}
for any
\mathbf{r}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Remark
.

Majorization is not a total preorder, i.e. there are non-majorizing pairs of vectors. For example
\mathbf{r}=\pqty{70,15,15}^{T}
and
\mathbf{s}=\pqty{50,45,5}^{T}
, both vectors have equal Ky Fan
3
-norm but
\left\|\mathbf{r}\right\|_{(1)}>\left\|\mathbf{s}\right\|_{(1)}
and
\left\|\mathbf{r}\right\|_{(2)}<\left\|\mathbf{s}\right\|_{(2)}
.

Proof.

For transitivity,
\mathbf{r}\succ\mathbf{s}
implies
\left\|\mathbf{r}\right\|_{(k)}\geq\left\|\mathbf{s}\right\|_{(k)}
for all
k
. Similarly, with the pair
\mathbf{s}
and
\mathbf{t}
. By transitivity of the
\geq
total order, we have
\left\|\mathbf{r}\right\|_{(k)}\geq\left\|\mathbf{s}\right\|_{(k)}
for any
k\in[n]
as well. Reflexivity follows from
\left\|\mathbf{r}\right\|_{(k)}
equals to itself.
∎

2
Probability vector majorization

The majorization relation is defined on the domain of any real vectors. In this thesis, we focus specifically on the probability vectors, as it is associated with a probability distribution. Given a probability distribution of a random variable
X:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
, if the image of the sample space
\Omega
under
X
is a finite set, then we can assign each outcome a label by number
1,2,\ldots,n
and arrange them into a vector. Mathematically, probability vector is defined as follows.

Probability vector

Definition 2.1
.

A vector
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
is said to be an
n
-dimensional probability vector
and write
\mathbf{p}\in\mathrm{Prob}(n)
if its elements are non-negative, and they sum to 1. That is

\mathbf{p}\in\mathrm{Prob}(n)\quad:\iff\quad\sum_{i\in[n]}p_{i}=1\quad\text{and}\quad p_{i}\geq 0\quad\forall i\in[n].

Restricting to the subset
\mathrm{Prob}(n)\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
defines a preorder on probability vectors.
The following is an example of two vectors that majorized one another.

\mathbf{p}=\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}^{T},\quad\mathbf{q}=\frac{1}{3}\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}^{T},\quad\mathbf{p}\succ\mathbf{q}

To motivate why the definition of majorization captures uncertainty inherent in the probability distribution, we follow similar characterizations in
[
51
]
and
[
52
]
. The majorization between probability vectors can be characterized from three conceptually distinct perspectives: constructive, axiomatic, and operational.

1
Characterizations of majorization with uncertainty

From constructive and axiomatic perspectives, we want to characterize majorization as an ability to transform a probability distribution without decreasing its uncertainty. That is, we will motivate two classes of transformation
M
to be classes of uncertainty non-increasing operations: one in axiomatic and another in constructive ways. As we will see shortly, these two approaches reach the same class of stochastic matrices—giving a solid notion of uncertainty comparison.

In defining a set of mixing operations in both constructive and axiomatic characterization, the mixing operations need to be linear, i.e.

M\quantity(\sum_{x\in[m]} t_x \*{p}_x)=\sum_{x\in[m]}t_{x}M(\mathbf{p}_{x})

(3)

where
\mathbf{t}=(t_{x})\in\mathrm{Prob}(m)
. The requirement of linearity is from a probabilistic interpretation of probability distribution. The left-hand side of the above equation is application of the mixing
M
to a probabilistic ensemble of probability distributions. The linearity minimally implies that applying
M
to the ensemble has to be equivalent to applying
M
to each individual distribution and mixed into probabilistic ensemble. In addition,
M
must map a probability vector to another probability vector, this requires
M
to be a column stochastic matrix,
M\in\mathrm{Stoch}(n,n)
. Later on whenever we say stochastic matrix we mean a column stochastic matrix.

Constructive characterization.
Majorization relation between probability vector can be characterized as an ability to convert probability vector with a random permutation matrix. Consider a stochastic matrix
M
representing an operation of randomly permute the outcomes. The intuition behind this is that relabelling of the outcomes do not change the level of uncertainty. However, by randomly relabelling, one increase the uncertainty of the distribution. That is
M
is a convex combination of a permutation matrix
\Pi_{y}
where
y
denotes the permutation scheme,

M=\sum_{y\in[n!]}s_{y}\Pi_{y}

(4)

for some
\mathbf{s}\in\mathrm{Prob}(n!)
. Suppose
\mathbf{p}
and
\mathbf{q}
are probability vectors. If
\mathbf{p}
can be randomly permuted into
\mathbf{q}
, then the distribution associated with
\mathbf{q}
is at least as uncertain as
\mathbf{p}
.

Axiomatic characterization.

Two majorization-comparable probability vectors can be characterized with convertibility via a doubly stochastic matrix. One would expect an uncertainty non-decreasing operation to preserve the most uncertain distributions for a dimension
n
, which is a uniform distribution
\mathbf{u}^{(n)}\in\mathrm{prob}(n)
.
From this perspective,
M
is defined to be a column stochastic matrix which preserve
\mathbf{u}^{(n)}\in\mathrm{prob}(n)
an
n
-dimension uniform vector. This set of matrix is known as a doubly-stochastic matrix.

Lemma 2.2
.

A stochastic matrix
M\in\mathrm{Stoch}(n,n)
satisfies
M\mathbf{u}^{(n)}=\mathbf{u}^{(n)}
if and only if it is a doubly stochastic matrix.

Proof.

A doubly stochastic matrix is a stochastic matrix which the sum of elements in each row is equal to one. That is we want to show that
\sum_{j\in[n]}M_{ij}=1
for any column
i\in[n]
.

\displaystyle M\mathbf{u}^{(n)}=\mathbf{u}^{(n)}

\displaystyle\implies\sum_{i\in[n]}\sum_{j\in[m]}M_{ij}\frac{1}{n}\mathbf{e}_{i}=\sum_{i\in[n]}\frac{1}{n}\mathbf{e}_{i}

\displaystyle\implies\sum_{j\in[n]}M_{ij}=1\implies M_{ij}\in\mathrm{DStoch}(n).

∎

Operational interpretation.

Given two probability vectors
\mathbf{p}
and
\mathbf{q}
representing two probability distributions, a
k
-game is a game of chance where a player gives
k
guesses to a sample draw from a probability distribution. The player of this game has full knowledge of the probability distributions. The winning strategy is to guess
k
most likely outcomes. The winning chance with a distribution
\mathbf{p}
is

\mathrm{Pr}_{k}(\mathbf{p})=\left\|\mathbf{p}\right\|_{(k)}

(5)

If a probability vector
\mathbf{p}
has a higher chance of winning a
k
-game for any
k\in[n]
, then for any
k\in[n]
,
\mathrm{Pr}_{k}(\mathbf{p})\geq\mathrm{Pr}_{k}(\mathbf{q})
or equivalently,
\left\|\mathbf{p}\right\|_{(k)}\geq\left\|\mathbf{q}\right\|_{(k)}
,
which is exact the same as Equation (
2
).

Characterization of majorization with uncertainty

Theorem 2.3
.

The following statements are equivalent

1.

There exists
M
a random permutation matrix transforming
\mathbf{p}
to
\mathbf{q}

2.

There exists
M
a doubly stochastic matrix transforming
\mathbf{p}
to
\mathbf{q}
.

3.

For any
k
,
\mathrm{Pr}_{k}(\mathbf{p})\geq\mathrm{Pr}_{k}\pqty{\q}
.

The direction
1.\implies 2.
is given by the Birkhoff–von Neumann theorem
[
20
]
. The direction
2.\implies 3.
, is given by a stochastic matrix being a contraction on the Ky Fan
k
-norms.

Proof of 2.
\implies
3.

Suppose that
\mathbf{q}=M\mathbf{p}
where
M
is a doubly stochastic matrix, we want to show
\left\|\mathbf{p}\right\|_{(k)}\geq\left\|\mathbf{q}\right\|_{(k)}
for all
k\in[n]
. We make use of the Birkhoff–von Neumann theorem to express
M
as a convex sum of permutation matrices,

M=\sum_{w\in[n!]}t_{w}\Pi_{w},\quad\sum_{w\in[n!]}t_{w}=1,\textrm{ and}\quad t_{w}\geq 1\quad\forall w\in[n!].

(6)

Now consider the following with any
k\in[n!]
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}_{k}(\mathbf{q})=\left\|\mathbf{q}\right\|_{(k)}

\displaystyle=\left\|\sum_{w\in[n!]}t_{w}\Pi_{w}\mathbf{p}\right\|_{(k)}\leq\sum_{w\in[n!]}t_{w}\left\|\Pi_{w}\mathbf{p}\right\|_{(k)}=\left\|\mathbf{p}\right\|_{(k)}=\mathrm{Pr}_{k}(\mathbf{p}).

(7)

∎

We will use
T
-transform to prove the direction
3.\implies 1.
.

T
-transform

Definition 2.4
.

A square matrix
T\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is said to be a
T
-transform
if it can be put in the form

T=tI_{n}+(1-t)\Pi

(8)

where
t\in[0,1]
and
\Pi
is a permutation matrix swapping only two elements.

There are two remarks. First, any action of a
T
-transform on a probability vector
\mathbf{p}
will result in a new probability vector, denoted with
\mathbf{r}
. The vector
\mathbf{r}
has elements
r_{z}
, and it always exists two distinct indices
x,y
such that

r_{z}=\begin{cases}p_{z}&(z\neq x\text{ and }z\neq y)\\
tp_{x}+(1-t)p_{y}&(z=x)\\
tp_{y}+(1-t)p_{x}&(z=y)\end{cases}.

(9)

Secondly, the resultant vector is always majorized by the original vector. That is
\mathbf{r}=T\mathbf{p}
implies
\mathbf{p}\succ\mathbf{r}
. This can be verified by expressing the
T
, Ky Fan norm, and exploiting the triangle inequality.

\displaystyle\forall k\in[n]\qquad\left\|\mathbf{r}\right\|_{(k)}=\left\|t\mathbf{p}+(1-t)\Pi\mathbf{p}\right\|_{(k)}\leq t\left\|\mathbf{p}\right\|_{(k)}+(1-t)\left\|\Pi\mathbf{p}\right\|_{(k)}=\left\|\mathbf{p}\right\|_{(k)}.

(10)

Lemma 2.5
.

\mathbf{p}\succ\mathbf{q}
if and only if there exists finite number of
T
-transforms such that
\mathbf{q}=T_{n}\ldots T_{2}T_{1}\mathbf{p}
.

Proof.

In the only-if direction, since
\mathbf{r}=T\mathbf{p}
implies
\mathbf{p}\succ\mathbf{r}
for any
\mathbf{p}\in\mathrm{Prob}(n)
, therefore the result of applying
T_{n}\ldots T_{2}T_{1}
on
\mathbf{p}
is majorized by
\mathbf{p}
.

Conversely, suppose
\mathbf{p}\succ\mathbf{q}
. Assume
\mathbf{p}
and
\mathbf{q}
are in non-increasing order. If it is not, then first apply
T
-transform to permute
\mathbf{p}
to its non-increasing order
\mathbf{p}^{\downarrow}
and similarly with
\mathbf{q}
. Furthermore, assume
\mathbf{p}\neq\mathbf{q}
, otherwise
T
-transform is an identity and the proof is trivial. We define each
T
-transform such that the resultant vector
\mathbf{r}
satisfies the followings.

1.

\mathbf{p}\succ\mathbf{r}\succ\mathbf{q}

2.

There are distinct
x
and
y
such that
r_{x}\neq p_{x}
and
r_{y}\neq p_{y}
.

3.

r_{x}=q_{x}
or
r_{y}=q_{y}
.

Such
T
-transform can be defined by
T=tI+(1-t)\Pi
when
\Pi
swap
x,y
and
t
,
x
, and
y
are defined as follows.
x
is defined to be the largest index such that
p_{x}>q_{x}
. Since
\mathbf{p}\succ\mathbf{q}
and
\mathbf{p}\neq\mathbf{q}
, such
x
always exists.
y
is defined to be the smallest index such that
p_{y}<q_{y}
. Such
y
always exists since the probability vector must have a unit 1-norm and there exists
p_{x}>q_{x}
. Lastly,
t
is defined as

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle=1-\frac{\varepsilon}{p_{x}-p_{y}}

\displaystyle\varepsilon

\displaystyle=\min\quantity{p_x - q_x, q_y - p_y}.

To show that
x,y,
and
t
result in
\mathbf{r}
that satisfies 1., 2., and 3., consider the elements
r_{x}
and
r_{y}

\displaystyle r_{x}

\displaystyle=tp_{x}+(1-t)p_{y}=p_{y}+t(p_{x}-p_{y})

\displaystyle r_{y}

\displaystyle=tp_{y}+(1-t)p_{x}=p_{x}+t(p_{y}-p_{x}).

If
\varepsilon=p_{x}-q_{x}
, then
t=1-\frac{p_{x}-q_{x}}{p_{x}-p_{y}}=\frac{q_{x}-p_{y}}{p_{x}-p_{y}}
and

r_{x}=p_{y}+\pqty{\frac{q_x - p_y}{p_x - p_y}}\pqty{p_x - p_y}=q_{x}.

Otherwise,
\varepsilon=p_{y}-q_{y}
, then

r_{y}=p_{x}+\pqty{\frac{p_x - q_y}{p_x - p_y}}\pqty{p_y - p_x}=q_{y}.

Both of the two cases satisfy 3. To show 2., since
p_{x}>q_{x}
and
p_{y}<q_{y}
, either
r_{x}=q_{x}
or
r_{y}=q_{y}
, and the
T
-transform changes two elements, therefore
p_{x}\neq r_{x}
and
p_{y}\neq r_{y}
. Note here that
x>y
as implied by the majorization relation. For the majorization condition of
\mathbf{r}\succ\mathbf{q}
, consider

\displaystyle\text{For $1\leq k<x$},\quad\left\|\mathbf{r}\right\|_{(k)}=\left\|\mathbf{r}\right\|_{(k)}\geq\left\|\mathbf{q}\right\|_{(k)}.

\displaystyle\text{For $x\leq k<y$},\quad\left\|\mathbf{r}\right\|_{(k)}\geq\left\|\mathbf{p}\right\|_{(x-1)}+\underbrace{r_{x}}_{r_{x}\geq q_{x}}+\sum_{w=x+1}^{k}\underbrace{p_{w}}_{=q_{w}}\geq\left\|\mathbf{q}\right\|_{(k)},

for
y\leq k
; use
r_{x}+r_{y}=p_{x}+p_{y}
, we have that
\left\|\mathbf{r}\right\|_{(k)}=\left\|\mathbf{p}\right\|_{(k)}
.

The requirement number 3. implies that after each application of the
T
-transform, one will have a new probability vector
r
which has one more elements identical to the element
q_{x}
. Repeating this process over a certain finite iterations results in the vector
\mathbf{q}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Proof of 3.
\implies
1.

By Lemma
2.5
and
T
-transform being a doubly-stochastic matrix, we have that
M=T_{n}\ldots T_{2}T_{1}\in\mathrm{DStoch}(n)
.
∎

2
Comparison between probability vector of different dimensions

Definition 2.6
.

Suppose
\mathbf{p}\in\mathrm{Prob}(n)
and
\mathbf{q}\in\mathrm{Prob}(m)
where
n\leq m
. Define
\ell=m-n
, we say
\mathbf{p}\succ\mathbf{q}
if
\mathbf{p}\oplus\mathbf{0}^{(\ell)}\succ\mathbf{q}
. Conversely, we say
\mathbf{q}\succ\mathbf{p}
if
\mathbf{q}\succ\mathbf{p}\oplus\mathbf{0}^{(\ell)}
.

Remark
.

Since we would like to capture uncertainty inherit in a distribution, the comparison should be invariant under the removal of events of zero probability. With this extension, we have that for any
n,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
and any
\mathbf{p}\in\mathrm{Prob}(n)
,

\mathbf{p}\sim\mathbf{p}\oplus\mathbf{0}^{(\ell)}.

(11)

That is, adding or removing impossible outcomes into consideration do not affect the uncertainty as captured by majorization relation.

3
Majorization and entanglement theory

Majorization can be extended to the domain of quantum states and provide a useful characterization of entanglement resources. Suppose
\rho,\sigma\in\mathfrak{D}(A)
, the majorization relation compares the vectors form by eigenvalues of
\rho
and
\sigma
, denoted by
\lambda_{\rho}
and
\lambda_{\sigma}
respectively.

Definition 2.7
.

Suppose
\rho,\sigma\in\mathfrak{D}(A)
, we say that the state
\rho
majorizes the state
\sigma
and write

\rho\succ\sigma
if
\lambda_{\rho}\succ\lambda_{\sigma}
where
\lambda_{\rho}
and
\lambda_{\sigma}
are vectors of eigenvalues of
\rho
and
\sigma
respectively.

When
\rho
and
\sigma
are classical, the majorizations, classical and quantum, coincide. This definition is equivalent to the existence of a
unital
channel transforming one quantum state to another.

Lemma 2.8
.

Given
\rho,\sigma\in\mathfrak{D}(A)
,
\rho\succ\sigma
if and only if there exists a unital channel
\mathcal{E}\in\mathrm{CPTP}(A\to A)
such that
\sigma=\mathcal{E}(\rho)
.

Proof.

Suppose
\rho\succ\sigma
. Then, there exists a doubly stochastic matrix
D=(d_{i,j})_{i,j}
such that
\lambda_{\sigma}=D\lambda_{\rho}
. Suppose
\rho
and
\sigma
are diagonal in the basis
\quantity{\psi_i : i \in[n]}
and
\quantity{\phi_i : i \in[n]}
respectively. We define a matrix
W
to be

W=\sum_{i,j}d_{ij}\outerproduct{\phi_i}{\psi_j}.

A channel
\mathcal{U}
defined as

\mathcal{U}(\rho)=W\rho W^{*}

is a unital channel transforming
\rho\mapsto\sigma
.

Conversely, suppose that there exists a unital channel
\mathcal{U}
mapping
\rho
to
\sigma
. Define

D=\sum_{i,j\in[n]}\bra{\phi_j}\mathcal{U}(\outerproduct{\psi_i}{\psi_i})\ket{\phi_j}\mathbf{e}_{j}\mathbf{e}_{i}^{T}.

The matrix
D
is doubly stochastic and maps
\lambda_{\rho}\mapsto\lambda_{\sigma}
.
∎

Remark
.

There is no constructive approach for quantum state majorization. Not all unital channel can be written as a convex sum of unitary channels.

One of the most famous application of majorization relation in the field of quantum information theorem is Nielsen majorization theorem
[
15
,
47
]
. The theorem pertains to convertibility relation of a bipartite quantum state using only
local operations and classical communications
(LOCC). The LOCC condition captures a physical constraint where a quantum state is spatially separated, e.g. two photons are in two different labs. The only allowed operation is the operation where the experimentalists perform operation on one side of the system and communicating with another lab with only classical communications, e.g. electrical signal, text messages, phone calls.

Nielsen majorization theorem

Theorem 2.9
.

Let
\psi,\phi\in\operatorname{Pure}(AB)
be two bipartite quantum states, and let
\rho^{A}:=\mathrm{Tr}_{B}\left[\psi^{AB}\right]
and
\sigma^{A}:=\operatorname{Tr}_{B}\left[\phi^{AB}\right]
be their corresponding reduced density matrices. Then,
\psi
is convertible to
\phi
via LOCC if and only if
\sigma^{A}\succ\rho^{B}
.

3
Monotones of probability vector majorization

In this section, we discuss the conditions and characteristics of a family of function which behave monotonically under majorization relation. First, we recite the definition of Schur-convex function on a subset
A
of
\mathbb{R}^{n}

[
53
]
.

Schur-convex function

Definition 3.1
.

A function
f:A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
is
Schur-convex
on
A
if
\mathbf{p}\succ\mathbf{q}
implies
f(\mathbf{p})\geq f(\mathbf{q})
for any pairs
\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\in A
. A function
f
is Schur-concave if its negative
-f
is Schur-convex.

The merit of this definition is in its generality. The function need not be defined all on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
but only on a subset
A
. In the interest of this thesis, Schur-convex functions are usually defined on a set of non-negative
n
-dimensional vectors
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
.

One of the immediate necessary condition of
f
to be Schur-convex is that
f
need to be invariant under permutation of its inputs. That is if
A
is symmetric set, which mean invariant under permutation, then
f(\mathbf{p})=f(\Pi\mathbf{p})
for all
\mathbf{p}\in A
and
\Pi\in\mathrm{Perm}(n)
. This is the case because
\mathbf{p}\succ\Pi\mathbf{p}
and
\Pi\mathbf{p}\succ\mathbf{p}
as well. This together with another condition give us a sufficient condition for
f
to be Schur-convex.

Theorem 3.2
.

If
f
is symmetric and convex then it is Schur-convex.

Proof.

Suppose
\mathbf{p}\succ\mathbf{q}
. Then, there exists a doubly stochastic matrix
D\in\mathrm{DStoch}(n)
such that
\mathbf{q}=D\mathbf{p}
. Therefore, we have the following chain of inequalities,

\displaystyle f(\mathbf{q})

\displaystyle=f(D\mathbf{q})=f\pqty{\sum_{i\in[n!]} t_i \Pi_i \p}\quad(t_{i}\geq 0,\sum_{i\in[n!]}t_{i}=1)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i\in[n!]}t_{i}f\pqty{\Pi_i \p}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in[n!]}t_{i}f(\mathbf{p})=f(\mathbf{p}),

showing that
f
is Schur-convex.
∎

Note that the converse is not true. If we have Schur-convexity, need not the function be convex. One of the examples is min-entropy, which will be introduced in the next section.

The sufficient and necessary condition of a continuously differentiable function to be Schur-convex is succinctly stated by the following theorem called Schur test.

Schur test

Theorem 3.3
.

Suppose
f:A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
where
\mathrm{Prob}(n)\subseteq A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
and the relative interior of
\mathrm{Prob}(n)
is in the interior of
A
. Suppose
f
is continuous on
A
and continuously differentiable on the interior of
A
.
f
is Schur-convex on
\mathrm{Prob}(n)
if and only if

1.

f
is symmetric.

2.

f
satisfies
Schur condition
. That is for any
\mathbf{p}
in the relative interior of
\mathrm{Prob}(n)
and
i,j\in[n]

\pqty{p_i - p_j}\pqty{\pdv{f(\p)}{p_i} -\pdv{f(\p)}{p_j} }\geq 0.

(12)

Remark
.

The condition of
f
and the domain
A
are to guarantee existence of the derivative on
\mathrm{Prob}(n)
.

Proof.

Suppose
f
is Schur-convex on
A
. Suppose
\mathbf{p}\in\mathrm{Prob}(n)
and
p_{1}>p_{2}
. Otherwise, if
p_{1}<p_{2}
, applied a permutation matrix to swap them, or if
p_{1}=p_{2}
the proof is trivial.

First,
f
is symmetric since for any permutation matrices
\Pi\in\mathrm{Perm}(n)

\mathbf{p}\succ\Pi\mathbf{p}
implies
f(\mathbf{p})\geq f(\Pi\mathbf{p})
and
\Pi\mathbf{p}\succ\mathbf{p}
implies
f(\Pi\mathbf{p})\geq f(\mathbf{p})
, therefore
f(\mathbf{p})=f(\Pi\mathbf{p})
for any permutation matrix
\Pi
.

To show the Schur condition, consider a
T
-transform
T=tI_{n}+(1-t)\Pi
, where
t\in[1/2,1]
and
\Pi
exchanges the first and second elements. Define
\mathbf{v}=(-1,1,0,\ldots,0)^{T}
and
\mathbf{p}_{\varepsilon}:=\mathbf{p}+\varepsilon\mathbf{v}
for any
\varepsilon\in[0,\frac{1}{2}(p_{1}-p_{2})]
. The transformation
T
maps
\mathbf{p}
to
\mathbf{p}_{\varepsilon}
with
\varepsilon=(1-t)(p_{1}-p_{2})
. This means
f(\mathbf{p}_{\varepsilon})\leq f(\mathbf{p})
. By taking the limit
t\to 1/2^{+}
or equivalently
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
, we have that

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\geq\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\pqty{f(\p_\varepsilon) - f(\p)}=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\pqty{f(\p+ \varepsilon\vctv) + f(\p)}

(13)

\displaystyle=D_{\mathbf{v}}f(\mathbf{p})=\nabla f\mathbf{v}=-\partial_{1}f(\mathbf{p})+\partial_{2}f(\mathbf{p}).

(14)

Since
p_{1}>p_{2}
and for any other pair of
p_{i},p_{j}
we can permute them to the first and second elements, thus we have the satisfaction of the Schur condition.

Conversely, we want to show that if
f
satisfies both conditions then
f(\mathbf{p})\geq f(\mathbf{q})
whenever
\mathbf{p}\succ\mathbf{q}
. Notice that if
\mathbf{p}\succ\mathbf{q}
, then there exists a doubly stochastic matrix such that it transform
\mathbf{p}
into
\mathbf{q}
and any doubly-stochastic can be written as finite applications of
T
-transforms. Therefore, it is enough to show that
f(\mathbf{p})\geq f(T\mathbf{p})
for any
T
being a
T
-transform.

Suppose
T=tI+(1-t)\Pi
. Since
f
is symmetric, we can assume the following without the loss of generality.

1.

T
swap the first and second elements Otherwise, apply
\Pi^{\prime}
to permute the wanted elements to 1 and 2.

2.

The parameter
t
is in the interval
t\in[\frac{1}{2},1]
. If
t\in[0,\frac{1}{2})
, apply
\Pi
to
T\mathbf{p}
. We have
f(\Pi T\mathbf{p})=f(T\mathbf{p})
.

3.

p_{1}>p_{2}
, otherwise switch the ordering or if
p_{1}=p_{2}
the proof is trivial.

Define
\mathbf{p}_{\varepsilon}
to be a probability vector
T\mathbf{p}
and
\varepsilon=(1-t)(p_{1}-p_{2})
. The parameter
\varepsilon\in[0,\frac{1}{2}(p_{1}-p_{2})]
. The vector
\mathbf{p}_{\varepsilon}
can be written as

\mathbf{p}_{\varepsilon}=\pqty{p_1 - \varepsilon, p_2 + \varepsilon, p_3 , p_4, \ldots, p_n}^{T}.

(15)

Therefore,

\displaystyle\derivative{\varepsilon}f(\mathbf{p}_{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle=\partialderivative{p_1}f(\mathbf{p}_{\varepsilon})\derivative{p_1}{\varepsilon}+\partialderivative{p_2}f(\mathbf{p}_{\varepsilon})\derivative{p_2}{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=-\varepsilon\pqty{\pdv{p_1} f(\p_\varepsilon) -\pdv{p_2} f(\p_\varepsilon)}\leq 0

The inequality is given by the Schur condition. Define
g(\varepsilon)=f(\mathbf{p}_{\varepsilon})
. Since
\varepsilon>0
we have that
{\partialderivative{p_1}f(\mathbf{p}_{\varepsilon})-\partialderivative{p_2}f(\mathbf{p}_{\varepsilon})}\geq 0
. The inequality shows that the function
g
is non-increasing with
\varepsilon
on the interval
\bqty{0,\frac{1}{2}(p_1 - p_2)}
. Therefore,
f(\mathbf{p})\geq f(T\mathbf{p})
.
∎

1
Entropies

Entropy in information theory was first purposed by Claude Shannon in 1948
[
2
]
. Later in 1961, Alfréd Rényi axiomatize and purpose a family of entropies generalizing Shannon entropy. The family is later known as Rényi entropies
[
54
]
. There are multiples axiomatization of Shannon entropy
[
55
,
54
,
56
,
57
]
. However, a recent axiomatic definition of ground the definition of entropy by a majorization
[
58
]
. As we have seen in the earlier section, the majorization is closely related with uncertainty. We present the definition in the following.

Entropy

Definition 3.4
.

A function
f:\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathrm{Prob}(n)\times\mathrm{Prob}(n)\to\mathbb{R}
is said to be an
entropy
if it satisfies the following conditions.

1.

It is Schur-concave (it is an antitone of majorization).

2.

It is additive under Kronecker tensor product of probability vector, i.e.

f(\mathbf{p}\otimes\mathbf{q})=f(\mathbf{p})+f(\mathbf{q})

for any
\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\in\mathrm{Prob}(n)
and any
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

The additivity condition together with being Schur-concave imply non-negativity of entropy on the domain of probability vector. To see this, consider an entropy of

f(p)=f(\mathbf{p}\otimes 1)=f(\mathbf{p})+f(1)\implies f(1)=0.

The first equality comes from
\mathbf{p}=\mathbf{p}\otimes 1
. Since
1\succ\mathbf{q}
for any
\mathbf{q}\in\mathrm{Prob}(m)
and any
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Therefore,
f(1)=0\leq f(\mathbf{q})
for any
\mathbf{q}\in\mathrm{Prob}(m)
and for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
.

2
Rényi entropies

Rényi entropies are parametrized with a parameter
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\cup\quantity{\infty}
, an
\alpha
-Rényi entropy of a probability vector
\mathbf{p}\in\mathrm{Prob}(n)
is defined as

H_{\alpha}(\mathbf{p})=\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\log\pqty{\sum_{x\in[n]} p_x ^\alpha}.

Notice that in the above equation at
\alpha=0,1,
and
\infty
,
H_{\alpha}
are not-well defined.
At those
\alpha
’s, the Rényi entropy is defined as the limit of
\alpha\to 0,1,
or
\infty
.

For
\alpha=0
, then entropy is called max-entropy and Hartley entropy.

\displaystyle H_{0}(\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle:=\lim_{\alpha\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\log\pqty{\sum_{x\in[n]} p_x ^\alpha}=\lim_{\alpha\to 0^{+}}\log(\sum_{x\in[n]} p_x ^\alpha)

(16)

\displaystyle=\log\pqty{\sum_{x\in[n]} \lim_{\alpha\to 0 ^+} p_x ^\alpha}

(17)

The limit
\alpha\to 0^{+}
of
p^{\alpha}
is 1 if
p\neq 0
and is
0
if
p=0
. This result in

H_{0}(\mathbf{p})=\log\absolutevalue{\rm{supp}(\p)}

(18)

where
\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{p})=\quantity{x: p_x \neq 0}
.

For
\alpha=1
, the entropy is the famous Shannon entropy, denoted with
H
,

H=H_{1}:=\lim_{\alpha\to 1}\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\log\pqty{\sum_{x\in[n]} p_i ^\alpha}.

(19)

Applying l’Hopital’s rule results in

\displaystyle H_{1}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\alpha\to 1}\frac{-1}{\sum_{x\in[n]}p_{x}^{\alpha}}\sum_{x\in[n]}p_{x}^{\alpha}\log(p_x)=\sum_{x\in[m]}-p_{x}\log p_{x},

(20)

where we use the convention
0\log(0)=0
.

For
\alpha=\infty
, the entropy is called min-entropy. We apply l’Hopital’s rule in the following derivation,

\displaystyle H_{\infty}(\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle=\lim_{\alpha\to\infty}\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\log\pqty{\sum_{x\in[n]} p_x ^\alpha}

(21)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\alpha\to\infty}\frac{-1}{\sum_{x\in[n]}p_{x}^{\alpha}}\sum_{x\in[n]}p_{x}^{\alpha}\log(p_x)

(22)

Consider the numerator terms and suppose
p_{m}=\max_{x\in[n]}p_{x}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{p_{m}^{\alpha}}\lim_{\alpha\to\infty}\sum_{x\in[n]}p_{x}^{\alpha}\log(p_x)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{x\in[n]}\lim_{\alpha\to\infty}\pqty{\frac{p_x}{p_m}}^{\alpha}\log(p_x)

\displaystyle=-n\log(p_m),

where
n
is the number of
x
’s that have
p_{x}=p_{m}
. The last equality is because of each term in the sum being equal to
0
if
p_{x}<p_{m}
and being equal to
\log(p_x)
if
p_{x}=p_{m}
. Similarly, for the denominator,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p_{m}^{\alpha}}\lim_{\alpha\to\infty}\sum_{x\in[n]}p_{x}^{\alpha}=\lim_{\alpha\to\infty}\sum_{x\in[n]}\pqty{\frac{p_x}{p_m}}^{\alpha}=n.

The min entropy is

H_{\infty}(\mathbf{p})=-\log(p_m).

(23)

Properties of Rényi entropies

Proposition 3.5
.

For any
\alpha\in[0,\infty]
, an
\alpha
-Rényi entropy is quasi concave. Moreover, for any
\alpha\in[0,1]
the entropy is concave.

Proof.

Suppose that
\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\in\mathrm{Prob}(n)
and
H_{\alpha}(\mathbf{p})\geq H_{\alpha}(\mathbf{q})
, that is
H_{\alpha}(\mathbf{q})=\min\quantity{\rH(\p),\rH(\q)}.

For
0<\alpha<1
, we have that

\sum_{x\in[n]}p_{x}^{\alpha}\geq\sum_{x\in[n]}q_{x}^{\alpha}.

(24)

On the other hand, for
\alpha>1
, we have that

\sum_{x\in[n]}p_{x}^{\alpha}\leq\sum_{x\in[n]}q_{x}^{\alpha}

(25)

Notice that the inequality sign flip because
\frac{1}{1-\alpha}
is negative when
\alpha>1
.
Consider an
\alpha
-Rényi entropy on a convex combination of
\mathbf{p}
and
\mathbf{q}
, denoted with
\mathbf{r}
,

\displaystyle H_{\alpha}(\mathbf{r})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\log\pqty{\sum_{x\in[n]} \pqty{t p_x + (1-t) q_x}^\alpha}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\log\pqty{\sum_{x\in[n]} t p_x ^\alpha+ (1-t) q_x ^\alpha}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\log\pqty{\sum_{x\in[n]} t q_x ^\alpha+ (1-t) q_x ^\alpha}=\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\log\pqty{\sum_{x\in[n]} q_x ^\alpha}=H_{\alpha}(\mathbf{q}).

The last inequality holds in
\alpha\in(0,1)
due to
\frac{1}{1-\alpha}
is positive, substitution by a smaller term leads to a smaller output of
\log
function. For
\alpha\in(0,1)
,
\frac{1}{1-\alpha}
is negative, substitution by a bigger term leads to smaller output of
\log
function.

For
\alpha=0,1,
and
\infty
, take a sequence of
\alpha
to each limit point. Since
H_{\alpha}
converges to point-wise, the quasi concavity follows.

For the concavity statement, consider
\alpha\in(0,1)
we have that

\displaystyle H_{\alpha}(\mathbf{r})

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\log\pqty{\sum_{x\in[n]} t q_x ^\alpha+ (1-t) q_x ^\alpha}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\log\pqty{t \sum_{x\in[n]} p_x ^\alpha+ (1-t) \sum_{w\in[n]} q_w ^\alpha}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{t}{1-\alpha}\log\pqty{ \sum_{x\in[n]} p_x ^\alpha}+\frac{1-t}{1-\alpha}\log\pqty{ \sum_{w\in[n]} q_w ^\alpha}=tH_{\alpha}(\mathbf{p})+(1-t)H_{\alpha}(\mathbf{q}).

∎

3
The min-entropy is Schur-concave but not concave.

It is enough to show that there is
t\in[0,1]
and
\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\in\mathrm{Prob}(n)

H_{\mathrm{min}}(t\mathbf{p}+(1-t)\mathbf{q})<tH_{\mathrm{min}}(\mathbf{p})+(1-t)H_{\mathrm{min}}(\mathbf{q}),

to conclude that
H_{\mathrm{min}}
is Schur-concave but not concave. Consider
t=1/2
,
\mathbf{p}=\mathbf{e}_{1}
, and
\mathbf{q}=\frac{1}{2}(1,1)^{T}
. The min entropy for each probability vector is

\displaystyle H_{\mathrm{min}}(\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle=-\log\max{1,0}=-\log(1)=0,

\displaystyle H_{\mathrm{min}}(\mathbf{q})

\displaystyle=-\log\max{1/2,1/2}=-\log(1/2)=1,

\displaystyle H_{\mathrm{min}}(0.5\mathbf{p}+0.5\mathbf{q})

\displaystyle=-\log\max{3/4,1/4}=-\log(3/4)=\log(4)-\log(3)\approx 0.415.

Notice that
0.5H_{\mathrm{min}}(\mathbf{p})+0.5H_{\mathrm{min}}(\mathbf{q})=0.5>0.415
.

4
Incompleteness of Rényi entropies

Is the condition
H_{\alpha}(\mathbf{p})\geq H_{\alpha}(\mathbf{q})
for any
\alpha\in[0,\infty]
sufficient to conclude that
\mathbf{p}\succ\mathbf{q}
? If the mentioned condition is sufficient, we would say that a family of Rényi entropies is a complete family of majorization monotones.
As it turns out, Rényi entropies do not give a sufficient condition for majorization. One of the simplest examples is from
[
59
]
, and we present it below.

Rényi entropies form an incomplete family of majorization monotones

Proposition 3.6
.

There is a pair of probability vectors such that

H_{\alpha}(\mathbf{p})\leq H_{\alpha}(\mathbf{q})\quad\forall\alpha\in[0,\infty]

(26)

but
\mathbf{p}\not\succ\mathbf{q}
and
\mathbf{q}\not\succ\mathbf{p}
.

Proof.

To prove this proposition, it is enough to give an example. Consider
\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\in\mathrm{Prob}(8)
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{p}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{36}\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}

\displaystyle\mathbf{q}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{36}\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}

\mathbf{q}\not\succ\mathbf{p}
because
\left\|\mathbf{q}\right\|_{(1)}<\left\|\mathbf{p}\right\|_{(1)}
and
