\mathbf{p}\not\succ\mathbf{q}
because
\left\|\mathbf{p}\right\|_{(4)}<\left\|\mathbf{q}\right\|_{(4)}
.

Next, we will show that
H_{\alpha}(\mathbf{p})\leq H_{\alpha}(\mathbf{q})
for any
\alpha\in[0,\infty]
.
First, consider at
\alpha=0
,

H_{0}(\mathbf{p})=\log\pqty{\sum_{x:p_x \neq 0} 1}=\log(6)\qquad H_{0}(\mathbf{q})=\log\pqty{\sum_{x:q_x \neq 0} 1}=\log(8).

We have that
H_{0}(\mathbf{p})<H_{0}(\mathbf{q})
. For
H
, Shannon entropy,

\displaystyle H(\mathbf{p})=-\sum_{x\in[8]}p_{x}\log\pqty{p_x}=-\frac{16}{36}\log\pqty{\frac{16}{36}}-5\times\frac{4}{36}\log\pqty{\frac{4}{36}}=\log(9)-\frac{8}{9}\log(2)

\displaystyle H(\mathbf{q})=-\sum_{x\in[8]}q_{x}\log\pqty{q_x}=-4\times\frac{8}{36}\log\pqty{\frac{8}{36}}-4\times\frac{1}{36}\log\pqty{\frac{1}{36}}=\log(9)-\frac{6}{9}\log(2)

We have that
H(\mathbf{p})<H(\mathbf{q})
. For
H_{\infty}
,

H_{\infty}(\mathbf{p})=-\log\pqty{\max_{x\in[8]} p_x}=\log(36)-\log(16)\qquad H_{\infty}(\mathbf{q})=-\log\pqty{\max_{x\in[8]} q_x}=\log(36)-\log(8)

We have that
H_{\infty}(\mathbf{p})<H_{\infty}(\mathbf{q})
.

For any other
\alpha\neq 0,1,
or
\infty
, if there is an
\alpha^{\prime}
such that
H_{\alpha^{\prime}}(\mathbf{p})>H_{\alpha^{\prime}}(\mathbf{q})
, then there is
\alpha
such that
H_{\alpha}(\mathbf{p})=H_{\alpha}(\mathbf{q})
, by continuity of the Rényi entropy on the parameter
\alpha
. We will show that there is no such
\alpha\in(0,\infty)\setminus\quantity{1}
. For any
\alpha\in(0,\infty)\setminus\quantity{1}
,

\displaystyle H_{\alpha}(\mathbf{p})=\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\log\pqty{\sum_{x\in[8]} p_x ^\alpha}

\displaystyle H_{\alpha}(\mathbf{q})=\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\log\pqty{\sum_{x\in[8]} q_x ^\alpha}.

Enforcing that both entropies are equal, we have that

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in[8]}p_{x}^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in[8]}q_{x}^{\alpha}

\displaystyle 16^{\alpha}+5\times 4^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=4\times 8^{\alpha}+4

\displaystyle 2^{4\alpha}+5\times 2^{2\alpha}

\displaystyle=4\times 2^{3\alpha}+4

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\pqty{a:=2^\alpha}\longrightarrow}\quad a^{4}-4a^{3}+5a^{2}-4

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle(a-2)(a(a-1)^{2}+2)

\displaystyle=0.

If
a=2
, then
\alpha=1
and it is outside the consideration as we suppose
\alpha\neq 1
. For all
a>0
, the term
a(a-1)^{2}+2>0
. Therefore, for any
\alpha\in[0,\infty]
,
H_{\alpha^{\prime}}(\mathbf{p})<H_{\alpha^{\prime}}(\mathbf{q})
, completing the example.
∎

4
Relative majorization and distinguishability of distributions

Majorization can be viewed as a special case of
relative majorization
, which is defined as follows.

Relative majorization

Definition 4.1
.

Suppose
\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\in\mathrm{Prob}(n)
and
\mathbf{p}^{\prime},\mathbf{q}^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Prob}(n^{\prime})
. We say that the pair of probability vectors
\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}

relatively majorizes
the pair
\mathbf{p}^{\prime},\mathbf{q}^{\prime}
and write
(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})\succ(\mathbf{p}^{\prime},\mathbf{q}^{\prime})
if there exists a stochastic matrix
E\in\mathrm{Stoch}(n^{\prime},n)
such that
(\mathbf{p}^{\prime},\mathbf{q}^{\prime})=(E\mathbf{p},E\mathbf{q})
.

Relative majorization relation captures the distinguishability between pairs of objects. One of the implications of relative majorization is that

\mathbf{p}^{\prime}=E\mathbf{p}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\mathbf{q}^{\prime}=E\mathbf{q}\implies\norm{\p- \q}_{1}\geq\norm{\p' - \q'}_{1}.

That is the distance, measured by the 1-norm, between the pair
\mathbf{p}
and
\mathbf{q}
are greater than the distance between
\mathbf{p}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{q}^{\prime}
. However, the converse direction of the implication is not true. There are pairs of probability vectors which are not majorizing one another but the 1-norm of their pairwise difference are greater than one another.

1
Properties of relative majorization

For a pair with a rational vector in the second component, they are equivalent to
\pqty{r,\u^{(n)}}
.

A pair with a rational vector.

Lemma 4.2
.

Suppose
\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\in\mathrm{Prob}(n)
and
\mathbf{q}
has rational components. Define
k
and
\quantity{k_i: i\in[n]}
to be non-negative integers such that

\mathbf{q}=\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}\quad\mathrm{and}\quad k=\sum_{i\in[n]}k_{i}.

(27)

The probability vector
\mathbf{r}
defined as

\mathbf{r}=\bigoplus_{x\in[n]}p_{x}\mathbf{u}^{(k_{x})}=\pqty{\underbrace{\frac{p_1}{k_1},\ldots,\frac{p_1}{k_1}}_{k_1~\text{repeats}},\underbrace{\frac{p_2}{k_2},\ldots,\frac{p_2}{k_2}}_{k_2~\text{repeats}},\ldots,\underbrace{\frac{p_n}{k_n},\ldots,\frac{p_n}{k_n}}_{k_n~\text{repeats}}}^{T}.

We have that
\pqty{\p,\q}\sim\pqty{\vctr,\u^{(k)}}
.

Proof.

(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})\succ(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{u}^{(k)})

We want to show that there exists
\exists E\in\mathrm{Stoch}(k,n)
such that
E\mathbf{p}=\mathbf{r}
and
E\mathbf{q}=\mathbf{u}
.
Define
E^{(x)}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{k_{x}\times n}
to be a matrix with
\mathbf{u}^{(k_{x})}
in the
x^{\text{th}}
column and zero elsewhere,

E^{(x)}=\pqty{ \*{0}^{(k_x)}, \ldots, \*{0}^{(k_x)}, \underbrace{\*{u}^{(k_x)}}_{x^{\rm{th}}-column},\*{0}^{(k_x)},\ldots, \*{0}^{(k_x)}}.

(28)

With this,

\displaystyle E^{(1)}\mathbf{p}

\displaystyle=p_{1}\mathbf{u}^{(k_{1})}=\frac{p_{1}}{k_{1}}\mathbf{1}^{(k_{1})}

\displaystyle E^{(1)}\mathbf{q}

\displaystyle=q_{1}\mathbf{u}^{(k_{1})}=\frac{k_{1}}{k}\times\frac{1}{k_{1}}\mathbf{1}^{(k_{1})}=\frac{1}{k}\mathbf{1}^{(k_{1})}.

Concatenation of each submatrix
E^{(x)}
gives the desired matrix
E
,

E=\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}.

(29)

(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{u}^{(k)})\succ(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})

Similarly, we will construct a matrix
F
such that
F\mathbf{r}=\mathbf{p}
and
F\mathbf{q}=\mathbf{u}^{(k)}
. Define
F^{(x)}
as

F^{(x)}=\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}.

With a concatenation, we define
F

F=\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}.

Application of
F
on the vector
\mathbf{r}
will take a sum
\sum_{i\in[k_{x}]}\frac{p_{x}}{k_{x}}=p_{x}
and assign the result to the
x
-th row resulting in the vector
\mathbf{p}
. Similarly, application of
F
on the vector
\mathbf{u}^{(k)}
will sum
\frac{1}{k}
for
k_{x}
times and assign the result to the
x
-th row resulting in the vector
\mathbf{q}
.
∎

2
Hypothesis testing and testing region

Specifically, relative majorization is related to the operational task called hypothesis testing. The hypothesis testing is a task characterized with the following conditions and constraints.

Hypothesis testing

Given:
a set of samples
\quantity{x_1, x_2, \ldots, x_n}
drawn independently with an unknown source.

Promise:
the distribution of this source is either
\mathbf{p}
or
\mathbf{q}
.

Goal:
determine which distribution the samples are drawn from.

Notice that the goal of this problem is to determine the source of the sample, a common task in the field of statistics. The optimal solution to this problem is generally not by a perfect accuracy but with a bounded error. Suppose that the distribution
\mathbf{p}
is our null hypothesis and
\mathbf{q}
is an alternative hypothesis. There are two possible types of errors.

1.

Type-I error:
a rejection of the null hypothesis when the null hypothesis is true. That is inferring
\mathbf{q}
as the source when
\mathbf{p}
is actually the source.

2.

Type-II error:
a failure to reject the null hypothesis when the null hypothesis is false. That is inferring
\mathbf{p}
as the source when
\mathbf{q}
is actually the source.

We can model a decision made based on a length-
n
string of numbers
\mathbf{x}=x_{1}x_{2}\ldots x_{n}
with a decision function
f:[m]^{n}\to\quantity{0,1}
. If
f(\mathbf{x})=0
, then we deterministically infer that the source is
\mathbf{p}
. On the other hand, if
f(\mathbf{x})=1
, then we deterministically infer that the source is
\mathbf{q}
, rejecting the null hypothesis. Now, a probabilistic decision-making, where we have probabilistically apply one of
\ell
decision functions
\quantity{f_j:j\in[\ell]}
with probability
t_{j}
. Define a function
g:[m]^{n}\to[0,1]
where

g(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{j\in[\ell]}t_{j}f_{j}(\mathbf{x}).

The function
g(\mathbf{x})
evaluate to 0 or 1 means deterministically infer
\mathbf{p}
or
\mathbf{q}
. The values between
0
and
1
tell the probability of rejecting the null hypothesis. The function
g
is linear; it can be rewritten as

g(\mathbf{x})=\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{x}}\cdot\mathbf{t}

for some vector
\mathbf{t}\in[0,1]^{n}
, where
\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{x}}
is a standard basis vector with the label
\mathbf{x}
in base-
m
. That is each probabilistic decision-making can be represented with a unique vector
\mathbf{t}
.

A probability of type-I error, denoted with
\alpha(\mathbf{t})
, conditioned on the source being
\mathbf{p}
is

\alpha(\mathbf{t})=\sum_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}:t_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}=1}\mathrm{Prob}(\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{x}^{\prime}|\text{ source is }\mathbf{p})=1-\mathbf{t}\cdot\mathbf{p}^{\otimes n}.

(30)

On the other hand, a probability of type-II error, denoted with
\beta(\mathbf{t})
, conditioned on the source being
\mathbf{q}
is

\beta(\mathbf{t})=\sum_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}:t_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}=0}\mathrm{Prob}(\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{x}^{\prime}|\text{ source is }\mathbf{q})=\mathbf{t}\cdot\mathbf{q}^{\otimes n}.

(31)

There are many strategies in optimally determined the source. The strategy that related to relative majorization is an asymmetric method, where the type-II error is minimized under a bounded type-I error. Suppose that the error bound of type-I error is
\varepsilon
, the optimal type-II error
\beta_{n}^{*}(\varepsilon)
given
n
-sample in this approach is

\beta_{n}^{*}(\varepsilon)=\min\quantity{\t\cdot\q^{\otimes n} : \t\in[0,1]^n \text{ and } 1- \t\cdot\p^{\otimes n} \leq\varepsilon}.

(32)

In the scenario where there is only one sample drawn, relative majorization compares the optimal type-II error
\beta^{*}(\varepsilon)
between two cases of probability vector pairs for any
\varepsilon\in[0,1]
. Notice that the subscript
n
is dropped for simplicity.

Relative majorization and hypothesis testing

Proposition 4.3
.

Suppose
\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\in\mathrm{Prob}(n)
and
\mathbf{p}^{\prime},\mathbf{q}^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Prob}(n^{\prime})
. We have that
(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})\succ(\mathbf{p}^{\prime},\mathbf{q}^{\prime})
if and only if
\beta^{*}(\varepsilon)(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})<\beta^{*}(\varepsilon)(\mathbf{p}^{\prime},\mathbf{q}^{\prime})
for any
\varepsilon\in[0,1]
.

The proof of this proposition is giving by the Theorem
4.10
. All possible value of
\alpha(\mathbf{t})
and
\beta(\mathbf{t})
in a single-shot regime for a pair
(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})
form a
testing region
.

Testing region

Definition 4.4
.

Suppose
\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\in\mathrm{Prob}(n)
. The
testing region

\mathfrak{T}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})
is a subset of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
defined as

\mathfrak{T}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})=\quantity{(\t\cdot\p,\t\cdot\q): \t\in[0,1]^n }.

(33)

A testing region of a pair
\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}
is convex, closed, and symmetric in such a way that
(x,y)\in\mathfrak{T}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})\implies(1-x,1-y)\in\mathfrak{T}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})
.

Proposition 4.5
.

A testing region is convex and rotational symmetric around
(0.5,0.5)
.

Proof.

Suppose
\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\in\mathrm{Prob}(n)
and
r=(r_{1},r_{2})
and
s=(s_{1},s_{2})
are in the testing region
\mathfrak{T}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})
.
For convexity, a convex sum of the two points is
tr+(1-t)s=(tr_{1}-(1-t)s_{1},tr_{2}-(1-t)s_{2})
. Using the fact that both points are in
\mathfrak{T}
, we have that for some
\mathbf{t}_{1},\mathbf{t}_{2}\in[0,1]^{n}

\displaystyle tr+(1-t)s

\displaystyle=\big(t(\mathbf{p}\cdot\mathbf{t}_{1})-(1-t)(\mathbf{p}\cdot\mathbf{t}_{2})~,~t(\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{t}_{1})-(1-t)(\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{t}_{2})\big)

\displaystyle=\big(\mathbf{p}\cdot(t\mathbf{t}_{1}+(1-t)\mathbf{t}_{2})~,~\mathbf{q}\cdot(t\mathbf{t}_{1}+(1-t)\mathbf{t}_{2})\big).

Since
(t\mathbf{t}_{1}+(1-t)\mathbf{t}_{2})\in[0,1]^{n}
, the convex sum of two points are in
\mathfrak{T}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})
, showing convexity of the testing region.

For the symmetry, since
(r_{1},r_{2})=(\mathbf{t}\cdot\mathbf{p},\mathbf{t}\cdot\mathbf{q})
, testing with vector
(\mathbf{1}-\mathbf{t})
gives
(1-r_{1},1-r_{2})
.
∎

It’s important to introduce the following form which make the subsequent definition and theorem easier to state.

The standard form of relative majorization

Definition 4.6
.

A pair of probability vectors
\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\in\mathrm{Prob}(n)
,
(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})
, is in the
standard form
if

1.

there is no
x\in[n]
such that
p_{x}=q_{x}=0
, and

2.

both vectors are arranged such that the ratios
p_{x}/q_{x}
are non-increasing orders, i.e.

\frac{p_{1}}{q_{1}}\geq\frac{p_{2}}{q_{2}}\geq\ldots\geq\frac{p_{n}}{q_{n}}.

The convention used here is
p_{x}/q_{x}=\infty
if
p_{x}>0
and
q_{x}=0
.

Any pair of probability vectors
(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})
can be put in the standard form by permuting elements and remove any entries where
p_{x}
and
q_{x}
are both zero.

The first application of the standard form is in describing
Lorenz curves
, a boundary of a testing region. A Lorenz curve can be described by its extreme points. Because of the symmetry, it is enough to gives a lower boundary.

Lower Lorenz curve

Theorem 4.7
.

Suppose
\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\in\mathrm{Prob}(n)
. A lower Lorenz curve of the testing region
\mathfrak{T}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})
is given by a linear interpolation of points
\quantity{(a_k,b_k): k\in[n]}
where

(a_{k},b_{k})=\pqty{\sum_{x\in[k]} p_x, \sum_{x\in[k]} q_x, }

(34)

and
\pqty{\p,\q}
is in the standard form.

Proof.

Suppose
f:[0,1]\to[0,1]
is a lower bound for a testing region
\mathfrak{T}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})
. The lower bound
f(r)
is a minimization of
\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{t}
over a valid testing vector
\mathbf{t}
subjected to the condition
\mathbf{p}\cdot\mathbf{t}=r
,

f(r)=\min\quantity{\q\cdot\t\vert~t\in[0,1]^n , \p\cdot\t= r}.

(35)

If there exists a vector
\mathbf{t}\in[0,1]^{n}
such that
\mathbf{p}\cdot\mathbf{t}>r
then there is a vector
\mathbf{t}^{\prime}
that satisfies the equality as well. If
\mathbf{p}\cdot\mathbf{t}=r/a
for some
a\in(0,1)
, then
\mathbf{p}\cdot a\mathbf{t}=r
and
a\mathbf{t}\in[0,1]^{n}
.
Therefore, the condition
\mathbf{t}\in[0,1]^{n}
can be replaced with
\mathbf{e}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{t}\leq 1
and
\mathbf{t}\geq 0
. This put
f(r)
to be an optimal value from a linear program,

\displaystyle\min\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{t}

\displaystyle=:\alpha

\displaystyle\text{subjected to}\quad\mathbf{p}\cdot\mathbf{t}

\displaystyle\geq r

\displaystyle-\mathbf{e}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{t}

\displaystyle\geq-1\quad\forall i\in[n]

\displaystyle\text{and }\quad\mathbf{t}

\displaystyle\geq 0.

Define
A=(\mathbf{p},-\mathbf{e}_{1},-\mathbf{e}_{2},\ldots,-\mathbf{e}_{n})^{T}
and
\mathbf{b}=\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}
. The dual of this linear program is

\displaystyle\max\mathbf{b}\cdot\mathbf{y}

\displaystyle=:\beta

\displaystyle\text{subjected to}\quad A^{T}\mathbf{y}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{q}

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad\mathbf{y}

\displaystyle\geq 0.

Define
\mathbf{y}=\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}
where
s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{++}
. Then,

\mathbf{b}\cdot\mathbf{y}=\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}\cdot\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}=rs-\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{1}^{(n)}

The constraint can be put as

A^{T}\mathbf{y}=s\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{v}\leq\mathbf{q}\iff s\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q}\leq\mathbf{v}.

For linear programming, the strong duality holds if the feasibility set of the primal program is not empty. Since
\mathbf{t}=\mathbf{1}
gives
\mathbf{p}\cdot\mathbf{t}=1\geq r
for any
r\in[0,1]
, the feasibility set is not empty. Therefore, the strong duality holds,
\alpha=\beta
.

Next, we consider the dual problem. Notice that
rs-\mathbf{1}\cdot\mathbf{v}
is decreasing with
\norm{\vctv}_{1}
. For each
s
, the vector
\mathbf{v}
with the smallest
\norm{\*{v}}_{1}
is the optimal
\mathbf{v}
, i.e.

\mathbf{v}=\pqty{s \p- \q}_{+}

where the
\pqty{\p}_{+}
is the application of positive cut-off into each element of a vector
\mathbf{p}
. Define
s_{x}=q_{x}/p_{x}
and the objective function becomes

\displaystyle f(r)=rs-\mathbf{1}^{(n)}\cdot\mathbf{v}=rs-\sum_{x\in[n]}(sp_{x}-q_{x})_{+}=rs-\sum_{x\in[n]}p_{x}(s-s_{x})

For any
s\geq 0
, there exists an
\ell\in\quantity{0,1,2,\ldots,n+1}
such that
s_{\ell}\leq s<s_{\ell+1}
, where
s_{0}=0
and
s_{n+1}=\infty
. The dual function becomes

f(r)=rs-\sum_{x\in[ell]}p_{x}(s-s_{x})=rs-\sum_{x\in[\ell]}sp_{x}-q_{x}=s(r-a_{\ell})-b_{\ell}.

Next, we will apply divide-and-conquer strategy to search for the optimal
s
in each interval
[s_{\ell},s_{\ell+1})
.

f(r)=\max_{\ell\in\quantity{0,1,\dots,n}}\sup_{s\in[s_{\ell},s_{\ell+1})}\quantity{ s(r-a_\ell) + b_{\ell}}.

(36)

If the optimal
\ell
satisfies
\ell\leq k
where
r\in[a_{k},a_{k+1}]
then
r-a_{\ell}\geq 0
,
M_{\ell}:=s(r-a_{\ell})+b_{\ell}=s_{\ell+1}(r-a_{\ell})+b_{\ell}.

Moreover,
\ell=k
gives the largest
M_{\ell}
because
M_{\ell}
is increasing with
\ell
. To see this increasing, consider

\displaystyle M_{\ell}-M_{\ell-1}

\displaystyle=s_{\ell+1}(r-a_{\ell})+b_{\ell}-s_{\ell}(r-a_{l-1})-b_{l-1}

\displaystyle=s_{\ell+1}(r-a_{\ell})-s_{\ell}(r-a_{\ell-1})+q_{\ell}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\pqty{a_{\ell-1} = a_\ell- p_\ell}\longrightarrow}

\displaystyle=s_{\ell+1}(r-a_{\ell})-s_{\ell}(r-a_{\ell}+p_{\ell})+q_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{(s_{\ell+1}-s_{\ell})}_{\geq 0}\underbrace{(r-a_{\ell})}_{\geq 0}+\underbrace{q_{\ell}-s_{\ell}p_{\ell}}_{=0}\geq 0.

On the other hand, if the optimal
\ell
satisfies
\ell>k
, then
r-a_{\ell}<0
,

\sup_{s\in[s_{\ell},s_{\ell+1}]}\quantity{s(r-a_\ell) + b_\ell}=s_{\ell}(r-a_{\ell})+b_{\ell}

\ell=k+1
gives the optimal, since
M_{\ell}
is decreasing with
\ell
. The decreasing part can be seen by modifies the above chain of equalities.
Now, we compare candidate of
\ell
,
\ell=k,k+1
.

\displaystyle M_{k}

\displaystyle=s_{k+1}(r-a_{k})+b_{k}

\displaystyle M_{k+1}

\displaystyle=s_{k+1}(r-a_{k+1})+b_{k+1}

Apply
a_{k}=a_{k+1}-p_{k+1}
,

M_{k}=s_{k+1}(r-a_{k}+p_{k+1})+b_{k}=s_{k+1}(r-a_{k})+q_{k+1}+b_{k}=M_{k+1}.

Therefore, the function
f(r)
is

f(r)=s_{k+1}(r-a_{k})+b_{k}\quad\text{for }r\in[s_{k},s_{k+1})

(37)

which is a linear interpolation between points
\quantity{(a_k, b_k): k\in\qty{0,1,2,\dots,n}}.

∎

Example 4.8
.

Suppose
\mathbf{p}=(0.9,0.1,0)^{T},\mathbf{q}=(0.1,0.8,0.1)^{T}
. The Lower Lorenz curve of
(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})
is an interpolation of

\displaystyle\pqty{a_0,b_0}=(0,0);

\displaystyle\pqty{a_1,b_1}=(0.9,0.1);

\displaystyle\pqty{a_1,b_1}=(1,0.9);

\displaystyle\pqty{a_2,b_2}=(1,1).

Once the lower Lorenz curve for the pair is known, one can compute the upper Lorenz curve of the pair by interpolation of
(\bar{a}_{i},\bar{b}_{i})=(1-a_{i},1-b_{i})
. See the Figure
1
for an illustration of this example.

0.1
0.9
0.9
0.1
1
1

Figure 1
:
The testing region of the pair
\mathbf{p}=(0.9,0.1,0)^{T},\mathbf{q}=(0.1,0.8,0.1)^{T}
.

One useful consequence of the characterization of the lower Lorenz curve is the removal of a point when three or more points are collinear.

Theorem 4.9
.

Suppose
\mathbf{p}
,
\mathbf{q}\in\mathrm{Prob}(n)
. There is a pair of probability vectors
\pqty{\p_0, \q_0}
equivalent with
\pqty{\p,\q}
such that there is no repeating ratio of
p_{0,x}/q_{0,x}
for any
x\in[n^{\prime}]
.

Proof.

First, we will turn a pair
\pqty{\p,\q}
into its equivalent pair
\pqty{\p_0, \q_0}
where
\pqty{\p_0,\q_0}
is a pair where
\mathfrak{T}\pqty{\p_0, \q_0}=\mathfrak{T}\pqty{\p, \q}
but there is no
x
such that the ratio between elements of
\mathbf{p}_{0}
and
\mathbf{q}_{0}
are not repeated, i.e. there is no
x\in[n]
that makes
p_{0,x}/q_{0,x}=p_{0,x+1}/q_{0,x+1}
. Suppose
\pqty{\p,\q}
has exactly one repeat ratio,
\frac{p_{x}}{q_{x}}=\frac{p_{x+1}}{q_{x+1}}
. Then, we set
E\in\mathrm{Stoch}(n-1,n)
to be a matrix representation of a linear map
g:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
such that

g(p_{1},\ldots,p_{x},p_{x+1},\ldots,p_{n})=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{x}+p_{x+1},\ldots,p_{n}).

To see that this operation is linear, consider
g
on
\alpha\mathbf{p}+\beta\mathbf{q}
for any
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
,

g(\alpha\mathbf{p}+\beta\mathbf{q})=\pqty{\alpha p_1 + \beta q_1, \ldots,\alpha(p_x+p_{x+1}) + \beta(q_x - q_{x+1}), \ldots, \alpha p_n + \beta q_n}=\alpha g(\mathbf{p})+\beta g(\mathbf{q}).

The linear map
g
preserves probability vectors. This operation has a left inverse,
h
, suppose
\mathbf{r},\mathbf{s}\in\mathrm{Prob}(n-1)
,

h(\mathbf{r})=\pqty{r_1, \ldots, r_{x-1},r_x \frac{p_x}{p_{x}+p_{x+1}}, r_x \frac{p_{x+1}}{p_{x}+p_{x+1}}, r_{x+1}, \ldots, r_{n}}.

Image of
h
on
f(\mathbf{p})
is
\mathbf{p}
and the image on
f(\mathbf{q})
is
\mathbf{q}
. The later is less obvious,

h(f(\mathbf{q}))=\pqty{q_x + q_{x+1}}\pqty{\frac{p_x }{p_x + p_{x+1}}}=\pqty{\frac{q_x + q_{x+1}}{p_x + p_{x+1}}}p_{x}=\frac{q_{x}}{p_{x}}p_{x}=q_{x}.

(38)

Since
h
is linear, there exists a matrix representing
h
, call it
F
. This
F
is stochastic since
h
preserve probability vectors.
∎

The testing region plays important role in characterizing relative majorization with the hypothesis testing task via the Blackwell’s theorem.

Blackwell’s theorem

Theorem 4.10
.

Suppose
\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\in\mathrm{Prob}(n)
and
\mathbf{p}^{\prime},\mathbf{q}^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Prob}(n^{\prime})
. The following are equivalent

1.

(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})\succ(\mathbf{p}^{\prime},\mathbf{q}^{\prime})

2.

for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
,
\norm{\p- t\q}_{1}\geq\norm{\p' - t\q'}_{1}

3.

\mathfrak{T}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})\supseteq\mathfrak{T}(\mathbf{p}^{\prime},\mathbf{q}^{\prime})

Proof.

1.\implies 2.

Suppose
\pqty{\p,\q}\succ\pqty{\p',\q'}
. For some
E\in\mathrm{Stoch}(n,n^{\prime})
,
\pqty{\p',\q'}=(E\mathbf{p},E\mathbf{q})
. For any
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we have that

\norm{\p'-t\q'}_{1}=\norm{E\p-tE\q}_{1}\geq\norm{\p- t \q}_{1}.

(39)

2.\implies 3.

Recall the lower Lorenz curve
f(r)
defined in previous proof. Specifically,
f_{(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})}(r)
is a function that gives a lower Lorenz curves for the pair
(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})
,

f(r)=\max_{s\geq 0}\quantity{sr - (s\p- \q)_+ \cdot\*{1}^{(n)}}

Define
t=1/s
,

f(r)=\max_{t>0}\quantity{\frac{1}{t} \pqty{r - \pqty{ \p- t \q}_+ \cdot\*{1}^{(n)}}}.

Consider,

\displaystyle(\mathbf{p}-t\mathbf{q})_{+}\cdot\mathbf{1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\pqty{ \norm{\p- t \q}_1 + \pqty{\p- t\q}}\cdot\mathbf{1}^{(n)}=\frac{1}{2}\norm{\p-t\q}_{1}+\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}t.

This

f(r)=\max_{t>0}\quantity{\frac{1}{t} \pqty{r - \frac{1}{2} \norm{\p-t\q}_1 + \frac{1}{2} - \frac{1}{2}t}}.

Suppose a lower Lorenz curve for the pair
(\mathbf{p}^{\prime},\mathbf{q}^{\prime})
is given by
f_{(\mathbf{p}^{\prime},\mathbf{q}^{\prime})}(r)
. Since
\norm{\p-t\q}_{1}\geq\norm{\p' - t \q'}_{1}
for all
t\in\geq 0
, therefore
f_{(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})}(r)\geq f_{(\mathbf{p}^{\prime},\mathbf{q}^{\prime})}(r)
, i.e.
T(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})\supseteq T(\mathbf{p}^{\prime},\mathbf{q}^{\prime})
.

3.\implies 1.

The proof of this will be postponed until we proved all required lemmas.
∎

Lemma 4.11
.

Suppose
\mathbf{q},\mathbf{q}^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Prob}(n)
has a rational component. If
\mathfrak{T}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})\supseteq\mathfrak{T}(\mathbf{p}^{\prime},\mathbf{q}^{\prime})
, then
\pqty{\p,\q}\succ\pqty{\p',\q'}
.

Proof.

From Lemma
4.2
, suppose
\pqty{\vctr',\*{u}^{(k)}}\sim\pqty{\*{p},\*{q}}
and
\pqty{\vctr',\*{u}^{(k)}}\sim\pqty{\p',\q'}
. Notice that we can make both
\mathbf{r}
and
\mathbf{r^{\prime}}
to be the same dimension. If two pairs of probability vectors are equivalent, then their testing regions are necessarily the same. This can be verified quickly by applying a chain of implication in the previous proof
1.\implies 2.\implies 3.
in both directions:
\succ
and
\prec
. Then, the premise is equivalent to

\mathfrak{T}(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{u}^{(k)})\supseteq\mathfrak{T}(\mathbf{r}^{\prime},\mathbf{u}^{(k)}).

(40)

The above containment implies that
\mathbf{r}\succ\mathbf{r}^{\prime}
and consequently
(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{u}^{(k)})\succ(\mathbf{r}^{\prime},\mathbf{u}^{(k)})
. Since we have,
\pqty{\p,\q}\succ(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{u}^{(k)})\succ(\mathbf{r}^{\prime},\mathbf{u}^{(k)})\succ(\mathbf{p}^{\prime},\mathbf{q}^{\prime})
, we have
(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})\succ(\mathbf{p}^{\prime},\mathbf{q}^{\prime})
by transitivity of the relative-majorization preorder.
∎

Lemma 4.12
.

Suppose
\mathfrak{T}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})\supseteq\mathfrak{T}(\mathbf{p}^{\prime},\mathbf{q}^{\prime})
. For any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, there exist
\mathbf{q}^{\pqty{\varepsilon}}\in\mathrm{Prob}(n)
and
\mathbf{q}^{\prime\pqty{\varepsilon}}\in\mathrm{Prob}(n^{\prime})
such that
\mathbf{q}^{(\varepsilon)}\approx_{\varepsilon}\mathbf{q}
,
\mathbf{q}^{\prime(\varepsilon)}\approx_{\varepsilon}\mathbf{q}^{\prime}
, and
\mathfrak{T}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}^{(\varepsilon)})\supseteq\mathfrak{T}(\mathbf{p}^{\prime},\mathbf{q}^{\prime(\varepsilon)})
.

Remark
.

\mathbf{p}\approx_{\varepsilon}\mathbf{q}
if and only if
\frac{1}{2}\norm{\p- \q}_{1}\leq\varepsilon
.

Which norm we are using doesn’t matter

Proof.

The idea of the proof is show that, to make component rationals, we can add small number
\varepsilon_{x}
to the almost all elements of
\mathbf{q}
and subtract the sum of all
\varepsilon_{x}
from the last element. The subtraction from the last element ensures that the final vector is a probability vector. In doing this, the lower Lorenz curve will move up a bit. Similarly, we can also move a Lorenz curve down a little. To keep the proof simple, we want to preserve the ordering of
\quantity{p_x/q_x}_{x\in[n]}
after adding the small amount into the elements of
\mathbf{q}
. See Figure
2
for visualization of this process.

\frac{p_{1}}{q_{1}}
\frac{p_{2}}{q_{2}}
\frac{p_{n}}{q_{n}}
\ldots
\ldots
\frac{p_{x}}{q_{x}}

Figure 2
:
Ratios between elements of
\mathbf{p}
and
\mathbf{q}
on a real line. The leftmost point is
p_{n}/q_{n}
. If
p_{n-1}/q_{n-1}=p_{n}/q_{n}
, then adding small number to
q_{n-1}
will shift the fraction
p_{n-1}/q_{n-1}
to the left while
p_{n}/q_{n}
is shifted to the right changing the ordering of
\quantity{p_x/q_x}_{x\in[n]}
.

Suppose that the pair
\pqty{\p',\q'}
has that the last
n^{\prime}
elements with the identical ratio
\frac{p_{n}}{q_{n}}
.
Pick
\vec{\varepsilon}=\pqty{\varepsilon_1, \ldots, \varepsilon_n}
to be positive except at the last
n^{\prime}
elements which will be set to be
-\frac{1}{n^{\prime}}\sum_{x\in[n-n^{\prime}]}\varepsilon_{x}
. By denseness of
\mathbb{Q}
, we can pick
\vec{\varepsilon}
such that

1.

it shifts all
q^{\prime}_{x}
to be rational numbers,
q^{\prime}_{x}+\varepsilon_{x}\in\mathbb{Q}_{+}
for all
x\in[n-n^{\prime}]
,

2.

its sum is not greater than the element
q_{n}
, i.e.
\sum_{x\in[n-1]}\varepsilon_{x}\leq q_{n}
,

3.

the shift is smaller than
\varepsilon/2
, i.e.
\sum_{x\in[n-1]}\varepsilon_{x}<\varepsilon/2
, and

4.

preserve the ordering

\frac{p_{1}}{q_{1}^{\prime(\varepsilon)}}\leq\frac{p_{2}}{q_{2}^{\prime(\varepsilon)}}\leq\ldots\leq\frac{p_{n}}{q_{n}^{\prime(\varepsilon)}}.

(41)

We denote the shifted probability vector
\mathbf{q}^{\prime(\varepsilon)}=\mathbf{q}^{\prime}+\vec{\varepsilon}
. The points on the lower Lorenz curve of
\mathfrak{T}(\mathbf{p}^{\prime},\mathbf{q}^{\prime(\varepsilon)})
are

(a_{k},b^{\prime}_{k})=\pqty{\sum_{x\in[k]} p' _x , \sum_{x\in[k]} q'_x + \varepsilon_x}.

Next, we pick
\vec{\nu}
to define an approximate
\mathbf{q}^{(\varepsilon)}
. Suppose similarly that the pair
\pqty{\p,\q}
has last
m
identical elements. Similar to
\vec{\varepsilon}
, by denseness of
\mathbb{Q}
we can pick the vector
\vec{\nu}
to be such that

1.

it shifts all
q_{x}
to be rational numbers,
q_{x}-\nu_{x}\in\mathbb{Q}_{+}
for all
x\in[n-m]
,

2.

its sum is not greater than the element
q_{n}
, i.e.
\sum_{x\in[n-m]}\nu_{x}\leq q_{n}
,

3.

the shift is smaller than
\varepsilon/2
, i.e.
\sum_{x\in[n-m]}\nu_{x}<\varepsilon/2
,

4.

preserve the ordering

\frac{p_{1}}{q_{1}^{(\varepsilon)}}\leq\frac{p_{2}}{q_{2}^{(\varepsilon)}}\leq\ldots\leq\frac{p_{n}}{q_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}},

(42)

Since
LC(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})
is under
LC(\mathbf{p}^{\prime},\mathbf{q}^{\prime})
, shifting one up and another one down will not make lower Lorenz curves cross, i.e.
LC(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}^{(\varepsilon)})
is under
LC(\mathbf{p}^{\prime},\mathbf{q}^{\prime(\varepsilon)})
. Therefore,
\mathfrak{T}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}^{(\varepsilon)})\supseteq\mathfrak{T}(\mathbf{p}^{\prime},\mathbf{q}^{\prime(\varepsilon)})

∎

Now we can proof the direction
3.\implies 1.

Proof of
3.\implies 1.
.

Set
\quantity{\varepsilon_\ell: \varepsilon_\ell\in(0,1), \ell\in\bb{N}}
converging to zero. By the Lemma
4.12
, there are probability vectors
\mathbf{q}^{(\varepsilon_{\ell})}
and
\mathbf{q}^{\prime(\varepsilon_{\ell})}
such that
\mathfrak{T}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}^{(\varepsilon)})\supseteq\mathfrak{T}(\mathbf{p}^{\prime},\mathbf{q}^{\prime(\varepsilon)})
for any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
. By the Lemma
4.2
,
(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}^{(\varepsilon_{\ell})})\succ(\mathbf{p}^{\prime},\mathbf{q}^{\prime(\varepsilon_{\ell})})
. Define
E^{(\ell)}\in\mathrm{Stoch}(n^{\prime},n)
to be such that
(\mathbf{p}^{\prime},\mathbf{q}^{\prime(\varepsilon_{\ell})})=(E^{(\ell)}\mathbf{p},E^{(\ell)}\mathbf{q}^{(\varepsilon_{\ell})})
. Since a set of stochastic matrices is compact
\quantity{E^{(\ell)}}_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
has a converging subsequence. Set
E^{\pqty{\ell_j}}
to be the subsequent and supose that it converges to
E
. Therefore,

\displaystyle E\mathbf{p}

\displaystyle=\lim_{j\to\infty}E^{\pqty{\ell_j}}\mathbf{p}=\lim_{j\to\infty}\mathbf{p}^{\prime}=\mathbf{p}^{\prime}

\displaystyle E\mathbf{q}

\displaystyle=\pqty{\lim_{j\to\infty} E^{\pqty{\ell_j}}}\pqty{\lim_{k\to\infty} \q^{(\varepsilon_k)}}=\lim_{j\to\infty}E^{\pqty{\ell_j}}\mathbf{q}^{(\varepsilon_{\ell})}=\mathbf{q}^{\prime},

proving that
\pqty{\p,\q}\succ\pqty{\p',\q'}
. This completes the proof of the Blackwell theorem.
∎

5
Monotones of relative majorization

1
Divergences

A divergence is a monotone for relative majorization. It quantifies how far apart one distribution is from another one. One of the most important divergences is called Kullback-Leibler divergence put forward by Solomon Kullback and Richard Leibler in 1951 to generalize Shannon entropy
[
60
]
. Later, a definition of divergence grounded on two axioms is purposed by Gilad Gour and Marco Tomamichel in 2020
[
61
]
.

Divergence

Definition 5.1
.

A function
\mathbb{D}:\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathrm{Prob}(n)\times\mathrm{Prob}(n)\to\mathbb{R}\cup\quantity{\infty}
is a
divergence
if

1.

(Data processing inequality: DPI) For any stochastic map
E\in\mathrm{Stoch}(m,n)
for any
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\in\mathrm{Prob}(n)
, an inequality
\mathbb{D}(E\mathbf{p}\|E\mathbf{q})\leq\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{p}\|\mathbf{q})
holds.

2.

(Normalization)
\mathbb{D}(1\|1)=0
.

Notice that the data processing inequality is the condition that make a divergence a relative majorization monotone.

One immediate result is non-negativity of the divergence. To see this, consider any probability pair
(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})
. The pair majorizes
(1,1)
. By the DPI,

(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})\succ(1,1)\implies D(\mathbf{p}\|\mathbf{q})\geq D(1\|1)=0.

(43)

with the equality if (but not only if)
\mathbf{p}=\mathbf{q}
.
For example,

\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{p}\|\mathbf{q})=\begin{cases}0&\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{p})=\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{q})\\
\infty&\mathrm{otherwise}\end{cases}.

This is a divergence. Suppose that
\mathbf{p}
and
\mathbf{q}
are
n
-dimensional probability vector. We want to shows that
\mathbb{D}
is a divergence, i.e. for any
E\in\mathrm{Stoch}(m,n)
, any
m\in\mathbb{N}
, we have that
\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{p}\|\mathbf{q})\geq\mathbb{D}(E\mathbf{p},E\mathbf{q})
. Notice that if
\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{p})\neq\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{q})
, then the proof of DPI is trivial. Suppose
\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{p})=\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{q})
. We claim that
\mathrm{supp}(E\mathbf{p})=\mathrm{supp}(E\mathbf{q})
as well. This is because,

\mathrm{supp}\pqty{E\p}=\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{p})\cap\bigcup_{i\in[n]}\mathrm{supp}(E\mathbf{e}_{i})

and similarly with
E\mathbf{q}
.

Any convex function can be extended to a divergence. Such divergence is called an
f
-divergence.

f
-divergence

Theorem 5.2
.

If
f:\pqty{0,\infty}\to\mathbb{R}
is a convex function such that
f(1)=0
, then a function
D_{f}:\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathrm{Prob}(n)\times\mathrm{Prob}(n)\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

D_{f}(\mathbf{p}\|\mathbf{q})=\sum_{x\in[n]}q_{x}f\pqty{\frac{p_x}{q_x}}

(44)

is a divergence. The conventions are

f(0)=\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}f(r),\quad 0f\left(\frac{0}{0}\right)=\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}rf\pqty{\frac{r}{r}}=0,\quad 0f\pqty{\frac{a}{0}}=\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}rf\pqty{\frac{a}{r}}=a\lim_{s\to 0^{+}}sf\pqty{\frac{1}{s}}.

Proof.

Normalization follows directly from
f(1)=0
. To see that
D_{f}
satisfies data processing inequality, suppose that
\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\in\mathrm{Prob}(n)
and
E=\pqty{e_{x\vert y}:x\in[m],y\in[n]}\in\mathrm{Stoch}(m,n)
be any stochastic matrix. Denote
\pqty{\vctr,\s}=\pqty{E\p,E\q}
. The image of
D_{f}
on the pair
(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{s})
is

\displaystyle D_{f}(\mathbf{r}\|\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in[m]}s_{x}f\pqty{\frac{r_x}{s_x}}

(45)

Define

t_{y|x}=\frac{e_{x|y}q_{y}}{s_{x}}.

Notice that
t_{y|x}\geq 0
and
\sum_{y\in[n]}t_{y|x}=\sum_{y\in[n]}\frac{e_{x|y}q_{y}}{s_{x}}=1
. The fraction
r_{x}/s_{x}
can be written as

\frac{r_{x}}{s_{x}}=\sum_{y\in[n]}\frac{e_{x|y}p_{y}}{s_{x}}=\sum_{y\in[n]}t_{y|x}\frac{p_{y}}{q_{y}}.

Using convexity of
f
, we have a chain of inequalities

\displaystyle D_{f}(\mathbf{r}\|\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in[m]}s_{x}f\pqty{\sum_{y\in[m]} t_{y\vert x} \frac{p_y}{q_y}}\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in[m]\\
y\in[n]\end{subarray}}s_{x}t_{y|x}f\pqty{\frac{p_y}{q_y}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in[n]}\underbrace{\sum_{x\in[m]}e_{x|y}}_{=1}q_{y}f\pqty{\frac{p_y}{q_y}}=D_{f}(\mathbf{p}\|\mathbf{q}),

showing that
D_{f}
satisfies DPI.
∎

This theorem gives us a large class of divergences. The famous KL-divergence is in this class.

Example 5.3
.

Kullback–Leibler divergence (KL divergence) is an
f
-divergence where

f(r)=r\log(r).

(46)

This gives a KL divergence of a pair of probability vectors
\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\in\mathrm{Prob}(n)
to be

D(\mathbf{p}\|\mathbf{q})=\sum_{x\in[n]}q_{x}\frac{p_{x}}{q_{x}}\log\pqty{\frac{p_x}{q_x}}=\sum_{x\in[n]}p_{x}(\log\pqty{p_x}-\log\pqty{q_x}).

(47)

This divergence is in some literature called the relative entropy.

Example 5.4
.

The chi-square distance

\chi^{2}(\mathbf{p}\|\mathbf{q})=\sum_{x\in[n]}\frac{\pqty{p_x - q_x}^{2}}{q_{x}}

(48)

is an
f
-divergence where

f(r)=\pqty{r-1}^{2}.

(49)

2
Relative entropies

Similarly to the previous section on entropy. A monotone of relative majorization that is additive under tensor product and satisfies an additional normalization condition is called a relative entropy.

Relative entropy

Definition 5.5
.

A function
\mathbb{D}:\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathrm{Prob}(n)\times\mathrm{Prob}(n)\to\mathbb{R}\cup\quantity{\infty}
is called a
relative entropy
if it satisfies

1.

Data processing inequality: for any
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\in\mathrm{Prob}(n)
and
E\in\mathrm{Stoch}(m,n)

\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{p}\|\mathbf{q})\geq\mathbb{D}(E\mathbf{p}\|E\mathbf{q})

2.

Additivity under tensor product: for any
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\in\mathrm{Prob}(n)
and
\mathbf{r},\mathbf{s}\in\mathrm{Prob}(m)

\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{p}\otimes\mathbf{r}\|\mathbf{q}\otimes\mathbf{s})=\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{p}\|\mathbf{q})+\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{r}\|\mathbf{s})

3.

Normalized
\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{e}_{1}\|\mathbf{u}^{(2)})=1
where
\mathbf{e}_{1}=(1,0)^{T}
and
\mathbf{u}=\frac{1}{2}(1,1)^{T}
.

Rényi relative entropies

For any
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\cup\quantity{\infty}
, an
\alpha
-Rényi relative entropy,
D_{\alpha}
, of a pair of probability vectors
\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\in\mathrm{Prob}(n)
is defined as

D_{\alpha}(\mathbf{p}\|\mathbf{q})=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log\pqty{\sum_{x\in[n]} p_x ^\alpha q_x ^{1-\alpha}}&\mathbf{p}\ll\mathbf{q}\text{ or }(\alpha\in[0,1)\text{ and }\mathbf{p}\cdot\mathbf{q}\neq 0)\\
\infty&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}.

where
\mathbf{p}\ll\mathbf{q}
denotes the condition
\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{p})\subseteq\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{q})
.

At
\alpha=0,1,\infty
, the
\alpha
-Rényi relative entropy is defined by taking limit of
\alpha
to the value.

Particularly at
\alpha=1
,
D_{1}:=\lim_{\alpha\to 1}D_{\alpha}(\mathbf{p}\|\mathbf{q})=D(\mathbf{p}\|\mathbf{q})
is a KL divergence.
Another two important Rényi relative entropies are
\alpha=0
, termed min-relative entropy,

D_{0}(\mathbf{p}\|\mathbf{q})=D_{\mathrm{min}}(\mathbf{p}\|\mathbf{q})=-\log\pqty{ \sum_{x\in\rm{supp}(\p)} q_x}

and
\alpha=\infty
, termed max-relative entropy,

D_{\infty}(\mathbf{p}\|\mathbf{q})=D_{\mathrm{max}}(\mathbf{p}\|\mathbf{q})=\log\max_{x\in[n]}\quantity{\frac{p_x}{q_x}}.

These relative entropies are derived similarly to the case of
\alpha=0,1,\infty
Rényi entropies.

The two min and max relative entropies give a sufficient condition for relative majorization.

Sufficient condition for relative majorization

Theorem 5.6
.

Suppose
\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\in\mathrm{Prob}(n)
and
\mathbf{p}^{\prime},\mathbf{q}^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Prob}(n^{\prime})
. If
D_{\mathrm{max}}(\mathbf{p}^{\prime}\|\mathbf{q}^{\prime})\leq D_{\mathrm{min}}(\mathbf{p}\|\mathbf{q})
, then
\pqty{\p,\q}\succ\pqty{\p',\q'}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
D_{\infty}(\mathbf{p}^{\prime}\|\mathbf{q}^{\prime})\leq D_{0}(\mathbf{p}\|\mathbf{q})
. Suppose both pairs are in the standard form. The inequality can be put as

\log\max_{x\in[n]}\pqty{\frac{p' _x}{q'_x}}=\log\pqty{\frac{p'_1}{q'_1}}\leq-\log\sum_{x\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{p})}q_{x}

Exponentiate both side of the inequality leads to

\frac{q^{\prime}_{1}}{p^{\prime}_{1}}\geq\sum_{x\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{p})}q_{x}

The term
\frac{q^{\prime}_{1}}{p^{\prime}_{1}}
gives a point
(a,b)=\pqty{p'_1, q'_1}
on a lower Lorenz curve. Extrapolating the line segment linearly to intersect with the line
a=1
, we have that the intersection is at
(1,\frac{q^{\prime}}{p^{\prime}})
. By convexity of the lower Lorenz curve, this extrapolation give the lowest possible Lorenz curve. On the other hand,
\sum_{x\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{p})}q_{x}
gives the extreme point of a lower Lorenz curve

(a,b)=\pqty{\sum_{x\in\rm{supp}(\p)} p_x,\sum_{x\in\rm{supp}(\p)} q_x}=\quantity(1,\sum_{x\in\rm{supp}(\p)} q_x).

Notice that this point is on the line
a=1
as well.
Intrapolating linearly between the origin and this yield a possible lower Lorenz curve for the pair
\pqty{\p,\q}
. This curve is the highest possible since the lower Lorenz curve must be convex.
All of this information is illustrated in Figure
3
. Since we have that the point
\pqty{1,\frac{q' _1}{p' _1}}
is above
\sum_{x\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{p})}q_{x}
, the lower Lorenz curve of
\pqty{\p,\q}
is under that of
\pqty{\p',\q'}
showing the relative majorization relation
\pqty{\p,\q}\succ\pqty{\p',\q'}
.
∎

\sum_{x\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{p})}q_{x}
p^{\prime}_{1}
q^{\prime}_{1}
\frac{q_{1}^{\prime}}{p_{1}^{\prime}}
1
1

Figure 3
:
Possible lower Lorenz curves for a pair
\pqty{\p,\q}
(solid and dashed
purple
line) and a pair
\pqty{\p',\q'}
(dashed
orange
). The dashed purple portion shows the lowest possible lower Lorenz curve for a given
D_{\mathrm{max}}(\mathbf{p}^{\prime}\|\mathbf{q}^{\prime})=p^{\prime}_{1}/q^{\prime}_{1}
. The dashed orange shows the highest possible lower Lorenz curve for a given
D_{\mathrm{min}}(\mathbf{p}\|\mathbf{q})=\sum_{x\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{p})}q_{x}
.

Remark
.

The converse of the above theorem is not true. There is a pair of probability vector pairs such that
\pqty{\p,\q}\succ\pqty{\p',\q'}
but
D_{\mathrm{max}}(\mathbf{p}^{\prime}\|\mathbf{q}^{\prime})>D_{\mathrm{min}}(\mathbf{p}\|\mathbf{q})
. For example,
\mathbf{p}=(0.9,0.1,0)^{T},\mathbf{q}=(0.1,0.8,0.1)^{T}
and
\mathbf{p}^{\prime}=\pqty{0.2,0.8}^{T}
,
\mathbf{q}^{\prime}=\pqty{0.1,0.9}^{T}
.

Mutual information

A mutual information between two classical systems
X
and
Y
, denoted with
I(X:Y)
, is defined as a divergence of a joint probability distribution from a product of the marginals,

I(X:Y)=D(\mathbf{p}^{XY}\|\mathbf{q}^{X}\otimes\mathbf{r}^{Y})

(50)

where
\mathbf{p}^{XY}
joint probability vector and
\mathbf{q}^{X}
and
\mathbf{r}^{Y}
are marginal distribution.

6
Conditional uncertainty of states

Suppose we have two finite-dimensional classical systems
X
and
Y
. A classical state on a bipartite system is described by
\mathbf{p}^{XY}
a joint probability vectors. We can compare two bipartite probability vector with the majorization relation. In that case, we compare uncertainty when
both
systems are inaccessible. On the other hand, the conditional majorization relation compares uncertainty relation when one specific system is accessible. In this thesis, we compare uncertainty of a system
X
when a system
Y
is accessible.

There are three perspectives to define conditional majorization: constructive, axiomatic, and operational. The conditional majorization was first introduced in
[
21
]
with the constructive appoarch, where the author describe what operations are allowed to use and define conditional uncertainty comparison to be an ability to reconstruct one joint probability distribution into another. Later on, the development in axiomatic definition of quantum conditional majorization was introduced in
[
22
]
, where the authors prescribe a properties of a general conditional uncertainty non-decreasing map and shows the coincident with the constructive approach. Moreover, they also purposed a family of games of chance that can fully characterize the majorization with operational tasks. The compilation of all three approaches in the classical regime was introduced in
[
47
]
.

From constructive and axiomatic point of views, an unconditional uncertainty is defined via a convertibility relation of mixing operations and say
\mathbf{p}^{XY}
conditionally majorizes
\mathbf{q}^{XY^{\prime}}
if there is a mixing operation transforming
\mathbf{p}^{XY}
to
\mathbf{q}^{XY^{\prime}}
. Similarly, to the probability vector majorization, this operation must be linear to preserve probabilistic interpretation.

In the following section, we will introduce the axiomatic definition of conditional uncertainty comparison. Later, we will characterize this definition via constructive and operational perspectives.

1
Axiomatic approach

Suppose
\mathbf{q}^{XY^{\prime}}=M\mathbf{p}^{XY}
. What should be the key properties of
M
so that accessing
Y^{\prime}
when the distribution is
\mathbf{q}^{XY^{\prime}}
gives less certainty regarding
X
compare to when accessing
Y
and the distribution is
\mathbf{p}^{XY}
?

Suppose
M
sends an information of
X
to
Y^{\prime}
. Then accessing
Y^{\prime}
on
M\mathbf{p}^{XY}
would be gain more information regarding
X
compare to accessing
Y
, from
\mathbf{p}^{XY}
. This gives us the first requirement of the mapping
M
must send no information from
X
to
Y
, which is called
X\not\to Y
no-signalling
.
Mathematically, this condition can be expressed as the following condition.
Suppose
M=\pqty{\mu_{x'y'\vert xy}}
.
M
is
X\to Y
no-signalling if

\sum_{x^{\prime}}\mu_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}|xy}=r_{y^{\prime}|y}.

(51)

That is an evolution of any classical joint distribution
\mathbf{p}^{XY}
by
M
is independent of
x
, making an agent on
Y,Y^{\prime}
cannot learn more about
X
by applying
M
even when they have access to
Y
and
Y^{\prime}
. Otherwise, consider a scenario where application of
M
result in two different probability distributions on
Y
depdending on what
x
is. Then, one could with a hypothesis testing strategy to test if the samples
\quantity{y_1, y_2, \ldots, y_k}
is drawn from which source.

Secondly, such
M
must preserves the most uncertain state on
X
which is a uniform distribution. That is for any joint probability distribution in the form
\mathbf{p}^{XY}=\mathbf{u}^{X}\otimes\mathbf{p}^{Y}
, we have that
M\mathbf{p}^{XY}=\mathbf{u}^{X}\otimes\mathbf{q}^{Y^{\prime}}
for some
\mathbf{q}^{Y^{\prime}}
. We call such a matrix
M
satisfying both conditions a
conditionally mixing operation
.

Conditionally mixing operation (CMO)

Definition 6.1
.

A stochastic matrix
M\in\mathrm{Stoch}(nm^{\prime},nm)
is said to be a
conditionally mixing operation
(CMO) conditioned on
Y
if it satisfies

1.

M
is
X\not\to Y
no-signalling: for any
x\in[n]
,
y\in[m]
, and
y^{\prime}\in[m^{\prime}]
,
\sum_{x^{\prime}}\mu_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}|xy}=r_{y^{\prime}|y}
.

2.

M
preserves uniform distribution on
X
.

Conditional majorization is defined to be a convertibility via this set of operations.

Conditional majorization

Definition 6.2
.

Suppose
\mathbf{p}^{XY}\in\mathrm{Prob}(nm)
,
\mathbf{q}^{XY^{\prime}}\in\mathrm{Prob}(nm^{\prime})
,
\absolutevalue{X}=n
,
\absolutevalue{Y}=m
, and
\absolutevalue{Y'}=m^{\prime}
. We say that
\mathbf{p}^{XY}

conditionally majorizes

\mathbf{q}^{XY^{\prime}}
on
X
and write
\mathbf{p}^{XY}\succ_{X}\mathbf{q}^{XY^{\prime}}
if there exists
M\in\mathrm{CMO}(nm^{\prime},nm)
such that
\mathbf{q}^{XY^{\prime}}=M\mathbf{p}^{XY}
.

The no-signaling
X\not\to Y
is equivalent with a being semicausal and semilocalizable. Here, we provide a quantum channel definition of semicausal channel.

Semicausal channel

Definition 6.3
.

A quantum channel
\mathcal{M}\in\mathrm{CPTP}(AB\to AB^{\prime})
is
A\not\to B^{\prime}

semi-casual
if

\Tr_{A}[\mathcal{M}^{AB\to AB^{\prime}}(\rho^{AB})]=\Tr_{A}[\mathcal{M}^{AB\to AB^{\prime}}(\mathcal{E}^{A\to A}\otimes\mathcal{I}^{B}(\rho^{AB}))]

(52)

for any
\mathcal{E}\in\mathrm{CPTP}(A\to A)
and
\rho\in\mathfrak{D}(AB)
. See Figure
4
for the diagram.

Figure 4
:
A diagramatic definition of semicausal channel.

In quantum domain, a channel is
A\not\to B
semicausal if and only if it is
A\not\to B
no-signalling. The same statement still holds true in the restriction to the domain of classical channel. We provide a proof for classical channels in the following theorem.

Classical
X\not\to Y
semicausal channels and
X\not\to Y
no-signalling

Theorem 6.4
.

A classical channel
\mathcal{M}^{XY\to XY^{\prime}}
is
X\not\to Y
semicausal if and only if it is
X\not\to Y
no-signalling.

Proof.

In the only-if direction,
\mathcal{M}^{XY\to XY^{\prime}}
is
X\not\to Y
semicausal.
We want to shows that

\sum_{x^{\prime}\in[m]}\mu_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}|xy}=r_{y^{\prime}|y}

where
\mu_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}|xy}
is an element of a transition matrix
M
associated with the channel
\mathcal{M}^{XY\to XY^{\prime}}
.
Suppose
\mathcal{N}^{XY\to Y^{\prime}}=\Tr_{X}\circ\mathcal{M}^{XY\to XY^{\prime}}
. Semicausal condition can be written as

\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{I})=\mathcal{N}

(53)

for any classical channel
\mathcal{E}
.
A transition matrix
N
associated with a channel
\mathcal{N}^{XY\to Y^{\prime}}
has their entries given by

\nu_{y^{\prime}|xy}=\sum_{x^{\prime}\in[n]}\mu_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}|xy}.

Suppose
\mathcal{E}
has a transition matrix
E=\pqty{\eta_{vw}}
. The semicausal condition in Equation (
53
) can be written as

\pqty{\sum_{y',x,y} \nu_{y'\vert xy} \*{e}_{y'} \*{e}_{x,y}^T}\pqty{\sum_{v,w} \eta_{v\vert w} \*{e}_{v}\*{e}_{w} ^T\otimes I}=\pqty{\sum_{y',x,y} \nu_{y'\vert xy} \*{e}_{y'} \*{e}_{x,y}^T}

where
\mathbf{e}_{x,y}=\mathbf{e}_{x}\otimes\mathbf{e}_{y}
. Consider the left side of equality

\displaystyle\pqty{\sum_{y',x,y} \nu_{y'\vert xy} \*{e}_{y'} \*{e}_{x,y}^T}\pqty{\sum_{v,w} \eta_{v\vert w} \*{e}_{v}\*{e}_{w} ^T\otimes I}

\displaystyle=\sum_{y^{\prime},x,y,w}\nu_{y^{\prime}|xy}\eta_{x|w}\mathbf{e}_{y^{\prime}}\mathbf{e}_{w,y}^{T}

\displaystyle=\sum_{y^{\prime},y,w}\pqty{\sum_{x\in[n]} \nu_{y'\vert xy} \eta_{x\vert w}}\mathbf{e}_{y^{\prime}}\mathbf{e}_{w,y}^{T}

That is for any
E
,
y
, and
y^{\prime}

\displaystyle\sum_{x}\nu_{y^{\prime}|xy}\eta_{x|w}=\nu_{y^{\prime}|wy}

Picking
\eta_{x|w}=\delta_{xx_{0}}
for all
x\in[n]
results in

\nu_{y^{\prime}|x_{0}y}=\nu_{y^{\prime}|wy},

implying that
\nu_{y^{\prime}|xy}
is a constant in
x
. Then we define
r_{y^{\prime}|y}:=\nu_{y^{\prime}|xy}
. Therefore,

\nu_{y^{\prime}|xy}=\sum_{x^{\prime}\in[n]}\mu_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}|xy}=r_{y^{\prime}|y}.

Conversely, if the channel is
X\not\to Y
no-signalling, we have that

\nu_{y^{\prime}|xy}=\sum_{x^{\prime}\in[n]}\mu_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}|xy}=r_{y^{\prime}|y}.

(54)

A composition of channels
\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{I})
is associated with a transitioin matrix

\displaystyle N(E\otimes I)=\pqty{\sum_{x,y,y'} \nu_{y'\vert xy} \*{e}_{y'}\*{e}_{x,y}^T}\pqty{\sum_{v,w\in[n]}\eta_{v\vert w} \*{e}_{v}\*{e}_{w} ^T\otimes\sum_{z\in[m]} \*{e}_{z} \*{e}_{z} ^T }.

Apply the property of
X\not\to Y
no-signalling (Equation (
54
)) leads to

\displaystyle N(E\otimes I)

\displaystyle=\pqty{\sum_{x,y,y'} r_{y'\vert y} \*{e}_{y'}\*{e}_{x,y}^T}\pqty{\sum_{v,w}\eta_{v\vert w} \*{e}_{v}\*{e}_{w} ^T \otimes I}

\displaystyle=\sum_{y^{\prime},y,w}r_{y^{\prime}|y}\pqty{\sum_{x\in[n]}\eta_{x\vert w}}\mathbf{e}_{y^{\prime}}\mathbf{e}_{w,y}^{T}

\displaystyle=\sum_{y^{\prime},y,w}r_{y^{\prime}|y}\mathbf{e}_{y^{\prime}}\mathbf{e}_{w,y}^{T}=N

showing
X\not\to Y
semicausality.
∎

Moreover,
X\not\to Y
non-signalling is realizable with a semilocalizable operation, an operation which decomposable into local operations on
X
and
Y
with one-way communication from
Y
to
X

[
62
]
. Here we present a quantum channel definition.

Figure 5
:
Diagram visualization of a semilocalizable map
\mathcal{M}
.

Semilocalizable channel

Definition 6.5
.

A quantum channel
\mathcal{M}\in\mathrm{CPTP}(AB\to AB^{\prime})
is
semilocalizable
with a communication
B\to A
if it can be decomposed into

\mathcal{M}=\pqty{\cal{F}^{AC\to A} \otimes\id^{B'}}\circ\pqty{\id^{A} \otimes\cal{S}^{B\to B' C}}

for some
\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{CPTP}(AC\to A)
and
\mathcal{S}\in\mathrm{CPTP}(B\to B^{\prime}C)
. See Figure
5
for diagram.

This definition of channel gives a specific construction of a semicausal channel and consequently, our conditionally mixing operation. In the classical case, any classical channel has their corresponding transition matrix. We define the classical systems
X,Y,
and
Z
in the place of
A,B,
and
C
respectively. We denotes transition matrix with the above channel
\mathcal{M}
to be
M
and
F
and
S
for their corresponding decomposition of the transition matrix

M=(F^{XZ\to X}\otimes I^{Y^{\prime}})\circ(I^{X}\otimes S^{Y\to YZ^{\prime}}).

Suppose that
Z
has dimension
\ell
. We denotes each distinct states of
Z
with
j\in[\ell]
, we can then write
S^{Y\to YZ}
,

S^{Y\to YZ}=\sum_{j\in[\ell]}S^{Y\to Y}_{j}\otimes\mathbf{e}^{Z}_{j}

where
S_{j}^{Y\to Y}
is a substochastic matrix and
\sum_{j\in[n]}S_{j}^{Y\to Y}
is a substochastic matrix. Similarly, we can write
F
in terms of its conditioning on
j\in[\ell]

F^{XZ\to X}=\sum_{j\in[\ell]}F_{j}^{X\to X}\otimes(\mathbf{e}_{j}^{Z})^{T},

where
F_{j}^{X\to X}
is a stochastic matrix. Puting above equations together, we can equivalently write the classical semilocalizable channel
M
with communication from
Y\to X
as

M=\sum_{j\in[\ell]}F^{X\to X}_{j}\otimes S^{Y\to Y}_{j}.

(55)

Lemma 6.6
.

A classical channel is
X\not\to Y
no-signalling if and only if it is semilocalizable with a communication from
Y\to X
.

Proof.

In the only-if direction, suppose
M=\pqty{\mu_{x'y'\vert xy}}
is a transition matirx associated with a classical
X\not\to Y
no-signalling. By being
X\not\to Y
no-signalling, we have that

\sum_{x^{\prime}\in[n]}\mu_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}|xy}=r_{y^{\prime}|y}.

Define

t^{(y,y^{\prime})}_{x^{\prime}|x}=\frac{\mu_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}|xy}}{r_{y^{\prime}|y}}.

We have that
M
can be put in the form

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,x^{\prime}\in[n]\\
y\in[m]\\
y^{\prime}\in[m^{\prime}]\end{subarray}}\mu_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}|xy}\mathbf{e}_{x^{\prime}}\mathbf{e}_{x}^{T}\otimes\mathbf{e}_{y^{\prime}}\mathbf{e}_{y}^{T}=\sum_{y,y^{\prime}}r_{y^{\prime}|y}\sum_{x,x^{\prime}}t^{(y,y^{\prime})}_{x^{\prime}|x}\mathbf{e}_{x^{\prime}}\mathbf{e}_{x}^{T}\otimes\mathbf{e}_{y^{\prime}}\mathbf{e}_{y}^{T}

(56)

\displaystyle=\sum_{y,y^{\prime}}T^{(y,y^{\prime})}\otimes r_{y^{\prime}|y}~\mathbf{e}_{y^{\prime}}\mathbf{e}_{y}^{T},

(57)

where
T^{(y,y^{\prime})}:=\sum_{x,x^{\prime}}t^{(y,y^{\prime})}_{x^{\prime}|x}\mathbf{e}_{x^{\prime}}\mathbf{e}_{x}^{T}\in\mathrm{Stoch}(m^{\prime},m)
. This shows that
M
is a semilocalizable with a communication from
Y\to X
. To put it clearly,
S^{(y,y^{\prime})}=r_{y^{\prime}|y}~\mathbf{e}_{y^{\prime}}\mathbf{e}_{y}^{T}
is the substochastic matrix.

Conversly, if
M
is semilocalizable, then it has a form as in Equation (
55
). Consider taking its elements
M=\pqty{\mu_{x'y'\vert xy} : x,x'\in[n], y\in[m], y'\in[m']}

\displaystyle\mu_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}|xy}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{e}_{x^{\prime}}^{T}\otimes\mathbf{e}_{y^{\prime}}^{T}\pqty{\sum_{j\in[\ell]} F_j \otimes S_j}\mathbf{e}_{x}\otimes\mathbf{e}_{y}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in[\ell]}(\mathbf{e}_{x^{\prime}}^{T}F_{j}\mathbf{e}_{x})(\mathbf{e}_{y^{\prime}}^{T}S_{j}\mathbf{e}_{y}).

Since
F_{j}
is stochastic, we have that the sum over
x^{\prime}
result in

\sum_{x^{\prime}\in[n]}\mu_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}|xy}=\sum_{x^{\prime}\in[n]}(\mathbf{e}_{x^{\prime}}^{T}F_{j}\mathbf{e}_{x})=(\mathbf{e}_{y^{\prime}}^{T}S_{j}\mathbf{e}_{y})=\mathbf{e}_{y^{\prime}}^{T}S_{j}\mathbf{e}_{y}

independent of
x
showing that
M
is
X\not\to Y
no-signalling.
∎

The identification of
X\not\to Y
no-signalling with semilocalizable channel characterize the set of conditional mixing operations with a specific schema of transformation on a joint probability vector. With this result we are ready to state the constructive characterization of conditional majorization.

2
Constructive characterization

Suppose there are a correlated source
\mathbf{p}^{XY}
and two agents: Alice and Bob. Alice has access to the system
X
and a receiving end of the communication from Bob, and Bob has access to the system
Y
. Alice and Bob cooperate to implement a map
M
which has to following structure. For Bob, he can read what
Y
is, apply data processing to his side and ask Alice to apply doubly-stochastic matrix on Alice side. The doubly-stochastic operation can be conditioned on Bob’s information of
Y
. However, Bob cannot receive or request Alice to send him information regarding
X
. After the application of this map, Bob end up having the same or higher level uncertainty regarding the system
X
. This is because Bob ask Alice to randomly permute outcome on
X
with a doubly-stochastic matrix, and Alice never have told or hinted Bob on the state of
X
.
These constriants give a concrete mathematical structure of the set of mixing operations, which we shall call
conditionally doubly stochastic
, as follows.

Conditionally doubly stochastic (CDS)

Definition 6.7
.

A stochastic matrix
M\in\mathrm{Stoch}(nm^{\prime},nm)
is said to be a
conditionally doubly stochastic
(CDS) conditioned on
Y
if it can be written in the form

M=\sum_{j\in[\ell]}D_{j}^{X\to X}\otimes S_{j}^{Y\to Y^{\prime}}

(58)

where
D_{j}
is a doubly stochastic matrix and
S_{j}
is a sub-stochastic matrix such that
\sum_{j\in[\ell]}S_{j}\in\mathrm{Stoch}(m^{\prime},m)
. See Figure
6
for the diagram.

Figure 6
:
A diagram of conditionally doubly stochastic map, where
S=\sum_{j\in[\ell]}S_{j}^{Y\to Y^{\prime}}\otimes\outerproduct{j}{j}
and
D=\sum_{j\in[\ell]}D_{j}^{X\to X}\otimes\outerproduct{j}{j}
.

Notice that a conditionally doubly stochastic as expressed in Equation (
58
) is indicative of being semilocalizable. We saw previously that the no-signalling condition from conditionally mixing operation is equivalent with semilocal operation. Indeed, with the condition of preserving uniform vector, the operation on
X
is restricted to be doubly stochastic.

Conditionally mixing operations and conditionally doubly stochastic

Theorem 6.8
.

A stochastic matrix
M\in\mathrm{Stoch}(nm^{\prime},nm)
is conditionally mixing operation if and only if it is conditionally doubly-stochastic.

Proof.

In the if direction, suppose
M\in\mathrm{CDS}(nm^{\prime},nm)
. We want to show that
M
satisfies both axioms of CMO. Since
\mathrm{CDS}(nm^{\prime},nm)
is a subset of semilocalizable operations, it is
X\not\to Y
no-signalling. For the second axiom,
M
preserves uniform distribution, since each
D_{j}
is doubly-stochastic.

Conversly, suppose
M
is conditionally mixing operation, we have that
M
is semilocalizable and can be expressed as in (
57
). Left to show is that
T
is doubly stochastic. By the second axiom of CMO, we have that
M
preserve uniform distribution on
X
, this makes

\displaystyle M\pqty{\u^X\otimes\e^Y _y}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{u}^{X}\otimes\mathbf{q}^{Y^{\prime}}_{y}\quad\exists\mathbf{q}_{y}\in\mathrm{Prob}(m^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{y^{\prime}\in[n^{\prime}]}q_{y^{\prime}|y}\mathbf{u}^{X}\otimes\mathbf{e}^{Y^{\prime}}_{y^{\prime}}

Equate to previous form of
M

\sum_{y^{\prime}\in[n]}r_{y^{\prime}|y}T^{(y,y^{\prime})}\mathbf{u}^{X}\otimes\mathbf{e}^{Y^{\prime}}_{y^{\prime}}=\sum_{y^{\prime}\in[n]}q_{y^{\prime}|y}\mathbf{u}^{X}\otimes\mathbf{e}^{Y^{\prime}}_{y^{\prime}}.

Taking a dot product with
\mathbf{e}^{Y^{\prime}}_{y^{\prime}}
on both sides of the equation results in

r_{y^{\prime}|y}T^{(y,y^{\prime})}\mathbf{u}^{X}=q_{y^{\prime}|y}\mathbf{u}^{X}.

(59)

Since
T
is a stochastic matrix, then taking a dot product with
\mathbf{1}
to the right on both side
r_{y^{\prime}|y}=q_{y^{\prime}|y}
. Consequently,
T^{(y,y^{\prime})}\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{u}
, equivalently
T^{(y,y^{\prime})}
is doubly stochastic for all
y
and
y^{\prime}
.
∎

Examples of conditionally mixing operations

For example, suppose we are given a certain
\mathbf{p}^{XY}=\sum_{y\in[m]}\mathbf{p}_{y}^{X}\otimes\mathbf{e}_{y}^{Y}
. Suppose

M=\sum_{j\in[m]}\Pi_{j}^{X\to X}\otimes S_{j}^{Y\to Y}\in\mathrm{Stoch}(nm,nm),

where
S_{j}=\mathbf{e}_{j}\mathbf{e}^{T}_{j}
and the matrix
\Pi_{j}
is a permutation matrix such that
\Pi_{j}\mathbf{p}_{j}=\mathbf{p}_{j}^{\downarrow}
for any
j\in[m]
. The matrix
M
leaves the state on
Y
unchanged and apply
\Pi_{y}
to rearrange
\mathbf{p}^{X}_{y}
in to non-increasing order
\mathbf{p}^{\downarrow}_{y}
. Since permutations are invertible, we have that
M
is invertible with the inverse

M^{-1}=\sum_{j\in[m]}\pqty{\Pi_j ^{-1}}^{X\to X}\otimes S_{j}^{Y\to Y}.

This example proves that with conditionally doubly stochastic, one can permute the probability distribution on
X
conditioned on
Y
in non-increasing order. That is, we have the following lemma.

Lemma 6.9
.

For any joint probability vector
\mathbf{p}^{XY}
, suppose
\mathbf{p}^{XY}=\sum_{y\in[m]}\mathbf{p}^{X}_{y}\otimes\mathbf{e}_{y}^{Y}
. The probability vector
\mathbf{p}^{XY}
is reversibly convertible to
\tilde{\mathbf{p}}^{XY}
by a conditionally doubly stochastic matrices where
\tilde{\mathbf{p}}^{XY}=\sum_{y\in[m]}(\mathbf{p}^{\downarrow}_{y})^{X}\otimes\mathbf{e}_{y}
.

The second example is a conditional mixing operation in the form, as appeared in Equation (
63
),

M=\sum_{y,y^{\prime}}D_{(y,y^{\prime})}\otimes r_{y^{\prime}|y}\mathbf{e}_{y^{\prime}}\mathbf{e}_{y}^{T},

where
D_{(y,y^{\prime})}\in\mathrm{DStoch}(n,n)
and
R=(r_{y^{\prime}|y})\in\mathrm{Stoch}(m^{\prime},m)
. Notice that
\sum_{y,y^{\prime}}r_{y^{\prime}|y}\mathbf{e}_{y^{\prime}}\mathbf{e}_{y}^{T}\in\mathrm{Stoch}(m^{\prime},m)
. Given that
y
is received on system
Y
, this map maps it to
y^{\prime}
and apply the map
D_{(y,y^{\prime})}
on system
X
with probability
r_{y^{\prime}|y}
. In general, any conditionally doubly stochastic can be put in this form.

Lemma 6.10
.

A matrix
M
is a conditionally doubly stochastic if and only if there exists a set of doubly stochastic matrices
\quantity{D_{(y,y')}: D_{(y,y')} \in\dstoch(n,n), y \in[m], y'\in[m']}
and
R=(r_{y^{\prime}|y})\in\mathrm{Stoch}(m^{\prime},m)
such that

M=\sum_{y,y^{\prime}}D_{(y,y^{\prime})}\otimes r_{y^{\prime}|y}\mathbf{e}_{y^{\prime}}\mathbf{e}_{y}^{T}

(60)

Proof.

In the if direction, since
D_{(y,y^{\prime})}
is doubly stochastic and
\sum_{y^{\prime},y}r_{y^{\prime}|y}\mathbf{e}_{y^{\prime}}\mathbf{e}_{y}^{T}\in\mathrm{Stoch}(m^{\prime},m)
, then
M
is conditionally doubly stochastic.

Conversely, suppose
M\in\mathrm{CDS}(nm^{\prime},nm)
. It is conditionally mixing operation. As in the proof Theorem
6.8
, any CMO can be put in the form as in (
63
) with
T
being a doubly stochastic matrix. Identifying
T^{(y,y^{\prime})}
with
D_{(y,y^{\prime})}
completes the proof.
∎

With these two lemmas, convertibility via conditional mixing operation is equivalently stated with a unnormalized conditioned probability vector on the system
X
:
\mathbf{p}^{X}_{y}
and
\mathbf{q}^{X}_{y^{\prime}}
. The conditioned probability vector are defined to be such that

\mathbf{p}^{XY}=\sum_{y\in[m]}\mathbf{p}^{X}_{y}\otimes\mathbf{e}^{Y}_{y},\qquad\mathbf{q}^{XY^{\prime}}=\sum_{y^{\prime}\in[m^{\prime}]}\mathbf{q}^{X}_{y^{\prime}}\otimes\mathbf{e}^{Y^{\prime}}_{y^{\prime}}.

Explicitly, the elements of the vector is given by

\mathbf{p}^{X}_{y}=\sum_{x\in[n]}p_{xy}\mathbf{e}_{x}^{X}=\pqty{I\otimes\e^T _{y}}\mathbf{p}^{XY},\qquad\mathbf{q}^{X}_{y^{\prime}}=\sum_{x\in[n]}q_{xy^{\prime}}\mathbf{e}_{x}^{X}=\pqty{I\otimes\e^T _{y'}}\mathbf{q}^{XY^{\prime}}.

(61)

The equivalent statement is in the following lemma.

Conditional mixing operation on the conditional

Lemma 6.11
.

Suppose
\mathbf{p}^{XY}\in\mathrm{Prob}(nm)
and
\mathbf{q}^{XY^{\prime}}\in\mathrm{Prob}(nm^{\prime})
. There is a conditional mixing operation
M
such that
\mathbf{p}^{XY}=M\mathbf{q}^{XY^{\prime}}
if and only if there exists a set of doubly stochastic matrices
\quantity{D_{(y,y')}: D_{(y,y')} \in\dstoch(n,n), y \in[m], y'\in[m']}
and
R=(r_{y^{\prime}|y})\in\mathrm{Stoch}(m^{\prime},m)
such that for any
y^{\prime}\in[m]

\mathbf{q}_{y^{\prime}}=\sum_{y\in[m]}r_{y^{\prime}|y}D^{(y,y^{\prime})}\mathbf{p}_{y}.

(62)

where
\mathbf{q}_{y^{\prime}}
and
\mathbf{p}_{y}
are defined as in Equation (
61
).

Proof.

First, consider the only-if direction. Suppose
\mathbf{p}^{XY}=M\mathbf{q}^{XY^{\prime}}
where
M
is a conditional mixing operation. Then, by the Lemma
6.10
,

M=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in[m]\\
y^{\prime}\in[m^{\prime}]\end{subarray}}D_{(y,y^{\prime})}\otimes r_{y^{\prime}|y}\mathbf{e}_{y^{\prime}}\mathbf{e}_{y}^{T}.

(63)

Since
\mathbf{q}_{y^{\prime}}=\pqty{I \otimes\e^T _{y'}}\mathbf{q}^{XY^{\prime}}
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbf{q}^{Y^{\prime}}_{y^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\pqty{I \otimes\e^T _{y'}}\pqty{\sum_{\substack{y\in[m]\\z\in[m']}} D_{(y,z')} \otimes r_{z\vert y} \*{e}_{z} \*{e}_{y}^T}\pqty{ \sum_{w\in[m]} \p^{X} _w \otimes\e^{Y} _{w}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{y}r_{y^{\prime}|y}D_{(y,y^{\prime})}\mathbf{p}^{X}_{y}.

Conversely, if there exists such
D
and
R
, then
\mathbf{p}^{XY}=M\mathbf{q}^{XY^{\prime}}
where
M
defined as in the Equation (
63
).
∎

The theorem above stated thtat one can turn a mixture of conditionals of
\mathbf{p}^{XY}
to the conditional of
\mathbf{q}^{XY^{\prime}}
via doubly stochastic matrices. Would this implies majorization relations between the conditional as well? As it turns out, there are majorization relations between the conditionals but only once we put the conditional of
\mathbf{p}^{XY}
in the non-increasing order.

Convex sum characterization

Theorem 6.12
.

Suppose
\mathbf{p}^{XY}\in\mathrm{Prob}(nm)
,
\mathbf{q}^{XY^{\prime}}\in\mathrm{Prob}(nm^{\prime})
,
\absolutevalue{X}=n
,
\absolutevalue{Y}=m
, and
\absolutevalue{Y'}=m
. There exists a conditional mixing operation
M
such that
\mathbf{q}^{XY^{\prime}}=M\mathbf{p}^{XY}
if and only if for any
y\in[n]
there exists a convex combination by the convex parameters
r_{y^{\prime}|y}
such that

\sum_{y\in[n]}r_{y^{\prime}|y}\mathbf{p}^{\downarrow}_{y}\succ\mathbf{q}_{y^{\prime}}

(64)

where
\mathbf{q}_{y^{\prime}}
and
\mathbf{p}_{y}
are defined as in Equation (
61
).

Proof.

Suppose that there is
M\in\mathrm{CMO}(nm^{\prime},nm)
such that
\mathbf{q}^{XY^{\prime}}=M\mathbf{p}^{XY}
. Equivalently, by the Lemma
6.11
, we have that

\sum_{y\in[m]}r_{y^{\prime}|y}D_{(y^{\prime},y)}\mathbf{p}^{X}_{y}=\mathbf{q}^{X}_{y^{\prime}}

Recall that, for each
y
and
y^{\prime}
,
\mathbf{p}^{X}_{y}\succ D_{(y^{\prime},y)}\mathbf{p}^{X}_{y}
. For a vector in non-increasing order,

\mathbf{p}^{\downarrow}_{y}\sim\mathbf{p}^{X}_{y}\succ D_{(y^{\prime},y)}\Pi_{y}\mathbf{p}^{\downarrow}_{y}

where for each
y
, the permutation matrix
\Pi_{y}
is such that
\Pi_{y}\mathbf{p}^{\downarrow}_{y}=\mathbf{p}^{X}_{y}
. Since the composition
D_{(y,y^{\prime})}\Pi_{y}
is doubly-stochastic for any
y,y^{\prime}
and the majorization relation above, we have that for all
k\in[n]

\left\|\mathbf{r}_{y^{\prime}|y}\mathbf{p}^{\downarrow}_{y}\right\|_{(k)}\geq\left\|\mathbf{r}_{y^{\prime}|y}D_{y^{\prime},y}\Pi_{y}\mathbf{p}^{\downarrow}_{y}\right\|_{(k)}.

Notice that summing over
y\in[n]
on the right side of the inequality is equal to
\left\|\mathbf{q}_{y^{\prime}}^{X}\right\|_{(k)}
. We have the following chain of inequalities for any
k\in[n]

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{q}^{X}_{y^{\prime}}\right\|_{(k)}

\displaystyle=\left\|\sum_{y\in[m]}r_{y^{\prime}|y}D_{y^{\prime},y}\Pi_{y}\mathbf{p}^{\downarrow}_{y}\right\|_{(k)}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\sum_{y\in[m]}r_{y^{\prime}|y}\pqty{D_{y',y} \Pi_y \p^\da_y}^{\downarrow}\right\|_{(k)}=\sum_{y\in[m]}r_{y^{\prime}|y}\left\|D_{y^{\prime},y}\Pi_{y}\mathbf{p}^{\downarrow}_{y}\right\|_{(k)}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\pqty{\p^\da_{y} \succ D_{(y', y)} \Pi_y \p^\da_y}\rightarrow}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{y\in[m]}r_{y^{\prime}|y}\left\|\mathbf{p}^{\downarrow}_{y}\right\|_{(k)}=\left\|\sum_{y\in[m]}r_{y^{\prime}|y}\mathbf{p}_{y}^{\downarrow}\right\|_{(k)}.

We have that
\left\|\mathbf{q}^{X}_{y^{\prime}}\right\|_{(k)}\leq\left\|\sum_{y\in[m]}r_{y^{\prime}|y}\mathbf{p}_{y}^{\downarrow}\right\|_{(k)}
for all
k\in[n]
, which is equivalent with the Equation (
64
).

Conversely, suppose that the Equation (
64
) holds. We want to show that there is a conditionally doubly stochastic
M
such that
\mathbf{q}^{XY^{\prime}}=M\mathbf{p}^{XY}
. The Equation (
64
), can be equivalently stated with some doubly stochastic matrix
D_{y^{\prime}}
as

\sum_{y\in[n]}r_{y^{\prime}|y}D_{y^{\prime}}\mathbf{p}^{\downarrow}_{y}=\mathbf{q}_{y^{\prime}}\quad\forall y^{\prime}\in[m^{\prime}].

We define
\Pi_{y}^{-1}
such that
\Pi_{y}^{-1}\mathbf{p}^{X}_{y}=\mathbf{p}^{\downarrow}_{y}
, this is leading to

\mathbf{q}_{y^{\prime}}=\sum_{y\in[n]}r_{y^{\prime}|y}D_{y^{\prime}}\mathbf{p}^{\downarrow}_{y}=\sum_{y\in[n]}r_{y^{\prime}|y}D_{y^{\prime}}\Pi_{y}^{-1}\mathbf{p}^{X}_{y}\quad\forall y^{\prime}\in[m^{\prime}],

which is equivalent to the convertibility in the Lemma
6.11
.
∎

This lemma leads to the characterization of conditional majorization with a feasibility problem.

Characterization from linear programming

Theorem 6.13
.

Let
\mathbf{p}^{XY}
and
\mathbf{q}^{XY^{\prime}}
, then
\mathbf{p}^{XY}\succ_{X}\mathbf{q}^{XY^{\prime}}
if and only if for all
S\in\mathrm{Stoch}_{\leq}(m,m^{\prime})
we have that

\sum_{y\in[n]}\max_{w\in[m^{\prime}]}\mathbf{s}_{w}^{\downarrow}\cdot\mathbf{p}_{y}^{\downarrow}\geq\sum_{w\in[n^{\prime}]}\mathbf{s}_{w}^{\downarrow}\cdot\mathbf{q}_{w}^{\downarrow}

(65)

where
\mathbf{s}_{w}
is the
w^{\mathrm{th}}
column of
S
.

Proof.

Suppose
\mathbf{p}^{XY}\succ_{X}\mathbf{q}^{XY^{\prime}}
, we want to show that it is equivalent with the Equation (
65
). The idea is to rewrite the Theorem
6.12
into a linear programming and then apply the Farkas lemma. For simplicity, we will denote in this proof
(\mathbf{p}^{X}_{y})^{\downarrow}=\mathbf{p}_{y}
and
(\mathbf{q}_{w}^{X})^{\downarrow}=\mathbf{q}_{w}

From the Theorem
6.12
, the conditional majorization relation is equivalent with the existence of a stochastic matrix
R=\pqty{r_{y'\vert y}}_{y^{\prime},y}
such that it satisfies

\sum_{y\in[m]}r_{y^{\prime}|y}L\mathbf{p}_{y}^{X}\geq L\mathbf{q}_{y^{\prime}}^{X},

(66)

where
L
is a lower triangular matrix with non-zero elements equal to 1.
Denote each row of
R
by
\mathbf{r}^{T}_{y^{\prime}}
for
y^{\prime}\in[m^{\prime}]
.
Define a concatenation of
\mathbf{r}_{y^{\prime}}
as
\mathbf{r}
,

\mathbf{r}=\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{mm^{\prime}}_{+}.

Define
P=\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}
. The condition in Equation (
66
)that
R
must satisfy can be written as

\sum_{y\in[m]}r_{y^{\prime}|y}L\mathbf{p}_{y}=LP\mathbf{r}_{y^{\prime}}\geq L\mathbf{q}_{y^{\prime}}\quad\forall y^{\prime}\in[m^{\prime}]

(67)

In addition, because
R
is stochastic,
\mathbf{r}
must satisfy

\sum_{y^{\prime}\in[m^{\prime}]}\mathbf{r}_{y^{\prime}}=\mathbf{1}^{(m)},

(68)

where
\mathbf{1}
is a vector containing only ones
1
of dimension
1
. However, if there exists a
\mathbf{r}
which sum of less than
\mathbf{1}^{(m)}
instead of equality, then there exists
\mathbf{r}^{\prime}
such that it satisfy the equality and the condition in (
67
). Notice that,

\displaystyle\sum_{y^{\prime}\in[m^{\prime}]}\mathbf{r}_{y^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{y^{\prime}\in[m]}\sum_{y\in[m]}r_{y^{\prime}|y}\mathbf{e}_{y}=\sum_{y\in[m]}\sum_{y^{\prime}\in[m]}r_{y^{\prime}|y}\mathbf{e}_{y}

If we have inequality for any
y\in[m]

\displaystyle\sum_{y^{\prime}\in[m]}r_{y^{\prime}|y}

\displaystyle\leq 1,

then we have that there is
\alpha_{y}\in(0,1]
such that

\sum_{y^{\prime}\in[m]}\frac{1}{\alpha_{y}}r_{y^{\prime}|y}=1.

(69)

The subscript
y
in
\alpha_{y}
signifies that
\alpha_{y}
need not to be identical among all
y\in[m]
. By redefining
\mathbf{r}=(r_{y^{\prime}|y})
to
\mathbf{r}^{\prime}=(\frac{r_{y^{\prime}|y}}{\alpha})
, we have a that
\mathbf{r}^{\prime}\geq\mathbf{r}
and

LP\mathbf{r}^{\prime}_{y^{\prime}}\geq LP\mathbf{r}_{y^{\prime}}\geq L\mathbf{q}_{y^{\prime}}

(70)

because
LP
is a matrix with all entries being positive.

With these notations, the existence of the stochastic matrix
R
is equivalent with non-emptiness of the feasible set

\quantity{\vctr: M\vctr\leq\b, \vctr\geq 0},

where
M
is defined as

M=\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}

(71)

and
\mathbf{b}
is defined as

\mathbf{b}=\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}^{T}.

(72)

From Farkas lemma, the feasible set is non-empty if and only if for any
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{nm^{\prime}+m}_{+}
, we have that
\mathbf{v}^{T}M\geq 0
implies
\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{b}\geq 0
. Suppose that
\mathbf{v}^{T}=\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}
, where
\mathbf{v}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{m^{\prime}}
for all
i\in[m^{\prime}]
and
\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
. The condition
\mathbf{v}^{T}M
can be written as

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}^{T}M\geq 0

\displaystyle\iff\mathbf{t}^{T}\geq\mathbf{v}_{w}^{T}LP\quad\forall w\in[m^{\prime}]

\displaystyle\iff\mathbf{t}^{T}\geq\max_{w\in[m]}\mathbf{v}_{w}^{T}LP

\displaystyle\iff t_{y}\geq\max_{w\in[m]}\mathbf{v}_{w}^{T}L\mathbf{p}_{y}\quad\forall y\in[m].

From
\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{b}\geq 0
, equivalently write

\displaystyle\sum_{w\in[m^{\prime}]}\mathbf{v}_{w}\cdot(-L\mathbf{q}_{w})+\mathbf{t}\cdot\mathbf{1}\geq 0

\displaystyle\iff\sum_{y\in[m]}t_{y}\geq\sum_{w\in[m^{\prime}]}\mathbf{v}_{w}\cdot(L\mathbf{q}_{w}).

The statement: for all
i\in[m^{\prime}]
, any
v_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
and
\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
,
t_{y}\geq\max_{w\in[m]}\mathbf{v}_{w}^{T}L\mathbf{p}_{y}
for all
y\in[m]
implies
\sum_{y\in[m]}t_{y}\geq\sum_{w\in[m^{\prime}]}\mathbf{v}_{w}\cdot(L\mathbf{q}_{w})
; holds if and only if for all
\mathbf{u}=\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{nm^{\prime}}_{+}

\sum_{y\in[m]}\max_{w\in[m^{\prime}]}\mathbf{u}^{T}_{w}L\mathbf{p}_{y}\geq\sum_{w\in[m^{\prime}]}\mathbf{u}_{w}^{T}\cdot L\mathbf{q}_{w}.

(73)

Using
\mathbf{u}^{T}_{w}L=U\mathbf{u}_{w}
and define
\mathbf{s}_{w}:=U\mathbf{u}_{w}
. Note that
\mathbf{s}_{w}^{\downarrow}=\mathbf{s}_{w}
. Therefore, the feasible stochastic matrix
R
exists if and only if for any
\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}
,

\sum_{y\in[m]}\max_{w\in[m^{\prime}]}\mathbf{s}_{w}^{\downarrow}\cdot\mathbf{p}_{y}^{\downarrow}\geq\sum_{w\in[m^{\prime}]}\mathbf{s}_{w}^{\downarrow}\cdot\mathbf{q}_{w}^{\downarrow}.

(74)

To rephrase in terms of stochastic matrix
S
, take
\overline{s}=\max_{w}\sum_{x\in[m]}\mathbf{e}_{x}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{s}_{w}
, we have that

S=\frac{1}{\overline{s}}\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}\in\mathrm{Stoch}_{\leq}(m,m^{\prime}).

Since
\frac{1}{\overline{s}}\mathbf{s}_{w}
is a scaling of
\mathbf{s}_{w}
, the inequality in (
74
) holds for all
S\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{m,m^{\prime}}
if and only if it holds for
S\in\mathrm{Stoch}_{\leq}(m,m^{\prime})
.
∎

With this lemma, a conditional majorization can be characterize with an optimization task in each dot product with the conditional. But what would be the task that naturally rised when one think about an uncertainty when given an access to a correlated random variable. We will introduce and disscuss such the task in the next section.

3
Operational characterization

A
k
-gambling game characterizes the probability vector majorization. Similarly, conditional majorization relation can be equivalently stated via the comparison of a family of gambling games.

In the case of the probability vector majorization, we have an operation meaning for one probability vector
\mathbf{p}^{X}
to be less uncertain than
\mathbf{q}^{XY^{\prime}}
if one has better chance winning with distribution
\mathbf{p}^{XY}
in any
k
-games. In conditional majorization, we consider an uncertainty of
X
when one have access to
Y
. That is a player in this family of gambling games must feature the utilization of information on
Y
to make better guesses. The simplest game one could devise to respect the conditional majorization would be to let to player access
y
in each
k
-game. That is the winning chance of player, instead of simply given by the Fan
k
-norm, one weight the Ky Fan
k
-norm with a probability of obtaining character
y
on the system
Y
, we have winning chance

\sum_{y\in[m]}p_{y}\left\|\mathbf{p}^{Y}_{|y}\right\|_{(k)}.

See Figure
7
for a diagram.

Figure 7
:
A diagram for a
k
-game in which a player is given
y
from the joint probability vector
\mathbf{p}^{XY}
.

In this family of games, each
k
game specifies the number of guesses a player can make. However, one also could devise a bigger family of gambling games which includes the scenario where a player can influence a number of guesses they are given. To incorporate this, we model a game with a transition matrix
T
. Each time a player receives the value
y
, a player choose an action out of
\ell
possibilities. The actions are labeled with
w
which gives a probability on
k
as
\mathbf{t}_{|w}=\sum_{k\in[n]}t_{k|w}\mathbf{e}_{k}
and probability of losing on the spot
1-\sum_{k\in[n]}t_{k|w}
.
That is
T
need not be a stochastic matrix.
T
can be a sub-stochastic matrix
T\in\mathrm{Stoch}_{\leq}(n,\ell)
. See Figure
8
for the diagram.

Figure 8
:
A diagram for a conditional
T
-game.

The characterization of conditional majorization with a gambling game is first presented in
[
52
]
.

Conditional
T
-gambling games

Given:
a matrix
T=(t_{k|w})_{k\in[n],w\in[\ell]}
, a joint probability distribution
\mathbf{p}^{XY}

Mechanism:

1.

draw
x
and
y
from the source with distribution
\mathbf{p}^{XY}
, give
y
to the player.

2.

player pick
w
and send
w
to the house and
k
is picked with probability
t_{k|w}
.

Goal:
gives correct value of
x
within
k
guesses.

To devise the best strategy, first consider a winning chance with
k
guesses. Similarly to the
k
-game, the best guesses is to guess the first
k
most likely outcomes conditioned on the received
y
. With
k
guesses, the likelihood of a person winning a
k
game is given by

\mathrm{Pr}_{k}(\mathbf{p}^{XY})=\sum_{y\in[m]}p_{y}\sum_{x\in[k]}p_{x|y}^{\downarrow}

where
p_{x|y}
is the probability that
X=x
given
Y=y
. If
y
is given, and the player chose
w
to be the input, the winning chance is

\sum_{k\in[n]}t_{k|w}\sum_{x\in[k]}p^{\downarrow}_{x|y}=\sum_{x\in[n]}p^{\downarrow}_{x|y}\sum_{k=x}^{n}t_{k|w}

Define
s_{xw}=\sum_{k=x}^{n}t_{k|w}.
The above equation* becomes,

\sum_{x\in[n]}p^{\downarrow}_{x|y}\sum_{k=x}^{n}t_{k|w}=\sum_{x\in[n]}s_{xw}p^{\downarrow}_{x|y}

Define
\mathbf{s}_{w}=\pqty{s_{1w},s_{2w},\ldots,s_{nw}}^{T}
and
\mathbf{p}_{y}=p_{y}\pqty{p_{1\vert y}, p_{2\vert y}, \ldots, p_{n\vert y}}^{T}
. We have that

p_{y}\sum_{x\in[n]}p^{\downarrow}_{x|y}\sum_{k=x}^{n}t_{k|w}=\mathbf{s}_{w}\cdot\mathbf{p}_{y}^{\downarrow}.

The best strategy is to pick
w
that maximize the probability of winning when
y
is received. In a game, prior to receiving
y
, a player playing a conditional
T
-gambling game has a winning probability

\mathrm{Pr}_{T}(\mathbf{p}^{XY})=\sum_{y\in[m]}\max_{w\in[\ell]}\mathbf{s}_{w}\cdot\mathbf{p}_{y}^{\downarrow}.

(75)

This chance of winning
T
-gambling games characterize the majorization relation.

Conditional majorization and the game of chance

Theorem 6.14
.

Suppose
\mathbf{p}^{XY}\in\mathrm{Prob}(nm)
and
\mathbf{q}^{XY^{\prime}}\in\mathrm{Prob}(nm^{\prime})
. The conditional majorization relation
\mathbf{p}^{XY}\succ_{X}\mathbf{q}^{XY^{\prime}}
holds if and only if for any
T-
gambling game where
T\in\mathrm{Stoch}_{\leq}(n\ell)
, we have that
\mathrm{Pr}_{T}(\mathbf{p}^{XY})\geq\mathrm{Pr}_{T}(\mathbf{q}^{XY})
.

Proof.

Suppose
\mathbf{p}^{XY}\succ\mathbf{q}^{XY^{\prime}}
. By the characterization in Theorem
6.12
, we have that

\sum_{y\in[m]}\frac{r_{y^{\prime}|y}}{q_{y^{\prime}}}p_{y}\mathbf{p}^{\downarrow}_{|y}\succ\mathbf{q}_{|y^{\prime}}\quad\forall y^{\prime}\in[m^{\prime}]

(76)

where
\mathbf{p}_{|y}
is a probability vector such that
\mathbf{p}_{y}=p_{y}\mathbf{p}_{|y}
and similarly for
\mathbf{q}_{|y^{\prime}}
. A winning chance in a
T
-gambiling game is

\mathrm{Pr}_{T}(\mathbf{p}^{XY})=\sum_{y\in[m]}p_{y}\max_{w\in[\ell]}\sum_{k\in[n]}t_{k|w}\left\|\mathbf{p}_{|y}\right\|_{(k)}.

(77)

Notice that the function
f:\mathrm{Prob}(n)\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
defined as

f(\mathbf{p}^{X}_{|y}):=\max_{w\in[\ell]}\sum_{k\in[n]}t_{k|w}\left\|\mathbf{p}_{|y}\right\|_{(k)}

is symmetric and convex. We rewrite the winning chance in the form

\mathrm{Pr}_{T}(\mathbf{p}^{XY})=\sum_{y\in[m]}p_{y}f(\mathbf{p}^{X}_{|y}).

(78)

Now, consider the winning chance with the probability vector
\mathbf{q}^{XY^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}_{T}(\mathbf{q}^{XY^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{y^{\prime}\in[m^{\prime}]}q_{y^{\prime}}f(\mathbf{q}_{|y^{\prime}}^{X})

\displaystyle=\sum_{y^{\prime}\in[m^{\prime}]}q_{y^{\prime}}f\pqty{\sum_{y\in[m']} \frac{r_{y'\vert y}}{q_{y'}} p_y \p^{\da} _{\vert y}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{y\in[m]}\sum_{y^{\prime}\in[m^{\prime}]}r_{y^{\prime}|y}p_{y}f\pqty{\p^{\da} _{\vert y}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in[m]}p_{y}f\pqty{\p_{\vert y}}=\mathrm{Pr}_{T}(\mathbf{p}^{XY}).

Conversely, suppose that we have the winning chance inequality

\forall\ell\in[n]~\forall T\in\mathrm{Stoch}_{\leq}(n,\ell)\quad\sum_{y\in[m]}\max_{w\in[\ell]}\mathbf{s}_{w}\cdot\mathbf{p}^{\downarrow}_{y}\geq\sum_{y^{\prime}\in[m^{\prime}]}\max_{w\in[\ell]}\mathbf{s}_{w}\cdot\mathbf{q}_{y^{\prime}},

where
\mathbf{s}_{w}=\pqty{s_{1w},s_{2w},\ldots,s_{nw}}^{T}
and
s_{xw}=\sum_{k=x}^{n}t_{k|w}.

For specific
\ell=m^{\prime}
, we have the inequality as well. By removing the maximization over
m^{\prime}
on the right side of the inequality, we have a lessor quantity
