\forall T\in\mathrm{Stoch}_{\leq}(n,m^{\prime})\quad\sum_{y\in[m]}\max_{w\in[m^{\prime}]}\mathbf{s}_{w}\cdot\mathbf{p}^{\downarrow}_{y}\geq\sum_{y^{\prime}\in[m^{\prime}]}\max_{w\in[m^{\prime}]}\mathbf{s}_{w}\cdot\mathbf{q}_{y^{\prime}}\geq\sum_{y^{\prime}\in[m^{\prime}]}\mathbf{s}_{y^{\prime}}\cdot\mathbf{q}_{y^{\prime}}.

(79)

We want to show the implication of above inequality that for all
R\in\mathrm{Stoch}_{\leq}^{\downarrow}(n,m^{\prime})

\sum_{y\in[m]}\max_{w\in[m^{\prime}]}\mathbf{r}_{w}\cdot\mathbf{p}_{y}^{\downarrow}\geq\sum_{w\in[m^{\prime}]}\mathbf{r}_{w}\cdot\mathbf{q}_{w}^{\downarrow}

where
\mathbf{r}_{w}
is the
w
-th column of
R
. For any
R\in\mathrm{Stoch}^{\downarrow}_{\leq}(n,m^{\prime})
, we have that there exists
T
such that
R=UT
where
U
is an upper triangular matrix whose its non-zero elements are equal to one. We have that any column
\mathbf{r}_{w}
of any
R\in\mathrm{Stoch}^{\downarrow}_{\leq}(n,m^{\prime})
can be written as

\mathbf{r}_{w}=U\mathbf{t}_{w}=\sum_{x=1}^{n}\sum_{k=x}^{n}t_{k|w}\mathbf{e}_{x}=\sum_{x=1}^{n}s_{x}\mathbf{e}_{x}=\mathbf{s}_{w}.

Since each element of
\mathbf{r}_{w}
is a sum over
t_{k|w}
and
\mathbf{r}_{w}
is in non-increasing order,
T
is a substochastic matrix. Notice that
\mathbf{s}_{w}
is defined exactly the same as in the inequality (
79
). With the above identification of
\mathbf{r}_{w}
with
\mathbf{s}_{w}
, we have the desired implication.
∎

4
Monotones of conditional majorization

Conditionally Schur-convex function

Definition 6.15
.

A function
f:\bigcup_{m,n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathrm{Prob}(nm)\to\mathbb{R}
is a
conditionally Schur-convex function
if for any
\mathbf{p}^{XY}\in\mathrm{Prob}(nm)
, any
\mathbf{q}^{XY^{\prime}}\in\mathrm{Prob}(nm^{\prime})
, and any
n,m,m^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}

\mathbf{p}^{XY}\succ_{X}\mathbf{q}^{XY^{\prime}}\implies f(\mathbf{p}^{XY})\geq f(\mathbf{q}^{XY}).

(80)

Notice that in the previous proof, we established that a function in the Equation (
78
) is convex. In showing that, we utilize only the characterization in Theorem
6.12
and the convexity of
f
. Therefore, we have the following lemma.

f
-conditionally Schur-convex

Lemma 6.16
.

Suppose a function
f:\bigcup_{n\in\mathcal{N}}\mathrm{Prob}(n)\to\mathbb{R}
is symmetric and convex. Then, a function
g:\bigcup_{m,n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathrm{Prob}(nm)\to\mathbb{R}
defined as

g(\mathbf{p}^{XY})=\sum_{y\in[m]}p_{y}f(\mathbf{p}_{|y}^{X})

(81)

is conditionally Schur-convex.

This leads to a succinct definition of the one of the important conditionally Shur-convex functions, namely the conditional entropy.

The classical conditional entropy

Definition 6.17
.

The
conditional entropy
of a classical system
X
conditioned on a classical system
Y
is defined as

\displaystyle H(X|Y)_{\mathbf{p}^{XY}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in[m]}p_{y}H(\mathbf{p}^{X}_{|y})

where
H(\mathbf{p}^{X}_{|y})
is a Shannon entropy,
\mathbf{p}^{XY}=(p_{x,y})_{x,y}
is a joint probability vector,
p_{y}
is a probability of
y\in[m]
and
\mathbf{p}^{X}_{|y}=(\mathbf{p}_{x|y})_{x}
is a probability vector associated the distribution of
x
given
y
.

With the Lemma
6.16
, the classical conditional entropy is conditionally Schur-concave. Similarly to entropies and relative entropies, the classical conditional entropy is additive. To see this, consider a product of bipartite classical probability vector

\displaystyle\mathbf{p}^{XX^{\prime}YY^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{q}^{XY}\otimes\mathbf{r}^{X^{\prime}Y^{\prime}}=\pqty{\sum_{y\in[m]} q_y \q_{\vert y} ^{X} \otimes\*{e}_y ^{Y} }\otimes\pqty{\sum_{y'\in[m']} r_{y'} \*{r}_{\vert y'} ^{X'} \otimes\*{e}_{y'} ^{Y'}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in[m]\\
y^{\prime}\in[m^{\prime}]\end{subarray}}q_{y}r_{y^{\prime}}\mathbf{q}_{|y}^{X}\otimes\mathbf{r}_{|y^{\prime}}^{X^{\prime}}\otimes\mathbf{e}_{y}^{Y}\otimes\mathbf{e}_{y^{\prime}}^{Y^{\prime}}.

The entropy is

\displaystyle H(XX^{\prime}|YY^{\prime})_{\mathbf{p}^{XX^{\prime}YY^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in[m]}p_{y}H(\mathbf{p}^{X}_{|y})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in[m]\\
y^{\prime}\in[m^{\prime}]\end{subarray}}q_{y}r_{y^{\prime}}H(\mathbf{q}_{|y}^{X}\otimes\mathbf{r}_{|y^{\prime}}^{X^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in[m]\\
y^{\prime}\in[m^{\prime}]\end{subarray}}q_{y}r_{y^{\prime}}H(\mathbf{q}_{|y}^{X})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in[m]\\
w^{\prime}\in[m^{\prime}]\end{subarray}}q_{w}r_{w^{\prime}}H(\mathbf{r}_{|w}^{X^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in[m]}q_{y}H(\mathbf{q}_{|y}^{X})+\sum_{w^{\prime}\in[m^{\prime}]}r_{w^{\prime}}H(\mathbf{r}_{|w^{\prime}}^{X^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=H(X|Y)_{\mathbf{q}^{XY}}+H(X^{\prime}|Y^{\prime})_{\mathbf{r}^{X^{\prime}Y^{\prime}}}.

where the third equality is from the additivity of Shannon entropy.

For example, if
\mathbf{p}^{XY}=\mathbf{q}^{X}\otimes\mathbf{r}^{Y}
, then for every
y\in[m]
,
\mathbf{p}^{X}_{|y}
is constant with
y
, indeed
\mathbf{p}^{X}_{|y}=\mathbf{q}^{X}
. We have

H(X|Y)_{\mathbf{p}^{XY}}=\sum_{y\in[m]}p_{y}H(\mathbf{p}^{X})=H(\mathbf{q}^{X}).

That is, the classical conditional entropy for two uncorrelated system
XY
condition on
Y
is a Shannon entropy of
X
.

Chapter 3
Uncertainty of classical channels

This chapter presents our answer to one of the main thesis questions: what is uncertainty of classical channel? Given two classical channels of the same output system,
\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}
and
\mathcal{M}^{X^{\prime}\to Y}
, we would like to define and compare the
uncertainty
inherent in each channel. Specifically, the goal is to compare the uncertainty of the two classical channels given that we have all the access and control over the input of the channels. To do this, we propose a definition of a majorization relation on the set of classical channels. We follow the approach we set in the probability vector majorization, in which we defined mixing operations axiomatically and constructively. The operational approach gives us a definition of majorization in terms of the comparison between success probabilities in carrying out channel-related tasks.

This chapter begins with a discussion of classical channels and classical superchannels. Then, we discuss the definition of classical channel majorization with three conceptually distinct approaches, coincidence of the approaches, and basic properties. The chapter concludes with the characterization and connection to conditional majorization.

1
Classical channels and superchannels

Given a classical channel
\mathcal{N}\in\mathrm{CPTP}(X\to Y)
, there is an associated transition matrix
N
. The probability vector
\mathbf{p}_{x}
represents
x
-th column of the transition matrix
N
. And similarly,
\mathbf{q}_{w}
represents the
w
-th column of the transition matrix
M
associated with a classical channel
\mathcal{M}\in\mathrm{CPTP}(X^{\prime}\to Y^{\prime})
.

1
Classical superchannels

Instead of concerning the whole class of quantum superchannels, we restrict to the superchannels that totally dephases an input channel and only output classical channels. Recall that a classical channel
\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}
is a channel that is invariant under the pre-composition and post-composition with totally dephasing channels
\mathcal{D}^{X}
and
\mathcal{D}^{Y}
. Succinctly, we denote this operation as a totally dephasing superchannel
\Delta^{(X\to Y)}[\mathcal{N}]:=\mathcal{D}^{Y}\circ\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}\circ\mathcal{D}^{X}
. Formally, we have the following definition of classical superchannel,

Classical superchannels

Definition 1.1
.

A superchannel
\Theta\in\mathrm{SUPER}((X\to Y)\to(X^{\prime}\to Y^{\prime}))
is a classical
superchannel
if it is invariant under the conjugation with totally dephasing superchannel
\Delta^{\pqty{X\to Y}}
. That is

\Theta=\Delta^{\pqty{X'\to Y'}}\circ\Theta\circ\Delta^{\pqty{X\to Y}}.

(1)

Notice that we require the superchannel to dephase the input channels, as the requirement of outputting classical channel alone would give a more general superchannel called a
maximally incoherent superchannel

[
63
]
.

A classical superchannel can be realized as classical pre-processing and post-processing channels. To denote classical systems, we use the last letters of the English alphabet
X,

Y,

Z,
and
W
.

The realization theorem for classical superchannel

Theorem 1.2
.

For any classical superchannel
\Theta\in\mathrm{SUPER}((X\to Y)\to(X^{\prime}\to Y^{\prime}))
, there exists a classical system
Z
, a classical pre-processing channel
\mathcal{F}^{X^{\prime}\to XZ}
, and a classical post-processing channel
\mathcal{E}^{YZ\to Y^{\prime}}
such that

\Theta[\mathcal{N}]=\mathcal{E}^{{YZ\to Y^{\prime}}}\circ\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}\circ\mathcal{F}^{X^{\prime}\to XZ}.

(2)

Proof.

From the realization Theorem
6.2
, we have that there exist an isometry channel
\mathcal{V}\in\mathrm{CPTP}(X^{\prime}\to XE)
and a channel
\mathcal{G}\in\mathrm{CPTP}(YE\to Y^{\prime})
,

\Theta[\mathcal{N}]=\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{N}\circ\mathcal{V}.

(3)

We would like to put an emphasis here that the realization theorem does not require the system
E
to be a classical system. The aim of this proof is to show that a classical channel is realizable with a classical system.

Using Equation (
1
), the realization of the superchannel can be equivalently written as

\Theta[\mathcal{N}]=\mathcal{D}^{Y^{\prime}}\circ\mathcal{G}^{YE\to Y^{\prime}}\circ\pqty{\cal{D}^Y\otimes\cal{I}^Z}\circ\pqty{\cal{N}^{X\to Y} \otimes\cal{I}^Z}\circ\pqty{\cal{D}^X\otimes\cal{I}^Z}\circ\mathcal{V}^{X^{\prime}\to XE}\circ\mathcal{D}^{X^{\prime}}.

(4)

The equation is expressed in a diagram in Figure
1
.

Figure 1
:
A realization of classical superchannel is invariant under composition with the dephasing channels.

Define these compositions with dephasing channels as new channels

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{X^{\prime}\to XE}

\displaystyle:=\pqty{\cal{D}^X\otimes\cal{I}^E}\circ\mathcal{V}^{X^{\prime}\to XE}\circ\mathcal{D}^{X^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{YE\to Y^{\prime}}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{D}^{Y^{\prime}}\circ\mathcal{G}^{YE\to Y^{\prime}}\circ\pqty{\cal{D}^Y\otimes\cal{I}^E}.

Since
\mathcal{P}^{X^{\prime}\to XE}
is a classical-quantum channel, its output is a product state, denotes them by

\mathcal{P}^{X^{\prime}\to XE}(\mathbf{e}_{w}^{X^{\prime}})=\sum_{i}\rho_{iw}^{X}\otimes\eta_{iw}^{E}\in\mathfrak{D}(XE)

(5)

where the sum is over
i
in some index set and
\mathbf{e}_{w}^{X^{\prime}}:=\outerproduct{w}{w}^{X^{\prime}}
. Since all systems are finite dimensional, it can be rewritten as

\mathcal{P}^{X^{\prime}\to XE}(\mathbf{e}_{w}^{X^{\prime}})=\sum_{x\in[m]}\mathbf{e}_{x}^{X}\otimes\xi_{xw}^{E}\in\mathfrak{D}(XE).

(6)

Note that the matrices
\xi_{xw}^{E}
are merely positive semi-definite matrices but not necessary density matrices.
Consider
\Theta[\mathcal{N}](\mathbf{e}_{w}^{X^{\prime}})
,

\displaystyle\Theta[\mathcal{N}](\mathbf{e}_{w}^{X^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Q}\circ\pqty{\cal{N}^{X\to Y}\otimes\cal{I}^E}\circ\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{e}_{w}^{X^{\prime}})

(7)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in[m]}\mathcal{Q}\pqty{ \cal{N}^{X\to Y}(\*{e}_x ^X)\otimes\xi_{xw}^{E}}

(8)

Now, we define
\mathcal{Q}_{xw}^{Y\to Y^{\prime}}
to be a currying of
\mathcal{Q}^{YE\to Y^{\prime}}
,

\mathcal{Q}_{xw}^{Y\to Y^{\prime}}(\rho^{Y}):=\frac{1}{\Tr[\xi^{E}_{xw}]}~\mathcal{Q}^{YE\to Y^{\prime}}(\rho^{Y}\otimes\xi_{xw}^{E}).

(9)

If the trace
\Tr[\xi^{E}_{xw}]
is zero, then define
\mathcal{Q}_{xw}^{Y\to Y^{\prime}}=\mathcal{I}_{c}
a classical identity channel.
This puts the above equation as

\Theta[\mathcal{N}](\mathbf{e}_{w}^{X^{\prime}})=\sum_{x\in[m]}\mathcal{Q}^{YE\to Y^{\prime}}(\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}(\mathbf{e}^{X}_{x})\otimes\xi_{xw}^{E})=\sum_{x\in[m]}\Tr[\xi^{E}_{xw}]\mathcal{Q}^{Y\to Y^{\prime}}_{xw}(\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}(\mathbf{e}_{x}^{X}))

(10)

Similarly, we define the pre-processing maps
\mathcal{P}_{xw}
to be

\mathcal{P}_{xw}^{X^{\prime}\to X}(\mathbf{e}_{v}^{X^{\prime}})=\delta_{vw}\Tr[\xi_{xw}]\mathbf{e}_{x}^{X}\quad\text{for any }x\in[m]\text{ and }w\in[m^{\prime}].

(11)

Notice that each pre-processing
\mathcal{P}_{xw}
is merely a completely positive map but not necessary a channel. However, the sum of
\mathcal{P}_{xw}^{X^{\prime}\to X}
is a channel, because

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in[m]\\
w\in[m^{\prime}]\end{subarray}}\mathcal{P}_{xw}^{X^{\prime}\to X}(\mathbf{e}_{v})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in[m]\\
w\in[m^{\prime}]\end{subarray}}\delta_{vw}\Tr[\xi_{xw}]\mathbf{e}_{x}^{X}

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in[m]}\Tr[\xi_{xv}]\mathbf{e}_{x}^{X}\in\mathrm{Prob}(m).

The inclusion is from
\mathcal{F}^{X^{\prime}\to XE}(\mathbf{e}_{w}^{X^{\prime}})=\sum_{x\in[m]}\mathbf{e}^{X}_{x}\otimes\xi_{xw}^{E}\in\mathfrak{D}(XE)
and
\mathbf{e}^{X}_{x}\in\mathfrak{D}(X)
.

All together, we have that the realization of
\Theta
can be written as

\Theta[\mathcal{N}]=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in[m]\\
w\in[m^{\prime}]\end{subarray}}\mathcal{Q}_{xw}\circ\mathcal{N}\circ\mathcal{P}_{xw}.

(12)

Uncurrying by defining
Z
to be a classical auxiliary space that its classical orthonormal basis contains all the pairs
(x,w)
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{X^{\prime}\to XZ}(\rho^{X^{\prime}})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in[m]\\
w\in[m^{\prime}]\end{subarray}}\Tr[\mathcal{P}_{xw}(\rho)]\mathbf{e}^{X}_{x}\otimes\mathbf{e}^{Z}_{(x,w)},

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{YZ\to Y^{\prime}}(\sigma^{YZ})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in[m]\\
w\in[m^{\prime}]\end{subarray}}\Tr_{Z}\bqty{\*{e}^{Z} _{(x,w)}\pqty{\cal{Q}^{Y\to Y'}_{xw}\otimes\cal{I}^Z (\sigma^{YZ})}}.

Now, consider the composition with the state input
\mathbf{e}^{X^{\prime}}_{x^{\prime}}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\circ\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}\otimes\mathcal{I}\circ\mathcal{F}(\mathbf{e}^{X^{\prime}}_{x^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}\circ\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}\otimes\mathcal{I}^{Z}\pqty{\sum_{x,w} \Tr\bqty{\cal{P}_{xw}(\*{e}^{X'} _{x'})} \*{e}^X _x \otimes\*{e}^Z _{(x,w)}}

(13)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}\circ\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}\otimes\mathcal{I}^{Z}\pqty{\sum_{x} \Tr[\xi_{xx'}]\*{e}^X _x \otimes\*{e}^Z _{(x,x')}}

(14)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x}\Tr[\xi_{xx^{\prime}}]\mathcal{E}\pqty{\cal{N}^{X\to Y}(\*{e}^X _x) \otimes\*{e}^Z _{(x,x')}}

(15)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\pqty{\cal{N}^{X\to Y}(\*{e}^X _x) \otimes\*{e}^Z _{(x,x')}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in[m]\\
\mathbf{v}\in[m^{\prime}]\end{subarray}}\Tr_{Z}\bqty{\*{e}^Z _{(u,v)} \pqty{\cal{Q}_{uv}\otimes\cal{I}^Z}\pqty{\cal{N}(\*{e}^X _x)\otimes\*{e}^Z _\pqty{x,x'}}}

(16)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Q}^{Y\to Y^{\prime}}_{xx^{\prime}}\pqty{\cal{N}(\*{e}^X _x)}

(17)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\circ\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}\otimes\mathcal{I}\circ\mathcal{F}(\mathbf{e}^{X^{\prime}}_{x^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{x}\Tr[\xi_{xx^{\prime}}]\mathcal{Q}^{Y\to Y^{\prime}}_{xx^{\prime}}\circ\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{e}^{X}_{x})

(18)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x,w}\mathcal{Q}^{Y\to Y^{\prime}}_{xw}\mathcal{N}\circ\mathcal{P}^{X^{\prime}\to X}_{xw}(\mathbf{e}^{X^{\prime}}_{x^{\prime}}).

(19)

The above equation holds for all
\mathbf{e}^{X^{\prime}}_{x^{\prime}}
, then the superchannel
\Theta
can be realized with the channel
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{F}
.
∎

We call the form of superchannel in (
19
) the
standard form
of a classical superchannel. The term standard this form exists and is unique for any classical superchannels.

The standard form of a classical superchannel

Corollary 1.3
.

A classical superchannel
\Theta\in\mathrm{SUPER}((X\to Y)\to(X^{\prime}\to Y^{\prime}))
can be realized as a sum of post-processing channels
\mathcal{E}_{x,w}\in\mathrm{CPTP}(Y\to Y^{\prime})
and the pre-processing maps
\mathcal{F}_{x,w}\in\mathrm{CP}(X^{\prime}\to X)
where
\mathcal{F}_{x,w}(\mathbf{e}_{v}^{X^{\prime}})=\delta_{w,v}f(x|w)\mathbf{e}_{x}^{X}
for all
x\in[m]
and
v\in[m^{\prime}]
.

That is for any classical channel
\mathcal{N}\in\mathrm{CPTP}
we have that

\Theta[\mathcal{N}]=\sum_{x,w}\mathcal{E}_{x,w}^{Y\to Y^{\prime}}\circ\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}\circ\mathcal{F}_{x,w}^{X^{\prime}\to X}

(20)

The proof of this corollary can be taken directly from the proof of classical realization channel. We present an alternative proof using classical realization theorem and the
currying
form of a classical superchannel.

Lemma 1.4

(Currying form of the superchannel)
.

A classical superchannel can be written in the currying form,

\Theta[\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}]=\sum_{z\in[k]}\mathcal{E}_{z}^{Y\to Y^{\prime}}\circ\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}\circ\mathcal{F}_{z}^{X^{\prime}\to X}

(21)

where
k=\absolutevalue{Z}
the dimension of reference system
Z
.

Proof.

First, suppose a classical superchannel is realized as follows

\Theta[\mathcal{N}]=\mathcal{E}^{YZ\to Y^{\prime}}\circ\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}\otimes\mathcal{I}^{Z}\circ\mathcal{F}^{X^{\prime}\to XZ}.

(22)

We define
f(xz|x^{\prime})
to be such that
\mathcal{F}^{X^{\prime}\to XZ}(\mathbf{e}^{X^{\prime}}_{x^{\prime}})=\sum_{x,z}f(xz|x^{\prime})\mathbf{e}^{X}_{x}\otimes\mathbf{e}^{Z}_{z}
. Now, consider an action of the channel
\Theta[\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}]
on an input
\mathbf{e}^{X^{\prime}}_{x^{\prime}}
.

\Theta[\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}](\mathbf{e}^{X^{\prime}}_{x^{\prime}})=\sum_{x,z}f(xz|x^{\prime})\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{e}^{X}_{x})\otimes\mathbf{e}^{Z}_{z})

(23)

From the above equation, we define
\mathcal{E}_{z}^{Y\to Y^{\prime}}(\mathbf{p}^{Y})=\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{p}^{Y}\otimes\mathbf{e}^{Z}_{z})
.

\displaystyle\Theta[\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}](\mathbf{e}^{X^{\prime}}_{x^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{x,z}f(xz|x^{\prime})\mathcal{E}_{z}\circ\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{e}^{X}_{x})

(24)

\displaystyle=\sum_{z}\mathcal{E}_{z}^{Y\to Y^{\prime}}\circ\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}\pqty{\sum_x f(xz\vert x') \*{e}^X_x}

(25)

\displaystyle=\sum_{z}\mathcal{E}_{z}^{Y\to Y^{\prime}}\circ\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}\circ\mathcal{F}_{z}^{X^{\prime}\to X}(\mathbf{e}^{X^{\prime}}_{x^{\prime}}).

(26)

That is, for all classical channel
\Theta
, we have that

\Theta[\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}]=\sum_{z}\mathcal{E}_{z}^{Y\to Y^{\prime}}\circ\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}\circ\mathcal{F}_{z}^{X^{\prime}\to X}.

(27)

∎

Proof of Corollary
1.3
.

From the currying form of the superchannel, we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{xx^{\prime}}^{Y\to Y}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{f(x|x^{\prime})}\sum_{z\in[k]}f(xz|x^{\prime})\mathcal{E}_{z}^{Y\to Y}

(28)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{xx^{\prime}}^{Y\to Y}\pqty{\*{e}_w}

\displaystyle:=\delta_{wx^{\prime}}f(x|x^{\prime})\mathbf{e}_{x}^{X}

(29)

\displaystyle f(x|x^{\prime})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{z\in[k]}f(xz|x^{\prime}).

(30)

If
f(x|x^{\prime})=0
, then we set
\mathcal{E}_{xx^{\prime}}^{Y\to Y}:=\mathcal{I}_{c}
, a classical identity channel. Now, we have that

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in[m]\\
x^{\prime}\in[m^{\prime}]\end{subarray}}\mathcal{E}_{xx^{\prime}}\circ\mathcal{N}\circ\mathcal{F}_{xx^{\prime}}(\mathbf{e}_{w}^{X^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in[m]}f(x|w)\mathcal{E}_{xw}\circ\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{e}^{X}_{x})

(31)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in[m]\\
z\in[k]\end{subarray}}f(xz|w)\mathcal{E}_{z}\circ\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{e}^{X}_{x})

(32)

\displaystyle=\sum_{z\in[k]}\mathcal{E}_{z}\circ\mathcal{N}\circ\mathcal{F}_{z}(\mathbf{e}^{X^{\prime}}_{w})\quad\forall w\in[m^{\prime}],

(33)

showing that
\Theta
can be written in the standard form.
∎

Uniqueness of the standard form

Lemma 1.5
.

Suppose a classical superchannel
\Theta
has the standard form
\Theta[\mathcal{N}]=\sum_{x,w}\mathcal{E}_{x,w}\circ\mathcal{N}\circ\mathcal{F}_{x,w}
. If there is another realization
\Theta[\mathcal{N}]=\sum_{x,w}\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{x,w}\circ\mathcal{N}\circ\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{x,w}
such that
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{x,w}(\mathbf{e}_{x^{\prime}})=\delta_{wx^{\prime}}f(x|x^{\prime})\mathbf{e}_{x}
. Then
\mathcal{F}=\tilde{\mathcal{F}}
and
\mathcal{E}=\tilde{\mathcal{E}}
.

Proof.

Suppose
\mathcal{N}_{xy}(\rho)=\Tr\bqty{\ketbra{x}{x}\rho}\outerproduct{y}{y}
. Consider,

\displaystyle\Theta[\mathcal{N}_{xy}](\mathbf{e}_{x^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tilde{x}{w}}\mathcal{E}_{\tilde{x}w}\circ\mathcal{N}_{xy}\mathcal{F}_{\tilde{x}w}(\mathbf{e}_{x^{\prime}})

(34)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tilde{x}}f(\tilde{x}|x^{\prime})\mathcal{E}_{\tilde{x}w}\circ\Tr[\outerproduct{x}{x}\mathbf{e}_{\tilde{x}}]\outerproduct{y}{y}

(35)

\displaystyle=f(x|x^{\prime})\mathcal{E}_{xx^{\prime}}(\mathbf{e}_{y}).

(36)

Similarly, via another realization, we have

\Theta[\mathcal{N}_{xy}](\mathbf{e}_{x^{\prime}})=\tilde{f}(x|x^{\prime})\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{xx^{\prime}}(\mathbf{e}_{y}).

(37)

Both realizations are of the same superchannel on the same channel, implying that

\displaystyle f(x|x^{\prime})\mathcal{E}_{xx^{\prime}}(\mathbf{e}_{y})

\displaystyle=\tilde{f}(x|x^{\prime})\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{xx^{\prime}}(\mathbf{e}_{y})

(38)

Since the post-processing
\mathcal{E}_{xx^{\prime}}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{xx^{\prime}}
are channels, then taking trace on both sides will yield
f(x|x^{\prime})=\tilde{f}(x|x^{\prime})
asserting
\mathcal{F}=\tilde{\mathcal{F}}
and directly from the above equation, we have
\mathcal{\mathcal{E}}=\tilde{\mathcal{E}}
.
∎

2
Classical channel majorization

With the classical realization theorem and the standard form of classical superchannel, we are ready to define majorization relation between classical channels. From now on, when refering to the classical channels, a channel
\mathcal{N}
is a classical channel from system
X
to
Y
, and a classical channel
\mathcal{M}
has input system
X^{\prime}
and output system
Y
. Their dimensions are denoted as follows

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}\quad\absolutevalue{X}=m\quad\absolutevalue{Y}=n

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{X^{\prime}\to Y}\quad\absolutevalue{X'}=m^{\prime}\quad.

Notice that we require the output system to be the same. However, we later will show that we can compare channels with different output dimensions.

1
Constructive approach

Figure 2
:
Random-permutation superchannel. The pre-processing channel
S
maps an input on system
X^{\prime}
to
XZ
. The system
Z
contains an instruction of which permutation
D_{z}
to apply. This figure previously appeared in
[
1
]
.

In the constructive approach, we will give a concrete description of the operations allows on the channel. As the goal is to conceptualize uncertainty of the outcome when given a control over input, the post-processing channel should not decrease the uncertainty of the outcome probability distribution. Such uncertainty non-decreasing operation is doubly-stochastic channel. Therefore, the only allowed operations are applications of doubly stochastic maps to the output of the channel
\mathcal{N}
and conditioning which doubly stochastic maps to use based on the pre-processing of the input. We call such superchannel a random-permutation superchannel.

Random-permutation superchannel

Definition 2.1
.

A random-permutation superchannel
\Theta\in\mathrm{SUPER}((X\to Y)\to(X^{\prime}\to Y))
is a superchannel that can be realized with an auxiliary system
Z
of dimension
k
as

\Theta[\mathcal{N}]=\sum_{z\in[k]}\mathcal{D}_{z}^{Y\to Y}\circ\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}\circ\mathcal{F}^{X^{\prime}\to X}_{z}

(39)

where
\mathcal{D}_{z}
is a doubly-stochastic channel and
\mathcal{F}_{z}
is a completely positive trace non-increasing map on
X^{\prime}\to X
. See figure
2
for the diagram.

For example, a pre-composition by a classical identity channel,
\mathcal{I}_{C}
, and a post-composition by a classical doubly-stochastic channel,
\mathcal{D}
,
\Theta[\mathcal{N}]=\mathcal{D}^{Y\to Y^{\prime}}\circ\mathcal{N}\circ\mathcal{I}_{C}^{X^{\prime}\to X}
is a random permutation superchannel. This highlights the fact that, the superchannel need not permute the output of
\mathcal{N}
randomly. A random-permutation superchannel could permute the output deterministically, as this superchannel
\Theta
does.

A random-permutation superchannel might not be exclusively realizable with the doubly-stochastic post-processing. For example, consider a classical superchannel
\Theta\in\mathrm{SUPER}((X\to Y)\to(X^{\prime}\to Y))
which realizable as

\Theta[\mathcal{N}]=\sum_{z\in[4]}\mathcal{E}_{z}\circ\mathcal{N}\circ\mathcal{F}_{z},

(40)

where
\mathcal{E}_{z}
and
\mathcal{F}_{z}
are defined as follows. For each
\mathcal{E}_{z}
, it has a corresponding transition matrix
E_{z}
and for each
\mathcal{F}_{z}
has a corresponding matrix
F_{z}
.
E_{1}=\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix},E_{2}=\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix},E_{3}=\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix},
and
E_{4}=\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}
. For
\mathcal{F}
, we define
F_{1}=\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix},F_{2}=\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix},F_{3}=\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}
and
F_{4}=\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}
. However, this channel is realizable with doubly-stochastic post-processing.

\Theta[\mathcal{N}]=\sum_{z\in[4]}\mathcal{D}_{z}\circ\mathcal{N}\circ\mathcal{S}_{z}

(41)

where
D_{1}=\frac{1}{2}\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}
,
D_{2}=\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix},D_{3}=D_{4}=I
,
S_{1}=\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}
,
S_{2}=\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}
, and
S_{3}=S_{4}=0
. To see that this realization give the same superchannel as the earlier realization, we compare transition matrices of the output channel denoted by
M
and
\tilde{M}
when the input channel is
\mathcal{N}
associated with the transition matrix
N
.
In the first realization, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{z\in[4]}\mathcal{E}_{z}\circ\mathcal{N}\circ\mathcal{F}_{z}\leftrightarrow M

\displaystyle=\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}N\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}+\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}N\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}+\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}N\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}

(42)

\displaystyle=\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}+\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}

\displaystyle\qquad+\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}

(43)

\displaystyle=\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}+\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}=\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}.

(44)

In the second realization,

\displaystyle\sum_{z\in[4]}\mathcal{D}_{z}\circ\mathcal{N}\mathcal{S}\leftrightarrow\tilde{M}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}N\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}+\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}N\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}

(45)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}+N\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}

(46)

\displaystyle=\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}.

(47)

We have
M=\tilde{M}
for any classical channel
\mathcal{N}
. This means both realizations maps a classical channel to a same classical channel, showing that both realizations are of the same superchannel.

We see that a random-permutation superchannel may not reveal its true nature in some realizations. A question is then if there is a streamline process to check if the superchannel is random-permutation or not. The answer to this question is yes. Looking back to the later realization of the random-permutation superchannel, it is exactly in the standard form when set
(x,w)=(\lfloor z/2\rfloor,z~\mathsf{mod}~2)
. Indeed, we have the following theorem.

The standard form of random-permutation superchannel

Theorem 2.2
.

A superchannel
\Theta\in\mathrm{SUPER}((X\to Y)\to(X^{\prime}\to Y))
is a random-permutation superchannel if and only if in the standard form it has only doubly-stochastic post-processing.

Proof.

If the standard form has only doubly-stochastic post-processing channels, then it is trivially random-permutation.

To prove this theorem in the only-if direction, suppose the superchannel is random-permutation. Then, we have that its post-processing
\mathcal{D}_{z}
are all doubly-stochastic. As the post-processing channel of
\mathcal{E}_{xx^{\prime}}
is defined as in the Equation (
28
) with a convex combination of
\mathcal{D}_{z}
, we have that
\mathcal{E}_{xx^{\prime}}
are doubly-stochastic as well. Using the uniqueness lemma of the standard form (Lemma
1.5
), this proves the theorem.
∎

With the random-permutation superchannel, we define the majorization of superchannel as follows

Constructive definition of channel majorization

Definition 2.3
.

Suppose
\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}
and
\mathcal{M}^{X^{\prime}\to Y}
are classical channels. We say
\mathcal{N}
majorize
\mathcal{M}
constructively if there exists a random-permutation superchannel such that
\mathcal{M}=\Theta[\mathcal{N}]
.

Consider two classical channels
\mathcal{N}
and
\mathcal{M}
where any output of
\mathcal{M}
can be permuted to simulate an output of
\mathcal{N}
. The act of permutation is itself a classical superchannel and indeed a random permutation one. This leads to the equivalence between the channel
\mathcal{N}
and
\mathcal{M}
since the permutation operation is invertible. In particular, a classical channel
\mathcal{N}
is equivalent under constructive majorization to
\mathcal{N}^{\downarrow}
where all of its output vector is permuted to be in a non-increasing order.

If any channel
\mathcal{N}
that has its output vectors in non-increasing order, i.e.
\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{N}^{\downarrow}
. The constructive majorization of channels can be characterized as follows. Suppose
\mathcal{N}
constructively majorizes
\mathcal{M}
. Given any output of
\mathcal{M}
, says
\mathbf{q}
one can always find the output of
\mathcal{N}
, denoted with
\mathbf{p}
, such that
\mathbf{p}\succ\mathbf{q}
. Formally, we have the following equivalence.

Channel majorization and majorization of channel outputs

Theorem 2.4
.

For any classical channels
\mathcal{N}
and
\mathcal{M}
, we have that
\mathcal{N}
constructively majorizes
\mathcal{M}
if and only if there exists a convex combination of
\mathbf{p}_{x}^{\downarrow}
’s such that

\sum_{x\in[m]}s_{x|w}\mathbf{p}_{x}^{\downarrow}\succ\mathbf{q}_{w}

(48)

where
s_{x|w}\geq 0
,
\sum_{x\in[m]}s_{x|w}=1
,
\mathbf{p}_{x}:=\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{e}_{x})
and
\mathbf{q}_{w}:=\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{e}_{w})
.

Remark
.

Columns of the transition matrix
N
are
\quantity{\p_x: x\in[m]}
, and of
M
are
\quantity{\q_w: w\in[m']}
.

Proof.

Suppose,
\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}
constructively majorize
\mathcal{M}^{X^{\prime}\to Y}
and
\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{N}^{\downarrow}
so that
\mathbf{p}_{x}=\mathbf{p}_{x}^{\downarrow}
. The superchannel
\Theta
can be put in the standard form

\Theta[\mathcal{N}]=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in[m]\\
x^{\prime}\in[m^{\prime}]\end{subarray}}\mathcal{E}_{xx^{\prime}}\circ\mathcal{N}\circ\mathcal{F}_{xx^{\prime}}.

(49)

From the definition of
\mathcal{E}_{xx^{\prime}}
in Equation (
28
), the superchannel
\Theta
being random-permutation superchannel implies that
\mathcal{E}_{xx^{\prime}}
is a doubly-stochastic channel. Consider the definition of the vectors
\mathbf{q}_{w}
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{q}_{w}=\Theta[\mathcal{N}](\mathbf{e}_{w})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in[m]\\
x^{\prime}\in[m^{\prime}]\end{subarray}}\mathcal{E}_{xx^{\prime}}\circ\mathcal{N}\circ\mathcal{F}_{xx^{\prime}}(\mathbf{e}_{w}^{X^{\prime}})

(50)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in[m]}f(x|w)\mathcal{D}_{xw}\circ\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{e}_{x})=\sum_{x}f(x|w)\mathcal{D}_{xw}(\mathbf{p}_{x}).

(51)

Since
\mathbf{p}\succ D\mathbf{p}
for any
\mathbf{p}\in\mathrm{Prob}(n)
and
\mathbf{p}_{x}=\mathbf{p}_{x}^{\downarrow}
, we have that for any
k\in[n]

\left\|\sum_{x\in[m]}f(x|w)\mathbf{p}_{x}\right\|_{(k)}\geq\sum_{x\in[m]}f(x|w)\left\|D_{xw}\mathbf{p}_{x}\right\|_{(k)}\geq\left\|\sum_{x\in[m]}f(x|w)\mathcal{D}_{xw}\mathbf{p}_{x}\right\|_{(k)}=\left\|\mathbf{q}_{w}\right\|_{(k)},

showing that
\sum_{x\in[m]}f(x|w)\mathbf{p}_{x}\succ\mathbf{q}_{w}
.

On the converse direction, suppose that there exists a doubly stochastic matrix
S=\pqty{s_{x\vert w}}
such that for all
w\in[m^{\prime}]
we have
\sum_{x\in[m]}s(x|w)\mathbf{p}_{x}\succ\mathbf{q}_{w}
. This is equivalent to the existence of a classical doubly-stochastic channel
\mathcal{D}_{w}
such that

\mathcal{D}_{w}\pqty{\sum_{x\in[m]} s(x\vert w) \*{p}_x }=\mathbf{q}_{w}

(52)

We define

\displaystyle S(\mathbf{e}_{w}^{X^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{x\in[m]}s(x|w)\mathbf{e}_{x}^{X}\otimes\mathbf{e}_{w}^{Z},

(53)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{p}^{Y}\otimes\mathbf{e}_{w}^{Z})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathcal{D}_{w}(\mathbf{p}^{Y})

(54)

\displaystyle\Theta[\mathcal{N}]

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{N}\circ\mathcal{S}.

(55)

The classical superchannel
\Theta
is a random-permutation superchannel since
\mathcal{D}_{z}
is doubly stochastic. From the above equation we have,

\Theta[\mathcal{N}](\mathbf{e}_{w}^{X^{\prime}})=\mathcal{D}_{w}\pqty{\sum_{x\in[m]} s(x\vert w) \cal{N}(\*{e}_x)}=\mathbf{q}_{w}.

(56)

This holds for all
w\in[m^{\prime}]
which implies that
\Theta[\mathcal{N}]=\mathcal{M}
.
∎

Remark
.

It is important to note here that removing the non-increasing-order condition in Equation (
48
) will not yield a valid statement. This invalidity illustrates that, even if
\mathcal{N}\succ\mathcal{M}
, not every output of the majorized channel
\mathcal{M}
can be majorized by the output of the majorizing channel
\mathcal{N}
, in contrast to what one might expect.

Example 2.5
.

This is an example of the above remark. Consider
N=\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}
and
M=\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}
, where
\mathbf{p}_{1}=\frac{1}{100}\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}^{T}
,
\mathbf{p}_{2}=\frac{1}{100}\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}^{T}
and
\mathbf{q}=\frac{1}{100}\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}^{T}
. There is no convex combination of
\mathbf{p}_{1}
and
\mathbf{p}_{2}
such that it majorizes
\mathbf{q}
. However, there is a convex combination of
\mathbf{p}_{1}^{\downarrow}
and
\mathbf{p}_{2}^{\downarrow}
such that it majorizes
\mathbf{q}
. The convex combination is exactly
\mathbf{p}=\frac{1}{2}(\mathbf{p}_{1}^{\downarrow}+\mathbf{p}_{2}^{\downarrow})
.

We will now show that there is no convex combination of
\mathbf{p}_{1}
and
\mathbf{p}_{2}
that majorizes
\mathbf{q}
. The convex combination can be parametrized by
s\in[0,1]
as

\mathbf{p}:=(1-s)\mathbf{p}_{1}+s\mathbf{p}_{2}=\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}=\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}

For the first Ky Fan norm,
\left\|\mathbf{q}\right\|_{(1)}=0.6
. For
\mathbf{p}
to majorize
\mathbf{q}
, the element
0.75-0.65s
need to be its biggest element with
s\in[0,1/65]
. The other elements are at most
0.5
when
s=1
. The infeasibility is in the second Ky Fan norm,
\left\|\mathbf{q}\right\|_{(2)}=0.9
. Notice that
0.15+0.35s\geq 0.15+0.30s
. It follows that
\left\|\mathbf{p}\right\|_{(2)}=0.75-0.65s+0.15+0.30s=0.85-0.35s
, which is lesser than
0.9
for any non-negative value
s
.

2
Axiomatic approach

Figure 3
:
Completely uniformity preserving superchannel. In this figure, the channel
\mathcal{N}
is a conditionally uniform channel. The channel outputs a product state where the state on
Y
is maximally mixed, denoted by a squiggly line. The channel
\Theta[\mathcal{N}]
is conditionally uniform as the output state on
Y^{\prime}
is maximally mixed. Previously appeared in
[
1
]
.

In the axiomatic approach, the maximally randomizing channel
\mathcal{R}
is the nosiest channel and the mixing operation must preserve the nosiest channel. However, there is a classical superchannel which preserve
\mathcal{R}^{X\to Y}
but does not preserve a
classical conditionally uniform
channel
\mathcal{N}^{XZ_{0}\to YZ_{1}}
, which is defined as follows.

Conditionally uniform channels

Definition 2.6
.

A classical channel
\mathcal{N}^{XZ_{0}\to YZ_{1}}
is said to be a conditionally uniform classical channel if for all
\mathbf{p}\in\mathrm{Prob}(nk)
where
k:=\absolutevalue{Z_0}
we have that

\mathcal{N}^{XZ_{0}\to YZ_{1}}(\mathbf{p})=\mathbf{u}^{Y}\otimes\mathbf{q}^{Z_{1}}

(57)

See Figure
3
for a diagram.

If a classical superchannel fails to preserve this class of channels, then the output of the channel
\Theta[\mathcal{N}]
can have less uncertainty than
\mathcal{N}
, by creating correlation between system
Y
and
Z_{1}
. To illustrate, suppose
\mathcal{M}^{XZ_{0}\to Y^{\prime}Z_{1}}=\Theta[\mathcal{N}]
, if
\mathcal{N}
is conditionally uniform channel but
\mathcal{M}
is not, then the outputs of the channel
\mathcal{M}
,
Y^{\prime}
and
Z_{1}
, are not independent and hence can have lesser uncertainty than that of
\mathcal{N}
.

This class of superchannel is a strict subset of the class of uniformity preserving superchannel. There exists a superchannel which is uniformity preserving but does not completely preserve uniformity. Presenting in the standard form of superchannel, an example of such superchannel is as follows. Consider a superchannel
\Theta\in\mathrm{SUPER}((X\to Y)\to(X^{\prime}\to Y^{\prime}))
where
\absolutevalue{X'}=1
and
\absolutevalue{X}=\absolutevalue{Y}=\absolutevalue{Y'}=2
.
Preprocessing channels
\mathcal{F}_{x,w}
and processing channel
\mathcal{E}_{x,w}
have corresponding transition matrices

\displaystyle E_{1,1}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}1&1\\
0&0\end{bmatrix},

\displaystyle E_{2,1}=\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
1&1\end{bmatrix},

(58)

\displaystyle F_{1,1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix},

\displaystyle F_{2,1}=\frac{1}{2}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}.

(59)

The indices
x
and
w
belong to
x\in\{1,2\}
and
w\in\{1\}
. This superchannel is uniformity preserving. Suppose
\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}
is a uniform channel and its corresponding transition matrix is
N
. We have

\displaystyle\Theta[\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in[2]}E_{x,1}NF_{x,1}

(60)

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}1&1\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}\times\frac{1}{2}\begin{bmatrix}1&1\\
1&1\end{bmatrix}\times\frac{1}{2}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}+\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
1&1\end{bmatrix}\times\frac{1}{2}\begin{bmatrix}1&1\\
1&1\end{bmatrix}\times\frac{1}{2}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}

(61)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}.

(62)

This shows that
\Theta
is uniformity preserving. Next, we will show that the superchannel is not completely uniformity preserving. Suppose
\mathcal{N}^{XZ\to YW}
is a conditionally uniform channel with a corresponding transition matrix
N

N=\mathbf{u}^{Y}\otimes I^{XZ\to W}=\frac{1}{2}\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\\
1&0\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}.

Denote a transition matrix corresponding to a marginal channel
\mathcal{N}^{XZ\to W}
with
\overline{N}
.
The action of the superchannel on this channel is
\Theta\otimes\mathsf{id}^{(Z\to W)}[\mathcal{N}^{XZ\to YW}]
. Suppose its corresponding transition matrix is
M

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle=\sum_{x,w}\mathcal{E}_{x,w}[\mathbf{u}^{Y}]\otimes\mathcal{N}^{XZ\to W^{\prime}}\circ(\mathcal{F}_{x,w}^{X^{\prime}\to X}\otimes\mathcal{I}^{Z\to Z})

(63)

\displaystyle=E_{1,1}\mathbf{u}^{Y}\otimes\overline{N}(F_{1,1}\otimes I^{Z})+E_{2,1}\mathbf{u}^{Y}\otimes\overline{N}(F_{2,1}\otimes I^{Z})

(64)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{e}_{1}\otimes\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\left(\frac{1}{2}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}\otimes 1\right)+\mathbf{e}_{2}\otimes\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\left(\frac{1}{2}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}\otimes 1\right)

(65)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{e}_{1}\otimes\frac{1}{2}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}+\mathbf{e}_{2}\otimes\frac{1}{2}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}=\frac{1}{2}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\\
0\\
0\end{bmatrix}+\frac{1}{2}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
0\\
0\\
1\end{bmatrix}=\frac{1}{2}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\\
0\\
1\end{bmatrix}.

(66)

This is not a conditionally uniform channel, since the conditionally uniform channel is in the form
\mathbf{u}^{(2)}\otimes(p_{1},p_{2})^{T}=\frac{1}{2}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{1},p_{2})^{T}
.

To have a well-defined ordering and an uncertainty comparison relation, we require that
\Theta
preserve conditionally uniform classical channels as well. We call this kind of superchannel
completely uniformity-preserving
classical superchannel. This leads to the following definition of majorization.

Axiomatic definition of channel majorization

Definition 2.7
.

Given two classical channels
\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}
and
\mathcal{M}^{X^{\prime}\to Y}
, we have that
\mathcal{N}
axiomatically majorize
\mathcal{M}
if there exists a classical superchannel
\Theta^{(X\to Y)\to(X^{\prime}\to Y)}
such that
\mathcal{M}=\Theta[\mathcal{N}]
and for any conditionally uniform classical channel
\mathcal{E}
, we have that
\Theta[\mathcal{E}]
is also a conditionally uniform classical channel.

With requirement of uniformity preserving in complete sense, the two approaches of majorization coincide.

Equivalence of constructive and axiomatic approaches

Theorem 2.8
.

A classical superchannel is completely uniformity-preserving if and only if it is a random-permutation superchannel.

Proof.

First, a random-permutation superchannel is completely uniformity-preserving. Suppose
\Theta
is random-permutation superchannel, then we can realize
\Theta
as

\Theta[\mathcal{N}]=\sum_{z\in[k]}\mathcal{D}^{Y\to Y}\circ\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}\circ\mathcal{S}^{X^{\prime}\to XZ}.

(67)

Suppose
\mathcal{N}^{XZ_{0}\to YZ_{1}}
is a conditionally uniform classical, the output channel from the superchannel
\Theta
is given by

\displaystyle\Theta^{(X\to Y)\to(X^{\prime}\to Y)}\otimes{1}^{(Z_{0}\to Z_{1})}[\mathcal{N}^{XZ_{0}\to YZ_{1}}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{z\in[k]}(\mathcal{D}^{Y\to Y}\otimes I^{Z_{1}})\circ\mathcal{N}^{XZ_{0}\to YZ_{1}}\circ(\mathcal{S}^{X^{\prime}\to XZ}\otimes{I}^{Z_{0}})

(68)

where
I^{Z_{0}}
and
I^{Z_{1}}
denote classical identity channels on
Z_{0}
and
Z_{1}
respectively.

We will shortly denote the left side of the equation by
\mathcal{M}
, to see that this is a conditionally uniform, consider any classical input
\mathbf{e}_{x^{\prime}}^{X^{\prime}}\otimes\mathbf{e}_{w}^{Z_{0}}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{e}_{x^{\prime}}^{X^{\prime}}\otimes\mathbf{e}_{w}^{Z_{0}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{z\in[k]}(\mathcal{D}^{Y\to Y}\otimes I^{Z_{1}})\circ\mathcal{N}^{XZ_{0}\to YZ_{1}}\circ(\mathcal{S}^{X^{\prime}\to XZ}\mathbf{e}_{x^{\prime}}^{X^{\prime}}\otimes{I}^{Z_{0}}\mathbf{e}_{w}^{Z_{0}})

(69)

\displaystyle=\sum_{z\in[k]}(\mathcal{D}^{Y\to Y}\otimes I^{Z_{1}})\circ\mathcal{N}^{XZ_{0}\to YZ_{1}}(\mathbf{s}_{x^{\prime}}\otimes\mathbf{e}_{w})

(70)

\displaystyle=\sum_{z\in[k]}\mathcal{D}^{Y\to Y}(\mathbf{u}^{Y})\otimes I^{Z_{1}}(\mathbf{q}^{Z_{1}})=\mathbf{u}^{Y}\otimes\mathbf{q}^{Z_{1}}.

(71)

This is for any
x^{\prime}\in[m^{\prime}]
and
w\in[k_{1}]
. Therefore,
\mathcal{M}
is conditionally uniform classical channel.

In the converse direction, a completely uniformity-preserving is a random-permutation superchannel. Suppose that
\Theta
is completely uniformity-preserving classical superchannel, we consider its action on the channel

\mathcal{N}^{X\to YZ_{1}}=\mathbf{u}^{Y}\otimes\mathcal{D}^{Z}\circ\mathcal{V}^{X\to Z_{1}}\circ\mathcal{D}^{X}

(72)

where
\mathcal{V}^{X\to Z_{1}}
is an isometry channel and
\mathcal{D}^{Z}
and
\mathcal{D}^{X}
are totally dephasing channels. This is equivalent to

\mathcal{N}^{X\to YZ_{1}}(\mathbf{e}_{x}^{X})=\mathbf{u}^{Y}\otimes\mathbf{e}_{x}^{Z_{1}}\quad\forall x\in[m].

(73)

Now, consider
\mathcal{M}^{X^{\prime}\to YZ_{1}}\pqty{\*{e}_x ^X}
where
\mathcal{M}^{X^{\prime}\to YZ_{1}}=\Theta^{(X\to Y)\to(X^{\prime}\to Y)}[\mathcal{N}^{X\to YZ_{1}}]
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{X^{\prime}\to YZ_{1}}\pqty{\*{e}_{x'} ^{X'}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}^{YZ\to Y}\circ\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}\circ\mathcal{F}^{X^{\prime}\to XZ}(\mathbf{e}_{x^{\prime}}^{X^{\prime}})

(74)

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}^{Y}\otimes\mathbf{q}^{Z_{1}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in[m]\\
z\in[k]\end{subarray}}f(xz|x^{\prime})\mathcal{E}^{YZ\to Y}\pqty{\cal{N}^{X\to Y}(\*{e}^X_x)\otimes\*{e}^Z _z}

(75)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in[m]\\
z\in[k]\end{subarray}}f(xz|x^{\prime})\mathcal{E}^{YZ\to Y}\pqty{\*{u}^Y\otimes\*{e}^{Z_1}_x \otimes\*{e}^Z _z}

(76)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in[m]\\
z\in[k]\end{subarray}}f(xz|x^{\prime})\mathcal{E}^{YZ\to Y}\pqty{\*{u}^Y \otimes\*{e}^Z _z}\otimes\mathbf{e}^{Z_{1}}_{x}

(77)

\displaystyle\pqty{\*{q}^{Z_1} \cdot\*{e}^{Z_1}_x }\mathbf{u}^{Y}

\displaystyle=\sum_{z\in[k]}f(xz|x^{\prime})\mathcal{E}^{YZ\to Y}\pqty{\*{u}^Y \otimes\*{e}^Z _z}

(78)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbf{q}^{Z_{1}}\cdot\mathbf{e}^{Z_{1}}_{x}}{f(x|x^{\prime})}\mathbf{u}^{Y}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{f(x|x^{\prime})}\sum_{z\in[k]}f(xz|x^{\prime})\mathcal{E}^{YZ\to Y}\pqty{\*{u}^Y \otimes\*{e}^Z _z}=\mathcal{E}^{Y\to Y}_{xx^{\prime}}\pqty{\*{u}^Y}

(79)

Since
\mathcal{E}_{xx^{\prime}}
is a channel, we have that
\mathbf{q}^{Z_{1}}\cdot\mathbf{e}^{Z_{1}}_{x}/f(x|x^{\prime})=1
. This shows that post-processing channels in the standard form are doubly-stochastic channels. This superchannel is a random-permutation superchannel.
That is a superchannel can be realized as

\Theta\bqty{\cal{N}^{X\to Y}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in[m]\\
w\in[m^{\prime}]\end{subarray}}\mathcal{E}^{Y\to Y}_{x,w}\circ\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}\circ\mathcal{F}^{X^{\prime}\to X}_{x,w},

(80)

where
\mathcal{E}^{Y\to Y}
is doubly-stochastic.
∎

A sufficient condition

Instead of proving property of uniformity preserving of all possible conditionally uniform channels, it is sufficient to show the property by showing that on a particular channel the superchannel preserve its marginal uniformity.

Classical completely uniformity-preserving checking channel

Theorem 2.9
.

\Theta
is completely uniformity preserving if and only if
\Theta[\mathcal{N}^{X\to YZ}]
is conditionally uniform channel where
\mathcal{N}^{X\to YZ}(\mathbf{e}^{X}_{x})=\mathbf{u}^{Y}\otimes\mathbf{e}^{Z_{1}}_{x}
.

Proof.

The only-if direction is trivial. Suppose that
\Theta[\mathcal{N}^{X\to YZ}]
is a conditionally uniform channels.
First consider an input
\mathbf{e}_{x^{\prime}}^{X^{\prime}}
of the channel
\Theta[\mathcal{N}]
,

\displaystyle\Theta[\mathcal{N}](\mathbf{e}^{X^{\prime}}_{x^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{z\in[k]}\mathcal{E}_{z}\circ\mathcal{N}\circ\mathcal{F}_{z}(\mathbf{e}^{X^{\prime}}_{x^{\prime}})

(81)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in[m]\\
z\in[k]\end{subarray}}f(xz|x)\mathcal{E}_{z}\circ\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{e}^{X}_{x})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in[m]\\
z\in[k]\end{subarray}}f(xz|x)\mathcal{E}_{z}(\mathbf{u}^{Y})\otimes\mathbf{e}^{Z_{1}}_{x}

(82)

The left side of the equality is a conditionally uniform channel,

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}^{Y}\otimes\mathbf{r}^{Z_{1}}_{x^{\prime}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in[m]\\
z\in[k]\end{subarray}}f(xz|x)\mathcal{E}_{z}(\mathbf{u}^{Y})\otimes\mathbf{e}^{Z_{1}}_{x}

(83)

Suppose
\mathcal{M}^{XZ_{0}\to YZ_{1}}
is another classical conditionally uniform channel, then its transformation by
\Theta
is given by

\displaystyle\Theta[\mathcal{M}](\mathbf{e}_{x^{\prime}}^{X^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{x,z}f(xz|x^{\prime})\mathcal{E}_{z}(\mathbf{u}^{Y})\otimes\mathbf{q}_{x}^{Z_{1}}

(84)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x,z,w}f(xz|x^{\prime})\mathcal{E}_{z}(\mathbf{u}^{Y})\otimes q_{w}\mathbf{e}_{w}^{Z_{1}}

(85)

\displaystyle=\sum_{w}q_{w}\pqty{\sum_{x,z} f(xz\vert x') \cal{E}_z (\*{u}^Y)\otimes\*{e}_w ^{Z_1}}

(86)

The parenthesis terms on the right side of the last line is identical to the right side of the Equation (
83
). The convex sum of
\mathbf{u}^{Y}\otimes\mathbf{r}^{Z_{1}}_{x^{\prime}}
over
x^{\prime}\in[m^{\prime}]
is equal to
\mathbf{u}^{Y}\otimes\mathbf{q}^{Z_{1}}
for some
\mathbf{q}^{Z_{1}}
, concluding the proof.
∎

3
Operational approach

In an operational approach, the channel majorization is defined by a game of chance played with a channel
\mathcal{N}
. The game is identical to that previously discuss in
[
52
]
. This game is a generalization of the game of chance called
k
-game introduced in the section of probability vector majorization. In a
k
-game with probability vector, a player aware of the probability distribution associated with probability vector
\mathbf{p}
and given
k
guesses of a sample drawn from the distribution.

Suppose we want to play a similar gambling game with a channel. A classical channel
\mathcal{N}
receive an input
\mathbf{e}^{X}_{x}
for some
x\in[m]
and output a sample drawn from a probability distribution associated with the probability vector
\mathbf{p}_{x}:=\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{e}_{x}^{X})
. That is, we can play a
k
-game with a probability vector
\mathbf{p}_{x}
, an output of the classical channel.

Unlike a probability vector, the player can choose an input to the channel, consequently influencing what probability distribution a sample of output to be drawn from. Moreover, the optimal input of the channel depends on whether the player knows the value of
k
. For example, if a player knows
k
prior to giving the input, the best strategy for the player is to pick an input that has the highest
k^{\text{th}}
Ky Fan norm, similar to the
k
-game with a probability vector. If a player knows nothing about
k
prior to giving an input (
k
is uniformly distributed), then the best strategy is to pick the input that result in
\mathbf{p}_{x}
with the highest average
k^{\text{th}}
Ky Fan norm over all possible
k\in[n]
.

How much knowledge of
k
that the player know prior to giving an input need not be between these two extremes: certainly know
k
and complete ignorant. Some partial information could be sent to the player prior to give an input via a number
w\in[\ell]
. For example,
w=0
represents
k
being an even number and
w=1
represent
k
being an odd number. In general, we can represent a full spectrum of prior knowledge of
k
by a joint probability vector
\mathbf{t}
between the random variables
k
, the number of guesses, and
w
, the value player learned before giving an input. Therefore, we coined the term
\mathbf{t}
-game to denote a gambling game with a classical channel when the prior knowledge of
k
is described by the joint probability
\mathbf{t}
. A diagram illustration for this family of games can be found in Figure
4
.

To give more examples, suppose a player knows nothing about
k
prior to giving an input to the channel. The joint conditional probability distribution is described by a vector
\mathbf{t}=\mathbf{t}^{W}\otimes\mathbf{u}^{K}
which represent the most uncertainty of
k
and independence of
w
and
k
. On the other hand, if a player gain perfect knowledge of
k
prior to giving the input, then the probability vector is
\mathbf{t}=\sum_{k\in[n]}t_{k}\mathbf{e}_{k}^{W}\otimes\mathbf{e}_{k}^{K}
where
\sum_{k\in[n]}t_{k}=1
and
0\leq t_{k}\leq 1
.

The best strategy for any
t
-games is as follows. After a player receives a value of
w
, they choose an input
\mathbf{e}_{x}^{X}
such that it maximizes a convex sum of the
k^{\text{th}}
Ky Fan norm of
\mathbf{p}_{x}
weighted by the conditional probability
\quantity{t_{k\vert w}: k\in[n]}
.
That is, a winning chance of a
\mathbf{t}
-game is expressible as

\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathcal{N})=\sum_{w\in[\ell]}t_{w}\max_{x\in[m]}\sum_{k\in[n]}t_{k|w}\left\|\mathbf{p}_{x}\right\|_{(k)}.

(87)

Figure 4
:
The diagram of how a
\mathbf{t}
-game is played. A player, named Alice, is given a full knowledge of a joint probability distribution
\mathbf{t}
and a transition matrix
N
associated with the classical channel
\mathcal{N}
. Once the game start, she will be made aware of the hint
w
and then give an input
x
. To win the game, she has to provide a correct guess of the value
y
, the output of the channel, within
k
guesses. This figure previously appeared in
[
64
]
.

We operationally define majorization as the comparison of the winning chance.

Operational definition of majorization

Definition 2.10
.

For any classical channels
\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}
and
\mathcal{M}^{X^{\prime}\to Y}
, we say
\mathcal{N}
majorize
\mathcal{M}
operationally if

\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathcal{N})\geq\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathcal{M})\quad\forall\mathbf{t}\in\mathrm{Prob}(n\ell)

(88)

In this approach, we can extend the winning chance to the concept of predictability. We define the
predictability
of a classical channel via a function
\mathbf{P}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
as follows.

Predictability of a classical channel

Definition 2.11
.

The predictability of a classical channel
\mathcal{N}
with the vector
\mathbf{s}
is defined as

\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathbf{s})=\max_{x\in[m]}\mathbf{s}\cdot\mathbf{p}_{x}^{\downarrow},

(89)

where
\mathbf{p}_{x}^{\downarrow}
is the
x
-th column of the transition matrix associated with
\mathcal{N}
and is rearranged in the non-increasing order.

The function
\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{N}}\pqty{\s}
captures the notion of predictability whenever
\mathbf{s}\in\mathrm{Prob}^{\downarrow}(n)
. Specifically, we have the following equivalent statement for operational majorization.

Corollary 2.12
.

For any classical channels
\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}
and
\mathcal{M}^{X^{\prime}\to Y}
, we have that
\mathcal{N}\succ\mathcal{M}
operationally if and only if

\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathbf{s})\geq\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathbf{s})\quad\forall\mathbf{s}\in\mathrm{Prob}^{\downarrow}(n).

(90)

Proof.

To prove that operational majorization implies the inequality of predictability functions, consider
\mathbf{t}\in\mathrm{Prob}(n)
, which is the scenario where player know nothing prior to giving the input,
\ell=1
. From
\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathcal{N})=\max_{x\in[m]}\sum_{k}t_{k}\left\|\mathbf{p}_{x}\right\|_{(k)}=\max_{x\in[m]}\mathbf{t}\cdot L\mathbf{p}^{\downarrow}_{x}
, the channel
\mathcal{N}
operationally majorize
\mathcal{M}
implies

\max_{x\in[m]}U\mathbf{t}\cdot\mathbf{p}_{x}^{\downarrow}\geq\max_{x^{\prime}\in[m^{\prime}]}U\mathbf{t}\cdot\mathbf{q}_{x^{\prime}}^{\downarrow}\quad\forall\mathbf{t}\in\mathrm{Prob}(n),

(91)

where
L
is a lower triangular matrix with all non-zero elements equal to one and
U=L^{T}
. By defining
\mathbf{s}:=\frac{U\mathbf{t}}{\norm{U\*{t}}_{1}}
, we have that the Equation (
90
) holds for all
\mathbf{s}\in\mathrm{Prob}^{\downarrow}(n)
.

Conversely, the winning chance of a
\mathbf{t}
-game can be written as a sum of
\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathbf{s}_{w})
where
\mathbf{s}_{w}=U\mathbf{t}_{w}
and
\mathbf{t}_{w}=\sum_{k\in[n]}\mathbf{t}_{k|w}\mathbf{e}_{k}
.

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathcal{N})

\displaystyle=\sum_{w\in[\ell]}t_{w}\max_{x\in[m]}\sum_{k\in[n]}t_{k|w}\left\|\mathbf{p}_{x}\right\|_{(k)}

(92)

\displaystyle=\sum_{w\in[\ell]}\max_{x\in[m]}\sum_{k\in[n]}t_{kw}\left\|\mathbf{p}_{x}\right\|_{(k)}

(93)

\displaystyle=\sum_{w\in[\ell]}\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathbf{s}_{w})

(94)

If we have that the Equation (
90
) holds for all
\mathbf{s}\in\mathrm{Prob}(n)
, then we have that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathcal{N})=\sum_{w\in[\ell]}\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathbf{s}_{w})\geq\sum_{w\in[\ell]}\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathbf{s}_{w})=\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathcal{M}),

(95)

showing the converse direction of the proof.

∎

A sufficient condition

This previous corollary implies that it is sufficient to consider only the
\mathbf{t}
-games where prior to giving an input, the player is completely clueless about the number of guesses allowed, that is
\mathbf{t}\in\mathrm{Prob}(n)
. This family of
\mathbf{t}
-games gives a sufficient condition to conclude the inequality in winning chance of any
\mathbf{t}
-games where
\mathbf{t}\in\mathrm{Prob}(n\ell)
.

Corollary 2.13
.

Suppose
\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}
and
\mathcal{M}^{X^{\prime}\to Y}
are classical channels. We have that

\forall\mathbf{t}\in\mathrm{Prob}(n\ell)\quad\mathbf{Pr}_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathcal{N})\geq\mathbf{Pr}_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathcal{M})\quad\iff\quad\forall\mathbf{t}\in\mathrm{Prob}(n)\quad\mathbf{Pr}_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathcal{N})\geq\mathbf{Pr}_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathcal{M}).

(96)

Proof.

The
\implies
direction is trivial by setting
\ell=1
. We left to show the converse implication.

Using the previous corollary, we want to show that the winning chance of
\mathbf{t}
-games for all
\mathbf{t}\in\mathrm{Prob}(n)
is enough to yield the predictability inequality
\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathbf{s})\geq\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathbf{s})
for any
\mathbf{s}\in\mathrm{Prob}^{\downarrow}(n)
. The winning chance can be rephrased as the predictability as follows
\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{N}}(U\mathbf{t})=\frac{1}{\norm{Ut}_{1}}\mathbf{Pr}_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathcal{N})
where
U
is an upper triangular matrix with non-zero elements equal to one. Since
U
is invertible, there is a one-to-one correspondence between
\mathbf{t}\in\mathrm{Prob}(n)
and
\mathbf{s}\in\mathrm{Prob}(n)
. If
\Pr_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathcal{N})\geq\Pr_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathcal{M})
for all
\mathbf{t}\in\mathrm{Prob}(n)
, then we have
\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathbf{s})\geq\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathbf{s})
for all
\mathbf{s}\in\mathrm{Prob}^{\downarrow}(n)
as well.
∎

The operational approach is coincided with the previous two approaches.

Equivalence between axiomatic and operational approaches

Theorem 2.14
.

Suppose
\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}
and
\mathcal{M}^{X^{\prime}\to Y}
are two classical channels. There exists a completely uniformity-preserving superchannel
\Theta
such that
\mathcal{M}=\Theta[\mathcal{N}]
if and only if

\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathcal{N})\geq\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathcal{M})\quad\forall\mathbf{t}\in\mathrm{Prob}(n).

(97)

Proof.

By the Corollary
2.12
,
\mathcal{N}
majorizing
\mathcal{M}
operationally is equivalent to having

\max_{x\in[m]}\mathbf{s}\cdot\mathbf{p}_{x}^{\downarrow}\geq\max_{x^{\prime}\in[m^{\prime}]}\mathbf{s}\cdot\mathbf{q}_{x^{\prime}}^{\downarrow},

(98)

for any
\mathbf{s}\in\mathrm{Prob}^{\downarrow}(n)
, where
\mathbf{p}_{x}:=\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{e}_{x})
for each
x\in[m]
and
\mathbf{q}_{x^{\prime}}:=\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{e}_{x^{\prime}})
for each
x^{\prime}\in[m^{\prime}]
. Furthermore, in Theorem
2.4
, there exists
\Theta
such that
\mathcal{M}=\Theta[\mathcal{N}]
if and only if there exists
S\in\mathrm{Stoch}(n,n)
,
S=(S_{x|w})
such that

\forall w\in[m^{\prime}]\quad\sum_{x\in[m]}s_{x|w}\mathbf{p}_{x}^{\downarrow}\succ\mathbf{q}_{w}.

(99)

The strategy is to phrase this in terms of feasibility problem and use Farkas’ lemma to show equivalence to the Inequality
98
.

Suppose
\mathbf{p}_{x}=\mathbf{p}_{x}^{\downarrow}
and
\mathbf{q}_{w}=\mathbf{q}_{w}^{\downarrow}
so that the Equation
99
can be written as

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in[m]}s_{x|w}L\mathbf{p}_{x}\geq L\mathbf{q}_{w}\quad\text{(element-wise)}

(100)

or equivalently,

LN\mathbf{s}_{w}\geq L\mathbf{q}_{w}

(101)

where
\mathbf{s}_{w}
is the
w^{\text{th}}
column of the stochastic matrix
S
.
\mathbf{s}_{w}
has to have its elements sum to
1
. That is
\mathbf{1}\cdot\mathbf{s}_{w}=1
. Notice that if we have
\mathbf{1}\cdot\mathbf{s}<1
then we can multiply by the number that is greater 1 to get equality and not violate the Inequality
101
. Therefore, we can rewrite the condition in the Theorem
2.4
as

\bmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}\mathbf{s}_{w}\leq\bmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}.

(102)

From Farkas’ lemma, such
\mathbf{s}
exists if and only if for all
\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, we define
\mathbf{r}:=\mathbf{t}\oplus\lambda
and have the following implication,

\mathbf{r}^{T}\bmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}\geq 0\implies\mathbf{r}^{T}\bmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}\geq 0

(103)

The premise is equivalent to

\lambda\geq\mathbf{t}^{T}LN=(U\mathbf{t})^{T}N.

(104)

Then the least
\lambda
that satisfy the Inequality is
\lambda_{\textrm{min}}=\max_{x\in[m]}U\mathbf{t}\cdot\mathbf{p}_{x}.
The implication is equivalent to
\lambda\geq U\mathbf{t}\cdot\mathbf{q}_{w}
. Define
\mathbf{s}=U\mathbf{t}
and consider the smallest
\lambda
that the premise holds, we have that

\max_{x\in[m]}\mathbf{s}\cdot\mathbf{p}_{x}\geq\mathbf{s}\cdot\mathbf{q}_{w}.

(105)

Since this holds for all
w
, then it holds for the maximum
\mathbf{s}\cdot\mathbf{q}_{w}
as well.
∎

4
Equivalence of the three approaches

From the Theorem
2.8
and the Theorem
2.14
, we see that all three approaches results in the same ordering of classical channels. This way, we can establish the majorization preorder on the domain of classical channel. From now on, the majorization of classical channels will be simply referred as majorization without any adverb: constructively, axiomatically, or operationally. Symbolically, the symbol
\succ
represents majorization of classical channels.

Approach

Definition of majorization

Constructive

Convertibility via random permutation superchannel

Axiomatic

Convertibility via completely uniformity preserving

Operational

Comparison of winning chance in any
\mathbf{t}
-games

Table 1
:
Definitions of classical channel majorization in each approach.

In hindsight, the coincidence makes an intuitive sense. The constructive approach prescribes a construction which randomly permutes the outcome, which preserves the uniform distribution and reduce the predictability of the outcome. The axiomatic approach enforces that the uniform distribution must be preserved similarly to the axiomatic characterization of the standard probability majorization. Lastly, the operational approach is an extension of
k
-games which characterize the standard probability majorization operationally.

5
Comparison of classical channels across all output dimensions

In previous sections, we consider only channels which its output has the same dimension. However, we can compare two channels that output states in different dimensions. Suppose that
\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}
and
\mathcal{M}^{X^{\prime}\to Y^{\prime}}
are classical channels and
\dim{Y}<\dim{Y^{\prime}}
. The channel majorization relation is defined as a majorization relation between
\mathcal{M}^{X^{\prime}\to Y^{\prime}}
and
\mathcal{V}^{Y\to Y^{\prime}}\circ\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}
. That is

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}\succ\mathcal{M}

\displaystyle:\iff\mathcal{V}^{Y\to Y^{\prime}}\circ\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}\succ\mathcal{M}^{X^{\prime}\to Y^{\prime}}

(106)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}\succ\mathcal{N}

\displaystyle:\iff\mathcal{M}^{X^{\prime}\to Y^{\prime}}\succ\mathcal{V}^{Y\to Y^{\prime}}\circ\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}

(107)

In terms of transition matrix associated with
N
, the dimensions of two transition matrices can be matched by adding zero into the smaller dimensions, similarly to the case of probability vectors.

3
Properties of channel majorization

In this section, we explore basic properties of the newly proposed majorization: its optimal elements, the preordering given by the majorization, and the recipe for picking a unique representative of an equivalence class given by this preordering.

1
Minimal and maximal elements

Consider the set of all
\bigcup_{X}\mathrm{CPTP}(X\to Y)
, the most uncertain channel in this domain is a maximally randomizing channel
\mathcal{R}\in\mathrm{CPTP}(1\to Y)
, and the most certain are channels
\mathcal{N}_{y}\in\mathrm{CPTP}(1\to Y)
that produce a standard basis vector
\mathbf{e}_{y}^{Y}
for a certain
y\in[n]
.

Proposition 3.1
.

For any classical channel
\mathcal{M}^{X\to Y}
, we have that

1.

\mathcal{M}^{X\to Y}\succ\mathcal{R}^{1\to Y}
.

2.

\mathcal{N}_{y}^{1\to Y}\succ\mathcal{M}^{X\to Y}

Proof.

For
\mathcal{N}\succ\mathcal{R}
, we have
\mathcal{R}=\Theta[\mathcal{N}]
where
\Theta[\mathcal{N}]=\Tr_{Y}[\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{e}_{1}^{X})]~\mathbf{u}^{Y}
. This superchannel is completely uniformity preserving, as

\Theta\otimes\mathsf{id}[\mathcal{N}^{XW\to YZ}](\mathbf{p}^{W})=\Tr_{Y}[\mathcal{N}^{XW\to YZ}(\mathbf{e}_{1}^{X}\otimes\mathbf{p}^{W})]\otimes\mathbf{u}^{Y}=\mathcal{N}^{XW\to Z}(\mathbf{e}_{1}^{X}\otimes\mathbf{p}^{W})\otimes\mathbf{u}^{Y}.

Notice that
\mathcal{R}^{X^{\prime}\to Y}
for any
X^{\prime}
also the minimal as the standard form of the maximally randomizing channel
\mathcal{R}^{X^{\prime}\to Y}
is the preparation channel preparing
\mathbf{u}^{Y}
from the trivial state
1
,
\mathcal{R}^{1\to Y}
.

For
\mathcal{N}^{1\to Y}_{y}\succ\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}
, suppose
\mathbf{p}_{x}
is the
x
-th column of a transition matrix corresponding to
\mathcal{N}
. We have that for any
\mathbf{s}=\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}^{T}\in\mathrm{Prob}^{\downarrow}(n)

\mathbf{s}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{y}^{\downarrow}=\mathbf{s}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{1}=s_{1}\geq\max_{x\in[m]}\mathbf{s}\cdot\mathbf{p}_{x}.

(108)

By the Corollary
2.12
, it follows that
\mathcal{N}_{y}^{1\to Y}\succ\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}
.
∎

2
Classical channel majorization is a preorder

Lemma 3.2
.

A classical channel majorization (
\succ
) is a preorder on the set of classical channels.

Proof.

To show the reflexivity of
\succ
, notice that an identity superchannel
1^{(X\to Y)}
is a completely uniformity-preserving superchannel. Therefore, any
\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}
is convertible to itself via an application of the identity superchannel.

On the transitivity of
\succ
, we will show that the composition of completely uniformity-preserving superchannels is also a uniformity preserving. Suppose a classical superchannel
\Theta\in\mathrm{SUPER}((X_{0}\to Y)\to(X_{1}\to Y))
, a classical superchannel
\Upsilon\in\mathrm{SUPER}((X_{1}\to Y)\to(X_{2}\to Y))
, and a conditionally uniform channel
\mathcal{M}^{X_{0}W\to YZ}
. We have that
\mathcal{N}:=\Theta\otimes 1^{(W\to Z)}[\mathcal{M}]
is a conditionally uniform channel and
\Upsilon\otimes\otimes 1^{(W\to Z)}[\mathcal{N}]
is also a conditionally uniform channel.

Suppose
\mathcal{N}\succ\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{M}\succ\mathcal{P}
. Suppose
\mathcal{M}=\Theta[\mathcal{N}]
and
\mathcal{P}=\Upsilon[\mathcal{M}]
. We have that
\Upsilon\circ\Theta
is a completely uniformity-preserving superchannel and therefore
\mathcal{P}=\Upsilon\circ\Theta[\mathcal{N}]
and
\mathcal{N}\succ\mathcal{P}
.
∎

3
Replacement channel: reduction to probability majorization

One can look at a quantum state as a replacement channel that map the trivial state,
1
, to the quantum state. For this reason, one would expect that the state majorization relation is reflected in the channel majorization as well. By definition, a replacement channel is a channel that always output the same quantum state, says
\sigma\in\mathfrak{D}(B)
, for any input
\rho\in\mathfrak{D}(A)
. The replacement channel
\mathcal{N}_{\sigma}\in\mathrm{CPTP}(A\to B)
is defined as

\mathcal{N}_{\sigma}(\rho)=\Tr[\rho]\sigma.

(109)

In the classical domain, a classical replacement channel takes classical state
\rho\in\mathfrak{D}(X)
and another classical state output
\sigma\in\mathfrak{D}(Y)
. As we use probability vectors to represent classical states, one can express a classical replacement channel as a transition matrix with all columns being an associated vector of
\sigma
, here we call it
\mathbf{q}
. We have the following equivalence

Lemma 3.3
.

Suppose
\mathcal{N}\in\mathrm{CPTP}(X\to Y)
and
\mathcal{M}\in\mathrm{CPTP}(X\to Y^{\prime})
be classical replacement channels replacing with
\mathbf{p}
and
\mathbf{q}
respectively. We have that
\mathcal{N}\succ\mathcal{M}
if and only if
\mathbf{p}\succ\mathbf{q}
.

Proof.

Suppose
\mathbf{p}\succ\mathbf{q}
; then we have
\mathbf{q}=D\mathbf{p}
. To show that
\mathcal{N}\succ\mathcal{M}
, define
\Theta
such that

\Theta[\mathcal{F}]=\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{F}.

(110)

This shows that
\Theta[\mathcal{N}]=\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{M}
.

On the converse direction, suppose
\mathcal{N}\succ\mathcal{M}
. We have that there is
\Theta
superchannel such that
\mathcal{M}=\Theta[\mathcal{N}]
. Writing this superchannel in its standard form results in

\Theta[\mathcal{N}]=\sum_{x,x^{\prime}}\mathcal{E}_{x,x^{\prime}}\circ\mathcal{N}\circ\mathcal{F}_{x,x^{\prime}}

(111)

Then input any
\mathbf{e}_{w}
into the channel,

\displaystyle\sum_{x,x^{\prime}}\mathcal{E}_{x,x^{\prime}}\circ\mathcal{N}\circ\mathcal{F}_{x,x^{\prime}}(\mathbf{e}_{w})

\displaystyle=\sum_{x,x^{\prime}}f(x|w)\mathcal{E}_{x,w}\circ\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}(\mathbf{e}^{X}).

(112)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x,x^{\prime}}f(x|w)\mathcal{E}_{x,w}(\mathbf{p})=D\mathbf{p}

(113)

∎

4
The standard form of channel

In the domain of probability vectors, two equivalent probability vectors are equal if both vectors have their elements in non-increasing order. Similarly, there is a way to turn majorization preorder on the classical channels into the partial order by restricting the set of classical channels to only those in the
standard form
.

Suppose we have a classical channel
\mathcal{N}
. If there is another classical channel
\mathcal{M}
that is equivalent with the channel
\mathcal{N}
, then we say that it is in the same equivalence class, but need not be the same channel. The goal of defining the standard form is to pick one unique representative of any equivalent class of channels. We define the standard form for a classical channel as follows.

The standard form of classical channel

Definition 3.4
.

A classical channel
\mathcal{N}
with the associated transition matrix
N=\pqty{\p_1 \ldots\p_n}
is in the standard form, if the followings are true for any
x\in[m]
.

1.

Non-increasing order:
\mathbf{p}_{x}=\mathbf{p}^{\downarrow}_{x}
.

2.

Incomparable columns:
\mathbf{p}_{x}
is not majorized by a convex sum of other columns.

3.

Lexicographical order: If
x\neq m
and
\ell
is the lowest index such that
(\mathbf{p}_{x})_{\ell}\neq(\mathbf{p}_{x+1})_{\ell}
, then
(\mathbf{p}_{x})_{\ell}>(\mathbf{p}_{x+1})_{\ell}
.

To turn a classical channel
\mathcal{N}
into its standard form, one obtains the transition matrix
N_{s}
associated with the standard form of the channel
\mathcal{N}
, denoted with
\mathcal{N}_{s}
, by the following steps.

1.

Replace each and every column
\mathbf{p}_{x}
with their own in non-increasing ordered vector
\mathbf{p}_{x}^{\downarrow}
.

2.

Remove, one at a time, a column that is majorized by a convex sum of other columns.

3.

Rearrange the columns so that for each
\mathbf{p}_{x}
and
\mathbf{p}_{x+1}
, the first
\ell-1
elements are equal while the
\ell^{\mathrm{th}}
elements of
\mathbf{p}_{x}
is greater than that of
\mathbf{p}_{x+1}
.

The above procedure turns a classical channel into its standard form. This process produces a unique and equivalent classical channel to the original one.

Lemma 3.5
.

Suppose
\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}
is a classical channel. Its standard form,
\mathcal{N}_{s}^{X^{\prime}\to Y}
, is equivalent to
\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}
.

Proof.

We use the Theorem
2.4
in both directions.

Consider the direction
\mathcal{N}_{s}\succ\mathcal{N}
. Any
\mathbf{p}_{x}
for
x\in[m]
is either in
\quantity{\cal{N}_s(\e_x): x\in[m']}
or is majorized by a convex sum of columns of
N_{s}
. Therefore,
\mathcal{N}_{s}\succ\mathcal{N}
.

Conversely, consider the direction
\mathcal{N}\succ\mathcal{N}_{s}
. Since, no new column is added to
N_{s}
, any
\mathbf{q}_{w}=\mathcal{N}_{s}(\mathbf{e}_{w})
is
\mathbf{q}=\mathbf{p}^{\downarrow}
for some
\mathbf{p}\in\mathcal{N}(\mathrm{Prob}(m))
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Once the channel is in the standard form, any two equivalents classical channels have to be equal. That is a preorder of channel majorization turns into a partial order on the set of classical channels in the standard form.

Channel majorization is a partial order on the set of channels in the standard form

Theorem 3.6
.

Suppose
\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}
and
\mathcal{M}^{X^{\prime}\to Y}
are classical channels in the standard form. We have that
\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}\sim\mathcal{M}^{X^{\prime}\to Y}
if and only if
\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}=\mathcal{M}^{X^{\prime}\to Y}
.

Proof.

The if direction is trivial, we will prove the only-if direction.
First, the majorization conditions are equivalent with

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}\succ\mathcal{M}

\displaystyle\iff\exists S=(s_{x|w})\in\mathrm{Stoch}(m,m^{\prime})\quad\sum_{x}s_{x|w}\mathbf{p}_{x}\succ\mathbf{q}_{w}\quad\forall w\in[m^{\prime}]

(114)

\displaystyle\iff LNS\geq LM

(115)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}\succ\mathcal{N}

\displaystyle\iff\exists T=(t_{w|x})\in\mathrm{Stoch}(m^{\prime},m)\quad\sum_{w}t_{w|x}\mathbf{q}_{w}\succ\mathbf{p}_{x}\quad\forall w\in[m^{\prime}]

(116)

\displaystyle\iff LMT\geq LN

(117)

Apply
T
on the right in the Inequality
115
, and
S
to the right in the Inequality
117
, and define
R=ST
and
R^{\prime}=TS
. Using the transitive property, we have

\displaystyle LNR\geq LMT\geq LN\quad\text{and}\quad LMR^{\prime}\geq LNS\geq LM.

(118)

That is

LN\mathbf{r}_{x}\geq L\mathbf{p}_{x}\quad\forall x\in[m]\quad\text{and}\quad LM\mathbf{r}_{w}\geq L\mathbf{q}_{w}\quad\forall w\in[m^{\prime}]

(119)

where
\mathbf{r}_{x}=(r_{x^{\prime}|x})
and
\mathbf{r}_{w}=(r^{\prime}_{w^{\prime}|w})
are the
x^{\mathrm{th}}
column of
R
and the
w^{\mathrm{th}}
column of
R^{\prime}
respectively.
If
r_{x|x}\neq 1
, then

\sum_{x^{\prime}\neq x}r_{x^{\prime}|x}L\mathbf{p}_{x^{\prime}}\geq(1-r_{x|x})L\mathbf{p}_{x},

(120)

which implies the existence of convex combination of other columns that majorizing the
x^{\mathrm{th}}
column, therefore
r_{x^{\prime}|x}=\delta_{xx^{\prime}}
and similarly with
R^{\prime}
,
r^{\prime}_{w^{\prime}|w}=\delta_{ww^{\prime}}
. With the consideration of
ST=R=I_{m}
and
TS=R^{\prime}=I_{m^{\prime}}
, we have that
S
and
T
is inverse of one another. The only invertible stochastic matrices are permutation matrices, which are square. From the following inequality, we have the equality

LNS\geq LM\text{ and }LM=LMTS\geq LNS\implies LNS=LM\implies NS=M.

(121)

This implies that
N
and
M
are identical up to column permutation, however being in the standard form impose a unique way of arranging columns. Therefore,
\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{M}
.
∎

4
Characterizations of classical channel majorization

In this section, we provide additional characterizations of the classical channel majorization. This is in addition to the characterization we previously provided in the Theorem
2.4
and in the corollary
2.12
.

1
Majorization of outputs

One interesting characterization of channel majorization is through the set of its outputs, which is a convex hull of the columns of its corresponding transition matrix. The image of all
\mathbf{p}\in\mathrm{Prob}(m)
by
\mathcal{N}
is a convex set. This is because the channel
\mathcal{N}
is linear and
\mathbf{p}
can be put in a convex sum of vectors in the standard basis,

\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{p})=\mathcal{N}(\sum_{x\in[m]}p_{x}\mathbf{e}_{x})=\sum_{x\in[m]}p_{x}\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{e}_{x})

(122)

Now we can see that the image is a convex hull of columns of
\mathcal{N}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}\pqty{\prob(m)}

\displaystyle=\quantity{\cal{N}(\p):\p\in\prob(n)}=\quantity{\sum_{x\in[m]} p_x \cal{N}(\e_{x}) : \p= \sum_{x\in[m]} p_x \e_x \in\prob(m)}

(123)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Conv}\quantity{\cal{N}(\e_x) : x\in[m]}.

(124)

Using the Theorem
2.4
and the standard form of channels, we have the following characterization classical channel majorization.

Majorization of outputs of channels

Corollary 4.1
.

Given two classical channels,
\mathcal{N}
and
\mathcal{M}
, in their standard form, we have
\mathcal{N}\succ\mathcal{M}
if and only if for any elements
\mathbf{q}\in\mathcal{M}(\mathrm{Prob}(m^{\prime}))
, there exists an element
\mathbf{p}\in\mathcal{N}(\mathrm{Prob}(m))
such that
\mathbf{p}\succ\mathbf{q}
.

Proof.

The proposition is directly followed from the Theorem
2.4
. In the if direction, we have that all any columns of
M
can be majorized by a convex combination of columns of
N
showing that,
\mathcal{N}\succ\mathcal{M}
.

On the only-if direction, suppose
\mathcal{N}\succ\mathcal{M}
. We have that any column of
M
,
\mathbf{q}_{w}
, is majorized by a convex combination of columns of
N
. Denote the convex combination with
\mathbf{r}_{w}=\sum_{x\in[m]}s_{x|w}\mathbf{p}_{x}
. Any
\mathbf{q}\in\mathcal{M}(\mathrm{Prob}(m^{\prime}))
, is a convex combination of
\mathbf{q}_{w}
, i.e.
\mathbf{q}=\sum_{w\in[m^{\prime}]}t_{w}\mathbf{q}_{w}
. Since
\mathbf{r}_{w}\succ\mathbf{q}_{w}
and
\mathbf{p}_{x},\mathbf{r}_{w},
and
\mathbf{q}_{w}
are in non-increasing order, we have the following chain of inequalities. For any
k\in[n]
,

\left\|\sum_{w\in[m^{\prime}]}t_{w}\mathbf{r}_{w}\right\|_{(k)}=\sum_{w\in[m^{\prime}]}t_{w}\left\|\mathbf{r}_{w}\right\|_{(k)}\geq\sum_{w\in[m^{\prime}]}t_{w}\left\|\mathbf{q}_{w}\right\|_{(k)}=\left\|\mathbf{q}\right\|_{(k)},

showing that
\mathbf{r}:=\sum_{w\in[m^{\prime}]}t_{w}\mathbf{r}_{w}\succ\mathbf{q}
. The vector
\mathbf{r}
is in the image
\mathcal{N}\pqty{\prob(m)}
as it is a convex combination of
\mathbf{p}_{x}
with the convex coefficients
\quantity{\sum_{w\in[m']} t_w s_{x\vert w}: x\in[m]}
.

∎

2
Set containment

In the case of classical probability vector,
\mathbf{p}\succ\mathbf{q}
if and only if
\mathbf{q}
is in the convex hull of all permutations of
\mathbf{p}
. Similarly, in the case of classical channels, we have the following characterization.

Set-containment characterization

Theorem 4.2
.

Suppose
\mathcal{N}
and
\mathcal{M}
are classical channels, we have that
\mathcal{N}\succ\mathcal{M}
if and only if

\mathrm{Conv}\quantity{\Pi\q_w:\Pi\in\perm(n),w\in[m']}\subseteq\mathrm{Conv}\quantity{\Pi\p_x:\Pi\in\perm(n),x\in[m]}

(125)

Proof.

Suppose the set containment is true. For any
w\in[m^{\prime}]
, we have that any
\mathbf{q}_{w}
can be written as a convex sum of
\mathbf{p}_{x}
’s and their permutations,

\mathbf{q}_{w}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in[m]\\
\mathbf{v}\in[n!]\end{subarray}}s_{xv}\Pi_{v}\mathbf{p}_{x},

where
s_{xv}
’s are the convex coefficients for each
\Pi_{v}\mathbf{p}_{x}
. Any
\mathbf{p}_{x}
can be turned into
\mathbf{p}_{x}^{\downarrow}
by applying an appropriate permutation matrix, denote such permutation for each
\mathbf{p}_{x}
with
S_{x}
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{q}_{w}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in[m]\\
v\in[n!]\end{subarray}}s_{xv}\Pi_{v}S_{x}^{-1}S_{x}\mathbf{p}_{x}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\pqty{P_{xv}:=\Pi_v S_{x}^{-1}}\rightarrow}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in[m]\\
v\in[n!]\end{subarray}}s_{xv}P_{xv}\mathbf{p}_{x}^{\downarrow}=\sum_{x\in[m]}\pqty{\sum_{v\in[n!]} s_{xv}P_{xv}}\mathbf{p}_{x}^{\downarrow}=\sum_{x\in[m]}t_{x}D_{x}\mathbf{p}_{x}^{\downarrow},

where
t_{x}D_{x}:=\sum_{v\in[n!]}s_{xv}P_{xv}
and
D_{x}\in\mathrm{DSTOCH}(n)
. Now, consider the following chain of inequalities of the Ky Fan norms, for any
k\in[n]

\left\|\mathbf{q}_{w}\right\|_{(k)}=\left\|\sum_{x\in[m]}t_{x}D_{x}\mathbf{p}_{x}^{\downarrow}\right\|_{(k)}\leq\sum_{x\in[m]}t_{x}\left\|D_{x}\mathbf{p}_{x}\right\|_{(k)}\leq\sum_{x\in[m]}t_{x}\left\|\mathbf{p}_{x}\right\|_{(k)}=\left\|\sum_{x\in[m]}t_{x}\mathbf{p}_{x}^{\downarrow}\right\|_{(k)},

showing that
\sum_{x\in[m]}t_{x}\mathbf{p}_{x}^{\downarrow}\succ\mathbf{q}_{w}
. By Theorem
2.4
, we have that
\mathcal{N}\succ\mathcal{M}
.

Conversely, in the only-if direction, we suppose that
\mathcal{N}\succ\mathcal{M}
.

For any
\mathbf{q}\in\mathrm{Conv}\quantity{\Pi\q_w: \Pi\in\perm(n), w\in[m']}
, it can be written as

\mathbf{q}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in[n!]\\
w\in[m]\end{subarray}}t_{uw}\Pi_{u}\mathbf{q}_{w}

Since
\mathcal{N}\succ\mathcal{M}
, then any
w\in[m^{\prime}]
there is majorized by a convex combination of
\mathbf{p}_{x}
’s. This leads to

\mathbf{q}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in[n!]\\
w\in[m]\end{subarray}}t_{uw}\Pi_{u}D_{w}\sum_{x\in[m]}s_{x|w}\mathbf{p}_{x}.

Any doubly stochastic matrix can be written as a convex combination of permutation matrices. Suppose the combination for each
D_{w}
is
D_{w}=\sum_{v\in[n!]}r_{v|w}\Pi_{v}.
We have that

\mathbf{q}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u,v\\
