w,x\end{subarray}}t_{uw}s_{x|w}r_{v|w}\underbrace{\Pi_{u}\Pi_{v}}_{=:P_{uv}}\mathbf{p}_{x}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u,v\\
w,x\end{subarray}}t_{uw}s_{x|w}r_{v|w}P_{uv}\mathbf{p}_{x},

showing that
\mathbf{q}
is a convex combination of
\mathbf{p}_{x}
’s and their permutations.
∎

3
Characterization in low dimensional cases

For any classical channel with one dimensional output, we have the trivial classical states. Recall that the standard form requires no column of the transition matrix to be majorized by a convex sum of another columns. If a channel has two-dimensional output, its standard form is a probability vector, because all two-dimensional vectors are comparable by the majorization relation.

On the side of the input dimension, the input dimensions represent the number of choices of the output. For a one-dimensional output, we have probability vector, the channel majorization relation reduces to probability vector majorization by the Lemma
3.3
. For the higher dimensions, a dimension of the input can be inflated by adding columns that is in the convex hull of the pre-existing columns. The next following characterization theorems require classical channels to be in the standard form to avoid this elusive notion of the input dimension.

Suppose a classical channel
\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}
has two-dimensional input
m=2
and another classical channel
\mathcal{M}^{X^{\prime}\to Y}
has
m^{\prime}
-dimensional input . Suppose the channel
\mathcal{N}
has columns
\mathbf{p}_{1}
and
\mathbf{p}_{2}
. By Theorem
2.4
, we have that

\mathcal{N}\succ\mathcal{M}\iff\forall w\in[m^{\prime}]~\exists t_{w}\in[0,1]\quad t_{w}\mathbf{p}_{1}+(1-t_{w})\mathbf{p}_{2}\succ\mathbf{q}_{w}.

(126)

Since we require
\mathcal{N}
and
\mathcal{M}
to be in the standard form, we have
\mathbf{p}_{1}
and
\mathbf{p}_{2}
are in non-increasing order. By the majorization relation, we have that their Ky Fan norms satisfy

\left\|t_{w}\mathbf{p}_{1}+(1-t_{w})\mathbf{p}_{2}\right\|_{(k)}=t_{w}\left\|\mathbf{p}_{1}\right\|_{(k)}+(1-t_{w})\left\|\mathbf{p}_{2}\right\|_{(k)}\geq\left\|\mathbf{q}_{w}\right\|_{(k)}.

(127)

That is

t_{w}(\left\|\mathbf{p}_{1}\right\|_{(k)}-\left\|\mathbf{p}_{2}\right\|_{(k)})\geq\left\|\mathbf{q}_{w}\right\|_{(k)}-\left\|\mathbf{p}_{2}\right\|_{(k)}.

(128)

This equality gives enough condition to check existence of
t_{w}
. The idea is to divide both side by
\left\|\mathbf{p}_{1}\right\|_{(k)}-\left\|\mathbf{p}_{2}\right\|_{(k)}
. We categorize the each index
k
into one of three sets
\mathfrak{I}_{+},\mathfrak{I}_{0},\mathfrak{I}_{-}
where the denominator is positive, zero, and negative respectively. That is

\displaystyle\mathfrak{I}_{+}

\displaystyle:=\quantity{k: \knorm{\p_1}{k} - \knorm{\p_2}{k} > 0},

(129)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{I}_{0}

\displaystyle:=\quantity{k: \knorm{\p_1}{k} - \knorm{\p_2}{k} = 0},

(130)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{I}_{-}

\displaystyle:=\quantity{k: \knorm{\p_1}{k} - \knorm{\p_2}{k} < 0}.

(131)

Notice that
k\in\mathfrak{I}_{0}
, we have
\left\|\mathbf{p}_{2}\right\|_{(k)}\geq\left\|\mathbf{q}_{w}\right\|_{(k)}
.

For
k\in\mathfrak{I}_{+}
, dividing both side of the inequality by
\left\|\mathbf{p}_{1}\right\|_{(k)}-\left\|\mathbf{p}_{2}\right\|_{(k)}
yields

\displaystyle t_{w}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\left\|\mathbf{q}_{w}\right\|_{(k)}-\left\|\mathbf{p}_{2}\right\|_{(k)}}{\left\|\mathbf{p}_{1}\right\|_{(k)}-\left\|\mathbf{p}_{2}\right\|_{(k)}}\quad\forall k\in[n]

(132)

\displaystyle\iff t_{w}

\displaystyle\geq\max_{k\in\mathfrak{I}_{+}}~\frac{\left\|\mathbf{q}_{w}\right\|_{(k)}-\left\|\mathbf{p}_{2}\right\|_{(k)}}{\left\|\mathbf{p}_{1}\right\|_{(k)}-\left\|\mathbf{p}_{2}\right\|_{(k)}}.

(133)

Similarly in
k\in\mathfrak{I}_{-}
, with converse inequality

\displaystyle t_{w}

\displaystyle\leq\min_{k\in\mathfrak{I}_{-}}~\frac{\left\|\mathbf{q}_{w}\right\|_{(k)}-\left\|\mathbf{p}_{2}\right\|_{(k)}}{\left\|\mathbf{p}_{1}\right\|_{(k)}-\left\|\mathbf{p}_{2}\right\|_{(k)}}.

(134)

Combine with
t_{w}\in[0,1]
we have

\displaystyle\max\quantity{0,\max_{k\in\fk{I}_+}~\frac{\knorm{\q_w}{k} - \knorm{\p_2}{k}}{\knorm{\p_1}{k} - \knorm{\p_2}{k}}}\leq t_{w}\leq\min\quantity{1, \min_{k\in\fk{I}_-}~\frac{\knorm{\q_w}{k} - \knorm{\p_2}{k}}{\knorm{\p_1}{k} - \knorm{\p_2}{k}}}

(135)

We define the leftmost quantity to be
\mu_{w}
and the rightmost
\nu_{w}
. We have the following theorem.

Characterization theorem for channels with two dimensional input

Theorem 4.3
.

For any
\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}
a classical channel in the standard form with two-dimensional input and any classical channel
\mathcal{M}
, we have that
\mathcal{N}\succ\mathcal{M}
if and only if the followings hold.

1.

For all
w\in[m^{\prime}]
,
\nu_{w}\geq\mu_{w}
.

2.

For all
k\in\mathfrak{I}_{0}
,
\left\|\mathbf{p}_{2}\right\|_{(k)}\geq\left\|\mathbf{q}_{w}\right\|_{(k)}
.

\nu_{w}
,
\mu_{w}
, and
\mathfrak{I}_{0}
are as previously defined.

4
Connection to conditional majorization

We proposed a channel majorization to capture uncertainty of the output given full control of the input. This notion is closely related with conditional majorization. Suppose
\mathbf{p}^{XY}
and
\mathbf{q}^{X^{\prime}Y}
are two joint probability vectors. The conditional majorization relation compares uncertainty of two probability distribution, given that we have access to the information on the systems
X
and
X^{\prime}

[
21
]
.

The classical channel naturally gives rise to a joint probability vector but not uniquely. Suppose Alice is an agent on the sending side of a classical channel
\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}
and Bob is at the receiving end. If Alice randomly gives an input from distribution
\mathbf{p}^{X}=(p_{x})
, then the joint probability between the input and the output is
\mathbf{p}^{XY}=\sum_{x}p_{x}\mathbf{e}_{x}\otimes\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{e}_{x})
. Notice that the joint probability vector
\mathbf{p}^{XY}
is dependent on the choice of input distribution
\mathbf{p}^{X}
, leading to multiple joint probability vectors associated with a classical channel.

Recall from the theorem
2.4
that a classical channels
\mathcal{N}
in its standard form majorizes another classical channel
\mathcal{M}
if we can find an input
\mathbf{p}
which give an output which majorize
\mathbf{q}_{w}:=\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{e}_{w})
for each of any
w\in[m^{\prime}]
. This motivates the following theorem.

Characterization with conditional majorization

Theorem 4.4
.

Suppose
\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}
and
\mathcal{M}^{X^{\prime}\to Y}
are classical channels and
\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}
is in the standard form. We have that
\mathcal{N}\succ\mathcal{M}
if and only if there exists
\mathbf{p}=(p_{x})
and
\mathbf{q}=(q_{w})
such that

\sum_{x\in[m]}p_{x}\mathbf{e}_{x}^{\tilde{X}}\otimes\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{e}_{x})\succ_{Y}\sum_{w\in[m^{\prime}]}q_{w}\mathbf{e}_{w}^{\tilde{X}^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{e}_{w}).

(136)

Proof.

Define
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{e}_{x})=\mathbf{p}_{x}
and
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{e}_{w})=\mathbf{q}_{w}
. The Equation (
136
) is equivalently written as

\sum_{x\in[m]}p_{x}\mathbf{e}_{x}^{\tilde{X}}\otimes\mathbf{p}_{x}\succ_{Y}\sum_{w\in[m^{\prime}]}q_{w}\mathbf{e}^{\tilde{X}^{\prime}}_{w}\otimes\mathbf{q}_{w}.

(137)

Using a characterization of conditional majorization with the existence of
R=(r_{w|x})\in\mathrm{Stoch}(m^{\prime},m)
such that for any
w\in[m^{\prime}]

\sum_{x\in[m]}r_{w|x}p_{x}\mathbf{p}_{x}\succ q_{w}\mathbf{q}_{w}.

(138)

For all
w\in[m^{\prime}]
, we have that

\sum_{x\in[m]}\frac{r_{w|x}p_{x}}{q_{w}}\mathbf{p}_{x}^{\downarrow}=\sum_{x\in[m]}\frac{r_{w|x}p_{x}}{q_{w}}\mathbf{p}_{x}\succ\mathbf{q}_{w},

(139)

showing that
\mathcal{N}\succ\mathcal{M}
by the characterization in theorem
2.4
.
∎

Chapter 4
Measures of classical channel uncertainty

With the foundation of channel majorization laid in previous chapter, we can provide a general definition of
a measure of classical channel uncertainty
. Instead of taking the preorder, we can define a
monotone
of the preorder and compare the mapping to the real number. Eventually, we want the monotone to represent an extensive physical quantity, hence requiring an additivity into the monotone, which form a class of monotones called
entropy
of classical channels.

1
Monotones of classical channel majorization

The classical channel majorzation relation is a preorder in the domain of classical channels. Similarly to the case of probability vectors and quantum states, the monotone of the classical channel majorization is a map that preserve the majorization relation between any two classical channels.

Classical channel majorization monotones

Definition 1.1
.

A function
g:\mathsf{ClassicalChannels}\to\mathbb{R}
is said to be a channel majorization monotone if for every classical channel
\mathcal{N}\in\mathrm{CPTP}(X\to Y)
and
\mathcal{M}\in\mathrm{CPTP}(X^{\prime}\to Y^{\prime})

\mathcal{N}\succ\mathcal{M}\implies g(\mathcal{N})\geq g(\mathcal{M}).

(1)

If
-g
is a channel majorization monotone, then
g
is called channel majorization antitone.

Remark
.

The implication is one way, because the
\geq
relation on
\mathbb{R}
is a total order while
\succ
on
\mathsf{ClassicalChannels}
is merely a preorder.

As we have seen in the previous chapter, the winning chance for a
\mathbf{t}
game
\mathbf{Pr}
and the predictability function
\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{N}}
are examples of the classical channel monotones. However, we are interested in a monotone that quantifies an extensive property of classical channel, which require an additional property.

Similarly to the case of states, if
\mathbb{H}
is an antitone and it is additive under tensor product, then it is called an entropy. We write
\mathbb{H}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}
to represent an entropy of the channel
\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}
. The
(Y|X)
part signifies conditioning
Y
on
X
.

Classical Channel entropy

Definition 1.2
.

A function
\mathbb{H}:\mathsf{ClassicalChannels}\to\mathbb{R}
is an entropy of a classical channel if for every two classical channels
\mathcal{N}\in\mathrm{CPTP}(X\to Y)
and
\mathcal{M}\in\mathrm{CPTP}(X^{\prime}\to Y^{\prime})
we have that

1.

\mathbb{H}
is an antitone of channel majorization.

\mathcal{N}\succ\mathcal{M}\implies\mathbb{H}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}\leq\mathbb{H}(Y^{\prime}|X^{\prime})_{\mathcal{M}}

(2)

2.

\mathbb{H}
is additive under parallel composition
\otimes

\mathbb{H}(YY^{\prime}|XX^{\prime})_{\mathcal{N}\otimes\mathcal{M}}=\mathbb{H}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}+\mathbb{H}(Y^{\prime}|X^{\prime})_{\mathcal{M}}

(3)

The additivity property ensures that the entropy is a measure of uncertainty that reflects amount of uncertainty inherent in a classical channel. With additivity, a bipartite channel
\mathcal{P}^{XX^{\prime}\to YY^{\prime}}
that is composed of two local operations,
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}\otimes\mathcal{M}^{X^{\prime}\to Y^{\prime}}
, has its entropy equal to the sum of the entropies of its parts. This allows entropy, as a measure of uncertainty, to reflect a well-defined physical quantity related to a classical channel.

Since the majorization of classical channel reduces to the majorization of probability vectors when the channels are preparation channels, a monotone of channel majorization is Schur-convex on the domain of probability vectors. This also implies that any
\mathbb{H}
entropy of a classical channel
\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}
is bounded below by 0 and is bounded above by
\log(n)
.

2
Extensions of the known entropy to channel domains

There are multiple monotones (Schur-convex function) and entropies on the domain of probability vectors. We take advantage of the existing monotones by extending it to be the monotone of channel majorization.

Definition 2.1
.

Suppose
A\supseteq B
. A function
f:A\to\mathbb{R}
is an
extension
of function
g:B\to\mathbb{R}
to the domain
A
if for all
x\in B
, we have
f(x)=g(x)
.

It has been shown in
[
61
]
that with a given resource monotone
M_{1}
define on a domain
\mathfrak{R}_{1}
. One can extend the monotone onto a domain
\mathfrak{R}\supset\mathfrak{R}_{1}
by the optimal extensions, namely
maximal
extension and
minimal
extension. These two extensions give a bound for any monotone extending
M_{1}
is bounded above and below by the optimal extensions. Similarly, given that we have an entropy function for a probability vector
\mathbb{H}
, we can apply minimal and maximal extensions to define a monotone on the domain of classical channels.
In particular, the maximal extension
\overline{\mathbb{H}}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}
and minimal extension
\underline{\mathbb{H}}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}
of entropy are defined as

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbb{H}}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}

\displaystyle=\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{q}\in\mathrm{Prob}(m)}\\
m\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\quantity{\bb{H}(\q):\cal{N}\succ\q}.

(4)

\displaystyle\underline{\mathbb{H}}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{q}\in\mathrm{Prob}(m)}\\
m\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\quantity{\bb{H}(\q):\q\succ\cal{N}}

(5)

In terms of uncertainty associated with a classical channel
\mathcal{N}
, the minimum extension is an entropy of the most uncertain probability vector that is more certain than the channel
\mathcal{N}
. On another end, the maximum extension is an entropy of the most predictable probability vector that is not more predictable than the channel
\mathcal{N}
. Moreover, any other extension
\mathbb{H}^{\prime}
to the domain of classical channel is bounded above and below by the maximal extension
\overline{\mathbb{H}}
and minimal extensions
\underline{\mathbb{H}}
.

Boundedness of entropy extensions

Theorem 2.2
.

Given a classical channel entropy
\mathbb{H}
. The functions
\overline{\mathbb{H}}
and
\underline{\mathbb{H}}
defined as in equations (
4
) and (
5
) respectively are channel majorization monotones. Moreover, if
\mathbb{H}^{\prime}
is an extension of
\mathbb{H}
onto the classical channel domain, then it is bounded by
\underline{\mathbb{H}}\leq\mathbb{H}^{\prime}\leq\overline{\mathbb{H}}
.

Proof.

First, monotonicity. For the minimal extension, suppose that two classical channel
\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}\succ\mathcal{M}^{X^{\prime}\to Y^{\prime}}
. Suppose further that
\mathbf{q}^{Y}\succ\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}
. By transitivity of majorization preorder, we have that
\mathbf{q}^{Y}\succ\mathcal{M}^{X^{\prime}\to Y^{\prime}}
as well. This show that

\quantity{\q: \cal{N} \succ\q}\subseteq\quantity{\q: \mM\succ\q}.

The supremum of the image of
\quantity{\q: \cal{N} \succ\q}
by
\mathbb{H}
is greater than the supremum of the image of
\quantity{\q: \cal{M} \succ\q}
by
\mathbb{H}
, i.e.

\underline{\mathbb{H}}(\mathcal{N})=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{q}\in\mathrm{Prob}(m)}\\
m\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\quantity{\q:\cal{N}\succ\q}\leq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{q}\in\mathrm{Prob}(m)}\\
m\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\quantity{\q: \mM\succ\q}=\underline{\mathbb{H}}(\mathcal{M}),

showing that
\underline{\mathbb{H}}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}\leq\underline{\mathbb{H}}(Y^{\prime}|X^{\prime})_{\mathcal{M}}
.

Similarly, with the monotonicity of the maximal extension. Suppose
\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}\succ\mathcal{M}^{X^{\prime}\to Y^{\prime}}
. Suppose
\mathcal{M}^{X\to Y}\succ\mathbf{q}^{Y}
. By transitivity, we have that,
\mathcal{N}\succ\mathbf{q}
. Following the similar line of logic from above, we have

\displaystyle\quantity{\q: \q\succ\cal{N}}

\displaystyle\supseteq\quantity{\q: \q\succ\mM}

\displaystyle\implies\quad\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{q}\in\mathrm{Prob}(m)}\\
m\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\quantity{H(\q):\q\succ\cal{N}}

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{q}\in\mathrm{Prob}(m)}\\
m\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\quantity{H(\q) : \q\succ\mM},

showing that
\overline{\mathbb{H}}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}\leq\overline{\mathbb{H}}(Y^{\prime}|X^{\prime})_{\mathcal{M}}
.

For the optimality, since
\mathbb{H}^{\prime}
is a monotone, we have that for any
\mathcal{N}\in\mathsf{ClassicalChannels}
,
\mathbf{p}\in\mathrm{Prob}(m)
, for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\mathbf{p}\succ\mathcal{N}

\mathbb{H}^{\prime}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}\leq\mathbb{H}(\mathbf{p}).

(6)

Then,

\mathbb{H}^{\prime}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}\leq\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{q}\in\mathrm{Prob}(m)}\\
m\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\quantity{\bb{H} (\q) :\q\succ\mN}=\overline{\mathbb{H}}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}

(7)

For
\mathcal{N}\in\mathsf{ClassicalChannels}
,
\mathbf{p}\in\mathrm{Prob}(m)
, for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\mathcal{N}\succ\mathbf{p}
, we have

\mathbb{H}^{\prime}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}\geq\mathbb{H}(\mathbf{p}).

(8)

Then, we complete the proof,

\mathbb{H}^{\prime}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}\geq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{q}\in\mathrm{Prob}(m)}\\
m\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\quantity{\bb{H} (\q) :\mN\succ\q}=\underline{\mathbb{H}}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}.

(9)

∎

We will apply the technique in the upcoming sections. Since we have a characterization of channel majorization in terms of convex set of probability vectors, the extensions can be equivalently defined by optimal bound of a set probability vectors. Specifically, for a probability vector
\mathbf{q}
to majorize a channel
\mathcal{N}
, it needs to majorize all outputs of
\mathcal{N}
, denoted with
\mathcal{N}(\mathrm{Prob}(n))
.

1
Optimal bounds of probability vector majorization

The optimal bounds

Definition 2.3
.

Suppose
A=\quantity{\p_1, \p_2, \ldots, \p_n}
is a set of probability vectors. A vector
\check{\mathbf{p}}
is the optimal upper bound of
A
if the followings hold.

1.

\check{\mathbf{p}}\succ\mathbf{p}_{i}
for any
i\in[n]
.

2.

Any probability vector
\mathbf{q}
such that
\mathbf{q}\succ\mathbf{p}_{i}
for all
i\in[n]
, we have that
\mathbf{q}\succ\vee\mathbf{p}
.

Similarly, a probability vector
\hat{\mathbf{p}}
is the optimal lower bound of
A
if the following holds.

1.

\mathbf{p}_{i}\succ\hat{\mathbf{p}}
for any
i\in[n]
.

2.

Any probability vector
\mathbf{q}
such that
\mathbf{p}_{i}\succ\mathbf{q}
for all
i\in[n]
, we have that
\wedge\mathbf{p}\succ\mathbf{q}
.

Do the optimal upper bound and lower bound always exists? Yes, to see this, we show the existence of the optimal lower bound first. Once the optimal lower bound is established, the optimal upper bound can be given as an optimal lower bound of all upper bound.

Proposition 2.4
.

Given a set of probability vectors
A
, a probability vector
\hat{\mathbf{p}}=(\hat{p}_{1},\hat{p}_{2},\ldots,\hat{p}_{n})^{T}
defined as

\hat{p}_{k}:=\inf_{x\in[m]}\left\|\mathbf{p}_{x}\right\|_{(k)}-\inf_{y\in[m]}\left\|\mathbf{p}_{y}\right\|_{(k-1)}

(10)

is an optimal lower bound of
A
. For
k=1
,
\left\|\mathbf{p}\right\|_{(0)}=0
.

Proof.

Suppose a probability vector
\mathbf{p}_{x}\in A
form a pair
\pqty{\p_x,\vctu^{(n)}}
which a lower Lorenz curve
r_{x}(t)
. By monotonicity of the lower Lorenz curve, we have that if
\left\|\mathbf{p}_{x}\right\|_{(k)}>\left\|\mathbf{p}_{w}\right\|_{(k)}
, then
r_{x}(t)<r_{w}(t)
at least on the interval
[\left\|\mathbf{p}_{w}\right\|_{(k)},\left\|\mathbf{p}_{x}\right\|_{(k)}]
. Then, the lower Lorenz curve of
\pqty{\hat{\p},\vctu^{(n)}}
is
\hat{r}(t)=\sup\quantity{ r(t) = \rm{LLC}(\p,\*{u}^{(n)}): \p\in A }
. Since each
r(t)
is convex, the maximization over all possible
r(t)
is also convex.
This means that
\hat{\mathbf{p}}
is a probability vector in non-increasing order. Consequently, the Ky Fan
k
-norm of
\hat{\mathbf{p}}
is given simply by

\left\|\hat{\mathbf{p}}\right\|_{(k)}=\inf_{x\in[m]}\left\|\mathbf{p}_{x}\right\|_{(k)},

(11)

which is the highest possible Ky Fan norm, otherwise some
\mathbf{p}\in A
does not majorize the vector.
∎

Remark
.

The opposite is not true for the optimal upper bound. A probability vector
\tilde{\mathbf{p}}
whose elements defined to be

\tilde{p}_{k}:=\max_{x\in[m]}\left\|\mathbf{p}_{x}\right\|_{(k)}-\max_{y\in[m]}\left\|\mathbf{p}_{y}\right\|_{(k-1)}

is not be necessary an optimal upper bound. For example, consider a set of
\mathbf{p}_{1}
and
\mathbf{p}_{2}
where

\displaystyle\mathbf{p}_{1}=\pqty{ 0.4, 0.2, 0.2, 0.2}^{T}\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\mathbf{p}_{2}=\pqty{ 0.3, 0.3, 0.3, 0.1}^{T}.

This set of probability vector gives
\tilde{\mathbf{p}}=\frac{1}{10}\pqty{4,2,3,1}
, which is not in non-increasing order. The probability vector
\check{\mathbf{p}}=\frac{1}{10}\pqty{4,2.5,2.5,1}
is not majorizing
\tilde{\mathbf{p}}
,
\check{\mathbf{p}}\not\succ\tilde{\mathbf{p}}
, and it is the optimal upper bound. See Figure
1
for illustration.

In the previous example, we claimed that
\check{\mathbf{p}}
prescribed above is the optimal upper bound. One can give a geometric reasoning from the Figure
1
. The vector
\check{\mathbf{p}}
has all Ky Fan
k
-norm equal to that of
\mathbf{p}_{1}
or
\mathbf{p}_{2}
except at
k=2
where it is strictly higher. This qualifies the curve to define an upper bound of the set
\quantity{\p_1,\p_2}
. On one hand, nudging the point
(2,0.65)
a down will disqualify the drawn line to be a plot of a Ky Fan norm, as it break non-increasing ordering of the slope. Equivalently, it breaks the concavity of the curve. On the other hand, if the point
(2,0.65)
is nudged up, then the probability vector defined by the nudged point cannot be majorized by the given upper bound
\check{\mathbf{p}}
.

\mathbf{p}_{1}
\mathbf{p}_{2}
\check{\mathbf{p}}
Ky Fan
k
-norm
0.3
0.4
0.6
0.8
0.9
1
1
2
3
4
1
k

Figure 1
:
The Ky Fan
k
-norm of
\mathbf{p}_{1}
,
\mathbf{p}_{2}
and the optimal upper bound
\check{\mathbf{p}}
.

Relating this back to the extensions of majorization monotones, we have that an optimal upper bound for the image of
\mathcal{N}
has the entropy equal to the maximal extension entropy of
\mathcal{N}
, i.e.

\mathbb{H}\pqty{\check{\p}}=\underline{\mathbb{H}}\pqty{Y\vert X}_{\mathcal{N}}.

(12)

To see this, consider any
\mathbf{q}\in\mathrm{Prob}(m)
such that
\mathbf{q}\succ\mathbf{p}
for all
\mathbf{p}\in\mathcal{N}(\mathrm{Prob}(n))
. We have that
\mathbf{q}\succ\check{\mathbf{p}}
by the definition of
\check{\mathbf{p}}
being an optimal upper bound. By monotonicity of an entropy with respect to the majorization ordering, the inequality
\mathbb{H}(\check{\mathbf{p}})\geq\mathbb{H}(\mathbf{q})
holds. Moreover, it is enough to check that
\check{p}
majorizes all
\mathbf{p}_{x}=\mathcal{N}\quantity(\ketbra{x}{x})
to conclude majorization over the entire image, because
\check{\mathbf{p}}\succ\mathbf{p}_{i}
and
\check{\mathbf{p}}\succ\mathbf{p}_{j}
implies
\check{\mathbf{p}}\succ\alpha\mathbf{p}_{i}+(1-\alpha)\mathbf{p}_{j}
for all
\alpha\in[0,1]
.

On the other hand, the optimal lower bound for the image of
\mathcal{N}
does not necessary yield the minimal extension entropy. This is because the majorization condition
\mathcal{N}\succ\mathbf{q}
does not require
\mathbf{q}
to be majorized by all elements in the image of
\mathcal{N}
.

2
Entropies from extensions

These extensions are only guaranteed to be a monotone and reduced to entropy on state but not necessary are additive. On some class of function, we find that its extension is additive. A quasiconcave entropy, e.g. Rényi entropies, minimally extends to an entropy function.

Maximal extension of a Rényi entropy

Theorem 2.5
.

Suppose
\mathcal{N}
is a classical channel,
\mathbb{H}
is a quasiconcave classical entropy, then the maximal extension of the entropy is

\overline{\mathbb{H}}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}=\min_{x\in[m]}\mathbb{H}(\mathbf{p}_{x})

(13)

where
\mathbf{p}_{x}\coloneqq\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{e}_{x})
and
\overline{\mathbb{H}}
is a classical channel entropy.

Remark
.

The entropy function defined as above is called minimal entropy output. It is first purposed by
[
65
]
.

Proof.

By definition the maximal extension is
\overline{H}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}=\inf\quantity{\bb{H}(\q): \cal{N}\succ\q}
. A probability
\mathbf{q}
satisfies
\mathcal{N}\succ\mathbf{q}
if and only if
\exists\mathbf{s}=(s_{x})\in\mathrm{Prob}(m)~\text{s.t.}~\sum_{x\in[m]}s_{x}\mathbf{p}_{x}\succ\mathbf{q}
. In terms of the entropy function,

\mathbb{H}\pqty{\sum_{x\in[m]} s_x \p_x}\leq\mathbb{H}(\mathbf{q})

(14)

If
\mathbb{H}
is quasiconcave, then any point in the convex hull of
\mathrm{Conv}\quantity{\p_x}
is greater than the minimum of
\mathbf{p}_{x}
, that is

\min_{x\in[m]}\mathbb{H}(\mathbf{p}_{x})\leq\mathbb{H}\pqty{\sum_{x\in[m]} s_x \p_x}\leq\mathbb{H}(\mathbf{q}).

(15)

This shows that the entropy of
\mathbf{q}
is at least the minimal of the
\mathcal{N}
’s outputs. To show that this value is attainable, pick
x_{0}
to be the argument of the minimum
\mathbb{H}(\mathbf{p}_{x_{0}})
, then define
\mathbf{s}=\mathbf{e}_{x_{0}}
shows that
\mathcal{N}\succ\mathbf{p}_{x_{0}}
.

Left to show is the additivity of the function, consider two channels
\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}
and
\mathcal{M}^{X^{\prime}\to Y^{\prime}}
. Set
m=\absolutevalue{X},m^{\prime}=\absolutevalue{X'},Z=X\otimes Y
, its max-entropy is

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbb{H}}(YY^{\prime}|XX^{\prime})_{\mathcal{N}\otimes\mathcal{M}}

\displaystyle=\min_{z\in[mm^{\prime}]}\mathbb{H}(\mathcal{N}\otimes\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{e}^{Z}_{z}))

(16)

\displaystyle=\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in[m]\\
x^{\prime}\in[m^{\prime}]\end{subarray}}\mathbb{H}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{e}^{X}_{x})\otimes\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{e}^{X^{\prime}}_{x^{\prime}}))

(17)

\displaystyle=\min_{x\in[m]}\mathbb{H}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{e}^{X}_{x}))+\min_{x^{\prime}\in[m^{\prime}]}\mathbb{H}(\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{e}^{X^{\prime}}_{x^{\prime}}))

(18)

\displaystyle=\overline{\mathbb{H}}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}+\overline{\mathbb{H}}(Y^{\prime}|X^{\prime})_{\mathcal{M}}.

(19)

This shows than a maximum extension of a quasiconcave entropy (e.g. Rényi entropies) is an entropy for classical channels.
∎

For example, the maximal extension of the min entropy is

\displaystyle\overline{H}_{\mathrm{min}}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}

\displaystyle=\min_{x\in[m]}-\log( \max_{w\in[n]} \qty{(\p_x)_w} )

(20)

\displaystyle=-\log\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in[m]\\
w\in[n]\end{subarray}}N_{wx}

(21)

If
\mathcal{N}
is in the standard form, then the transition matrix element
N_{wx}
that is the biggest is
N_{11}
.

\overline{H}_{\mathrm{min}}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}=-\log(N_{11}).

The maximal extension of the max entropy is

\displaystyle\overline{H}_{\mathrm{max}}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}=\log\min_{x\in[m]}\absolutevalue{\rm{supp}(\p_x)}

(22)

In fact, there is no other way to extend max-entropy or min-entropy. Any function that extends
H_{\mathrm{min}}
or
H_{\mathrm{max}}
to the domain of classical channel has the expression equivalent to that in Equation (
21
) or Equation (
22
).

Uniqueness of min-entropy and max-entropies extensions

Theorem 2.6
.

The extensions of max-entropy and min-entropy to the domain of classical channels are unique.

Proof.

For the min-entropy, the minimal extension of the min-entropy is

\displaystyle\underline{H}_{\mathrm{min}}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}

\displaystyle=\sup\quantity{- \log\pqty{\max_{x\in[m]} q_x}: \q\succ\cal{N}}

Suppose
\mathcal{N}
is in the standard form. Define
k_{0}=\lfloor\frac{1}{N_{11}}\rfloor
, define a probability vector

\mathbf{q}=(\underbrace{N_{11},\ldots,N_{11}}_{k_{0}~\mathrm{elements}},1-k_{0}N_{11})^{T}.

We will show that
\mathbf{q}\succ\mathcal{N}
. Since
\mathbf{p}_{x}
is in non-increasing order and
\mathcal{N}
is in the standard form, we have
N_{11}\geq N_{1j}
for any
j\in[n]
,
\mathbf{p}_{x}^{\downarrow}
for all
x\in[n]
, and for all
k\leq k_{0}
,

\left\|\mathbf{p}_{x}\right\|_{(k)}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}p_{x,i}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k}N_{11}=\left\|\mathbf{q}\right\|_{(k)}.

For
k>k_{0}
,
\left\|\mathbf{q}\right\|_{(k)}=1
by definition. Therefore,
\mathbf{q}\succ\mathbf{p}_{x}
for all
x\in[n]
and, consequently,
\mathbf{q}\succ\mathcal{N}
.
Going back to the minimal extension of the min-entropy, we have an inequality

\underline{H}_{\mathrm{min}}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}\geq-\log(N_{11})=\overline{H}_{\mathrm{min}}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}.

From the theorem, Theorem
2.2
, we have that the maximal and minimal extensions are identical

\underline{H}_{\mathrm{min}}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}=\overline{H}_{\mathrm{min}}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}=-\log(N_{11}).

For the max-entropy, the minimal extension of the max-entropy is

\displaystyle\underline{H}_{\mathrm{max}}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}

\displaystyle=\sup\quantity{\log\pqty{\abs{\rm{supp}(\q)}}: \q\succ\cal{N}}

We will give an element
\mathbf{q}
of the above set which has support equal to the minimum support of
\mathbf{p}_{x}
over all
x\in[n]
. Suppose the minimum support of
\mathbf{p}_{x}
is
k_{0}
. Recall that the Ky Fan norm is increasing with
k
. If there is
\mathbf{q}
such that
\left\|\mathbf{q}\right\|_{(1)}=\max_{x\in[n]}\left\|\mathbf{p}_{x}\right\|_{(k_{0}-1)}
, then
\left\|\mathbf{q}\right\|_{(k)}\geq\left\|\mathbf{q}\right\|_{(1)}\geq\left\|\mathbf{p}_{x}\right\|_{(k^{\prime})}
for all
k,k^{\prime}\in[k_{0}-1]
.

Suppose
\left\|\mathbf{p}_{z}\right\|_{(k_{0}-1)}=\max_{x\in[n]}\left\|\mathbf{p}_{x}\right\|_{(k_{0}-1)}
, define
\mathbf{q}=(\left\|\mathbf{p}_{z}\right\|_{(k_{0}-1)},q_{2},\ldots,q_{k_{0}},\ldots,q_{n})
where
q_{i}=(1-q_{1})/(k_{0}-1)
for all
i\in[2:k_{0}]
and
q_{j}=0
for
j>k_{0}
. This
\mathbf{q}
is a probability vector in non-increasing order, has a cardinality of the support equal to
k_{0}
and
\mathbf{q}\succ\mathbf{p}_{x}
for all
x\in[n]
. That is, we have an inequality

\underline{H}_{\mathrm{max}}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}\geq\log\absolutevalue{\rm{supp}(\q)}=\log\min_{x\in[m]}\absolutevalue{\rm{supp}(\p_x)}=\overline{H}_{\mathrm{max}}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}.

From Theorem
2.2
, we have that
\underline{H}_{\mathrm{max}}\leq\overline{H}_{\mathrm{max}}
. Therefore, extension of the max-entropy is unique, i.e

\underline{H}_{\mathrm{max}}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}=\overline{H}_{\mathrm{max}}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}=\log\min_{x\in[m]}\absolutevalue{\rm{supp}(\p_x)}.

∎

3
Regularization and uniqueness of extensions

Regularization of a classical channel majorization monotone

Definition 2.7
.

Suppose
\mathbb{H}:\mathsf{ClassicalChannels}\to\mathbb{R}
is a classical channel majorization monotone. Its regularization is defined as

\mathbb{H}^{\mathrm{reg}}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}:=\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{H}(Y^{k}|X^{k})_{\mathcal{N}^{\otimes k}}

(23)

Remark
.

The limit in above definition is not guaranteed to exist in general. In a resource theory that admit tensor product structure, the optimal extensions of monotone are sub-additive and super-additive. By Fekete’s subadditive lemma, the limit exists.

If we apply the regularization to inequality in the Theorem
2.2
, we have that

\underline{\mathbb{H}}^{\mathrm{reg}}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}\leq\mathbb{H}^{\mathrm{reg}}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}\leq\overline{\mathbb{H}}^{\mathrm{reg}}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}.

If
\mathbb{H}
is an entropy, i.e. it is additive, then the regularized
\mathbb{H}^{\mathrm{reg}}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}
is exactly the entropy
\mathbb{H}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}
. That is we have, for
\mathbb{H}
a channel entropy,

\underline{\mathbb{H}}^{\mathrm{reg}}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}\leq\mathbb{H}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}\leq\overline{\mathbb{H}}^{\mathrm{reg}}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}.

(24)

This simple result will help us show the uniqueness of a channel entropy that extends Shannon entropy. In particular, the extension in Theorem
2.5
provide a unique extension of the Shannon entropy to the classical channel majorization.

Uniqueness of the channel entropy extending Shannon entropy

Theorem 2.8
.

Suppose
\mathbb{H}
be a classical channel entropy. If
\mathbb{H}
extends Shannon entropy, then it is the maximal extension, i.e.

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}\pqty{Y\vert X}_{\mathcal{N}}

\displaystyle=\underline{H}^{\mathrm{reg}}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}=\overline{H}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}=\min_{x\in[m]}H(\mathbf{p}_{x}).

Remark
.

The statement of this is weaker than the statement with min-entropy and max-entropy. It does not state the uniqueness for a monotone that extends Shannon entropy but is not additive.

Remark

(2)
.

The proof of this theorem follows the same spirit as the uniqueness of a channel divergence extending KL-divergence in
[
45
]
.

We are working toward the proof of this theorem, which requires the following lemma.

Lemma 2.9
.

Suppose
\mathbf{p}\in\mathrm{Prob}(n)
, and
0<\varepsilon,\delta<1
. There exists an integer
m
such that for all integers
k\geq m
,

\mathbf{r}_{k}=\pqty{\delta, \underbrace{2^{-k(H(\p) - \varepsilon)},\ldots,2^{-k(H(\p) - \varepsilon)}}_{c_k -\rm{times}},s_k}\succ\mathbf{p}^{\otimes k},

where
c_{k}=\lfloor(1-\delta)2^{k(H(\mathbf{p})-\varepsilon)}\rfloor
and
s_{k}
is a remainder of the division
(1-\delta)/2^{-k(H(\mathbf{p})-\varepsilon)}
.

Proof.

For any probability vector
\mathbf{p}\in\mathrm{Prob}(n)
, its tensor product
\mathbf{p}^{\otimes k}
describes a probability of obtaining a sequence
\mathbf{X}=X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{k}
where each
X_{i}
is drawn from the same distribution
\mathbf{p}
independently. The vector
\mathbf{p}^{\otimes k}
is equivalent with any permutation, so we have

\mathbf{p}^{\otimes k}\sim\bigoplus_{\mathbf{x}\in[n]^{k}}p_{\mathbf{x}}

where
\mathbf{x}
is a sequence of the values of
X_{1},\ldots,X_{k}
and
p_{\mathbf{x}}
is a probability of the sequence
\mathbf{x}
drawn.

Next, we merge all the entries that are not
\varepsilon
-typical into an element. This results in

\mathbf{p}^{\otimes k}\sim\bigoplus_{\mathbf{x}\in[n]^{k}}p_{\mathbf{x}}\prec\mathrm{P}(\mathbf{X}\not\in A^{(k)}_{\varepsilon})\oplus\bigoplus_{\mathbf{x}\in A^{(k)}_{\varepsilon}}p_{\mathbf{x}}

(25)

where
A^{(k)}_{\varepsilon}
denotes a set of all
\varepsilon
-typical sequences of length
k
. The majorization relation above can be seen quickly by applying finite number of
T
-transform mapping the elements
\mathrm{P}(\mathbf{X}\not\in A^{(k)}_{\varepsilon})
back to the original in
\mathbf{p}^{\otimes k}
.

We will use the transitivity of majorization relation to show that the right side of the Equation (
25
) is majorized by
\mathbf{r}_{k}
. First, by the application of the weak law of large number on i.i.d. sequence (see Theorem
6.A.9
), for any
\delta>0
, there exists a
k_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
k\geq k_{0}
, we have

\mathrm{P}(\mathbf{X}\in A^{(k)}_{\varepsilon})>1-\delta,

or equivalently

\mathrm{P}(\mathbf{X}\not\in A^{(k)}_{\varepsilon})<\delta.

For any
\mathbf{x}\in A^{(k)}_{\varepsilon}
, the probability of drawing the sequence
\mathbf{x}
is bounded,

p_{\mathbf{x}}\leq 2^{-k(H(\mathbf{p})-\varepsilon)}.

Two equations above give a sufficient condition for

\mathbf{r}_{k}\succ\mathrm{P}(\mathbf{X}\not\in A^{(k)}_{\varepsilon})\oplus\bigoplus_{\mathbf{x}\in A^{(k)}_{\varepsilon}}p_{\mathbf{x}}\succ\mathbf{p}^{\otimes k}.

∎

Now, we are ready to proof the uniqueness theorem.

Proof of Theorem
2.8
.

The minimal extension of the Shannon entropy
\underline{H}(Y^{k}|X^{k})_{\mathcal{N}^{\otimes k}}
is the entropy of
\check{\mathbf{p}}
the optimal upper bound of a set of probability vectors
\quantity{\bigotimes_{x\in\x} \p_x\quad\forall\x\in[m]^k}
. Such bound can be assumed to have the same dimension, i.e.
\check{\mathbf{p}}\in\mathrm{Prob}(mk)
. We have

\displaystyle\underline{H}(Y^{k}|X^{k})_{\mathcal{N}^{\otimes k}}

\displaystyle=\sup\quantity{H(\q): \q\succ\bigotimes_{x\in\x} \p_x\quad\forall\x\in[m]^k, \q\in\prob(mk)}

\displaystyle=\sup\quantity{H(\q): \q\succ\bigotimes_{x\in[m]} \p_x ^{\otimes kt_x}\quad\forall\t\in\bb{T}_{m,k},\q\in\prob(mk)},

where
\mathbb{T}_{m,k}
is a set of all types
\mathbf{t}
of length-
k
sequence with possible
m
alphabets.

Define
k_{x}=kt_{x}
. Define for all
x\in[m]
and
\varepsilon,\delta\in(0,1)
,

\overline{\mathbf{p}}^{(k_{x})}_{x}:=\pqty{\delta, r_2, \ldots, r_{c_x}, s'_x},

where
c_{x}=\lfloor(1-\delta)2^{k_{x}(H(\mathbf{p}_{x}))}\rfloor
, for all
i\in[2:c_{x}]
, we define
r_{i}=2^{-k_{x}(H(\mathbf{p}_{x}))-\varepsilon}
, and
s^{\prime}_{x}=1-\delta-c_{x}2^{-k_{x}(H(\mathbf{p}_{x}))}
. We use Lemma
2.9
,
\mathbf{p}^{\otimes k_{x}}_{x}\prec\overline{\mathbf{p}}^{(k_{x})}_{x}

Furthermore, suppose
H(\mathbf{p}_{z})=\min_{x\in[m]}H(\mathbf{p}_{x})
. We have that

\mathbf{v}_{x}:=\pqty{\delta, v_2, \ldots, v_{d_x}, s _x}

where
d_{x}=\lfloor(1-\delta)2^{k_{x}(H(\mathbf{p}_{z})-\varepsilon)}\rfloor
, for all
i\in[2:d_{x}]
, we define
v_{i}=2^{-k_{x}(H(\mathbf{p}_{z})-\varepsilon)}
, and
s_{x}=1-\delta-d_{x}2^{-k_{x}(H(\mathbf{p}_{z})-\varepsilon)}
. We have
\mathbf{v}_{x}\succ\overline{\mathbf{p}}_{x}^{(k_{x})}\succ\mathbf{p}^{\otimes k_{x}}_{x}
. That is

\underline{H}(Y^{k}|X^{k})_{\mathcal{N}}\geq H(\mathbf{v}_{1}\otimes\mathbf{v}_{2}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathbf{v}_{m}).

Using additiviy of Shannon entropy,

H(\mathbf{v}_{1}\otimes\mathbf{v}_{2}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathbf{v}_{m})=\sum_{x\in[m]}H(\mathbf{v}_{x})=-m\delta\log(\delta)-\sum_{x\in[m]}s_{x}\log(s_x)+d_{x}2^{-k_{x}(H(\mathbf{p}_{z})-\varepsilon)}k_{x}(H(\mathbf{p}_{z})-\varepsilon).

Notice that, at each
k
and each
x
,

d_{x}=\left\lfloor\frac{1-\delta}{2^{-k_{x}(H(\mathbf{p})-\varepsilon)}}\right\rfloor=\frac{1-\delta}{2^{-k_{x}(H(\mathbf{p}_{z})-\varepsilon)}}-\frac{g^{(k)}_{x}}{2^{-k_{x}(H(\mathbf{p}_{z})-\varepsilon)}}

for some
g^{(k)}_{x}\in[0,2^{-k_{x}(H(\mathbf{p}_{z})-\varepsilon)})
. Taking the limit
k\to\infty
,
2^{-k_{x}(H(\mathbf{p}_{z})-\varepsilon)}\to 0
and
g^{(k)}\to 0
. We have

\lim_{k\to\infty}d_{x}2^{-k_{x}(H(\mathbf{p}_{z})-\varepsilon)}=\lim_{k\to\infty}\left\lfloor\frac{1-\delta}{2^{-k_{x}(H(\mathbf{p}_{z})-\varepsilon)}}\right\rfloor 2^{-k_{x}(H(\mathbf{p}_{z})-\varepsilon)}=1-\delta.

We have

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{k}\sum_{x\in[m]}s_{x}\log s_{x}

\displaystyle=\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{k}\sum_{x\in[m]}\pqty{1-\delta- d_x 2^{-k_x (H(\p_z)-\varepsilon)}}\log\pqty{1-\delta- d_x 2^{-k_x (H(\p_z)-\varepsilon)}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{g^{(k)}_{x}}{k}\log(g^{(k)} _x)=0

Now, we are ready to apply these results into the regularized maximally extended entropy,

\displaystyle\underline{H}^{\mathrm{reg}}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{k}\underline{H}(Y^{k}|X^{k})_{\mathcal{N}^{\otimes k}}

\displaystyle\geq\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{k}H(\mathbf{v}_{1}\otimes\ldots\mathbf{v}_{m})

\displaystyle=\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{k_{x}}{k}\pqty{H(\p_z) - \varepsilon}d_{x}2^{-k_{x}(H(\mathbf{p}_{z})-\varepsilon)}

\displaystyle=\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{k_{x}}{k}\pqty{H(\p_z) - \varepsilon}(1-\delta-g^{(k)}_{x})

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in[m]}t_{x}(H(\mathbf{p}_{z})-\varepsilon)(1-\delta)

\displaystyle=(H(\mathbf{p}_{z})-\varepsilon)(1-\delta).

Since the above equations hold for all
\epsilon,\delta\in(0,1)
, we have an inequality

\underline{H}^{\mathrm{reg}}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}\geq H(\mathbf{p}_{z})=\max_{x\in[n]}H(\mathbf{p}_{x})=\overline{H}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}.

(26)

The bound in the inequality (
24
), leads to

\underline{H}^{\mathrm{reg}}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}\leq\mathbb{H}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}\leq\overline{H}^{\mathrm{reg}}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}=\overline{H}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}=\max_{x\in[n]}H(\mathbf{p}_{x}).

Notice that
\overline{H}^{\mathrm{reg}}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}=\overline{H}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}
because the maximal extension of Shannon entropy is additive. Together with the inequality (
26
), we have

\mathbb{H}\pqty{Y\vert X}_{\mathcal{N}}=\underline{H}^{\mathrm{reg}}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}=\overline{H}(Y|X)_{\mathcal{N}}=\min_{x\in[m]}H(\mathbf{p}_{x}).

(27)

∎

3
Channel entropy from divergences

Other than extending entropy from the known classical state entropy, we can define a classical channel entropy from the divergence of a quantum channel. The divergence-defined channel entropy relates to the degree of indistinguishability of classical channels. In this section, we present two previously proposed divergence-defined channel entropies and their relations to our axiomatic definition of channel entropy.

1
Divergence from the maximally randomizing channel

This class of channel entropies appears in multiple literature, to the best of my knowledge Gour and Wilde
[
45
]
are the first proposed this.

Given
{D}
is a divergence of a pair of classical states. Define a channel divergence
D
as

D(\mathcal{N}\lVert\mathcal{M})=\sup_{\mathbf{q}\in\mathrm{Prob}(m)}D(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{p})\lVert\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{p})).

A channel entropy of
\mathcal{N}^{X\to Y}
is defined from a distance from a maximally randomizing channel,

H(\mathcal{N})=\log_{2}(\absolutevalue{Y})-D(\mathcal{N}\lVert\mathcal{R}).

There are two important properties: being an antitone of channel majorization and is additive (if
D
is additive). The first property follows from a completely uniformity-preserving superchannel is uniformity-preserving as well and that
D
satisfies DPI, we can arrive at
H(\mathcal{N})\leq H(\Theta[\mathcal{N}])
.

2
Divergence from a bipartite quantum channel

In
[
66
]
, the author purposed a set of axiom for a function to be called conditional entropy of a quantum channel. Their purpose entropy is called
generalized conditional entropy
. The conditional entropy of a channel
\mathcal{N}^{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}\to AB}
is defined as

\mathbb{H}(A|B)_{\mathcal{N}}=\log\absolutevalue{A}-\inf_{\mathcal{M}\in\mathrm{CPTP}(B^{\prime}\to B)}\mathbb{D}(\mathcal{N}\lVert\mathcal{R}^{A^{\prime}\to A}\otimes\mathcal{M}^{B^{\prime}\to B}).

(28)

This definition of entropy is shown to reduce to an entropy of quantum channel when the input channel is a product bipartite channel, e.g.
\mathcal{N}^{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}\to AB}=\mathcal{N}_{1}^{A^{\prime}\to A}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{2}^{B^{\prime}\to B}
. From the example,

\mathbb{H}(A|B)_{\mathcal{N}}=\mathbb{H}(\mathcal{N}_{1}^{A^{\prime}\to A})=\log\absolutevalue{A}-\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{N}|\mathcal{R}).

This definition of conditional entropy is monotone under an action of a completely uniformity-preserving superchannel. Suppose that
\mathcal{N},\mathcal{M}\in\mathrm{CPTP}(A^{\prime}B^{\prime}\to AB)
,
\Theta
is a superchannel,
\Theta\in\mathrm{SUPER}((A\to B))
, and
\Theta\otimes\mathsf{id}[\mathcal{N}]=\mathcal{M}
. Consider the divergence

\displaystyle\inf_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathrm{CPTP}(B^{\prime}\to B)}\mathbb{D}(\mathcal{M}\|\mathcal{R}\otimes\mathcal{P})

\displaystyle=\inf_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathrm{CPTP}(B^{\prime}\to B)}\mathbb{D}(\Theta\otimes\mathsf{id}[\mathcal{N}]\|\Theta\otimes\mathsf{id}[\mathcal{R}\otimes\mathcal{P}])

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathrm{CPTP}(B^{\prime}\to B)}\mathbb{D}(\mathcal{N}\|\mathcal{R}\otimes\mathcal{P}).

With this inequality, we have monotonicity, for any
\mathcal{N}\in\mathrm{CPTP}(A^{\prime}B^{\prime}\to AB)
and
\Theta
completely uniformity-preserving,

\mathbb{H}(A|B)_{\mathcal{N}}\leq\mathbb{H}(A|B)_{\Theta[\mathcal{N}]}.

Notice that with above derivation of the inequality, we require only that
\Theta
preserve a maximally randomizing channel
\mathcal{R}
. Therefore, this definition of quantum channel conditional entropy reduces to the entropy of channel defined on the first section.

With all of these, we can define an entropy of a unipartite channel
\mathcal{N}^{A^{\prime}\to A}
to be a conditional entropy of a product of the channel with any channel in
\mathrm{CPTP}(B^{\prime}\to B)
. It satisfies monotonicity and additivity.

In the same work
[
66
]
, the author define a non-signalling quantum conditional entropy to be a divergence of a channel
\mathcal{N}^{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}\to AB}
from
\mathcal{R}^{A\to A}\circ\mathcal{N}^{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}\to AB}
. Instead, we can take the infimum on the second argument, we define a channel entropy to be

\mathbb{H}(\mathcal{N})=\log(A)-\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{P}\in\mathrm{CPTP}(A^{\prime}B^{\prime}\to B)\\
B,B^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{D}(\mathcal{N}^{A^{\prime}\to A}\otimes\mathsf{id}^{B^{\prime}\to B}\|\mathbf{u}^{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}^{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}\to B}).

(29)

This definition of a quantum channel entropy from a quantum channel relative entropy satisfies monotonicity under completely uniformity preserving and is additive. The proof is similar to that of the generalized conditional entropy being a channel entropy.

We have no positive or negative result if the function coincide with the entropy defined by the divergence from maximally randomizing channel. However, specifically for the Umegaki relative entropy, we have that the channel entropy as defined in the Equation (
28
), and in the Equation (
29
) are coincided. This is due to a theorem in
[
66
]
.

Proposition 3.1
.

For a quantum channel
\mathcal{N}^{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}\to AB}
, its von Neumann conditional entropy
H(A|B)_{\mathcal{N}}
(as defined in Equation (
28
)) satisfies the following identity,

H(A|B)_{\mathcal{N}}=\log(A)-\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{P}\in\mathrm{CPTP}(A^{\prime}B^{\prime}\to B)\\
B,B^{\prime}\end{subarray}}D(\mathcal{N}^{A^{\prime}\to A}\otimes\mathsf{id}^{B^{\prime}\to B}\|\mathbf{u}^{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}^{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}\to B})

if and only if
\mathcal{N}^{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}\to AB}
is non-signalling from
A^{\prime}\to B
. Here
D
is Umegaki relative entropy defined as

D(\rho\lVert\sigma)=\Tr[\rho\log(\rho)]-\Tr[\rho\log(\sigma)].

The proof of this statement use an identity

D(\rho^{AB}\|I^{A}\otimes\sigma^{B})=D(\rho^{AB}\|I^{A}\otimes\rho^{B})+D(\rho^{B}\|\sigma^{B}),

which is specific to the Umegaki relative entropy.

Suppose the proposition is true. Since
\mathcal{N}^{A^{\prime}\to A}\otimes\mathsf{id}^{B\to B}
is non-signalling, then the entropy as defined in Equation (
28
) coincide with that as defined in Equation (
29
).

4
Channel entropy from Choi isomorphism

In
[
67
,
68
]
, the authors purposed a channel entropy that can be defined from the Choi matrix from Choi–Jamiołkowski isomorphism. They showed that this function satisfies all properties put forward from
[
50
]
, which is similar to ours but replace the monotonicity with completely uniformity-preserving with monotonicity under random-unitary superchannel, a superchannel which realizable with a unitary pre-processing channel and a post-processing channel and has no auxiliary system. However, this difference leads to incompatibility with our definition as a random-unitary superchannel is a strict subset of completely uniformity-preserving.

Suppose that
\mathcal{N}\in\mathrm{CPTP}(X\to Y)
. We define
J_{\mathcal{N}}\in\mathfrak{L}(XY)
to be its Choi matrix and
\hat{J}_{\mathcal{N}}
to be the normalized Choi matrix. A function
f
of
N
is defined by

f(\mathcal{N})=H\pqty{\hat{J}_\cal{N}}-\log(\abs{X})

where
H
is a von Neumann entropy. The function
f
is purposed to be an entropy function. The key property that the author showed in their work is that
f
is antitone with respect to a random-unitary superchannel, which is a superchannel
\Theta
of the form

\Theta[\mathcal{N}^{A\to B}]=\sum_{x\in[\ell]}p_{x}\mathcal{U}_{x}^{B\to B^{\prime}}\circ\mathcal{N}^{A\to B}\circ\mathcal{V}_{x}^{A^{\prime}\to A}

where
\mathcal{U}_{x}
and
\mathcal{V}_{x}
are unitary channels,
\sum_{x\in[\ell]}p_{x}=1
, and
p_{x}\geq 0
.

This function is antitone with respect to a random-unitary superchannel. To see this, consider

\displaystyle f(\Theta[\mathcal{N}])

\displaystyle=H(\hat{J}_{\Theta[\mathcal{N}]})-\log(\abs{X})

\displaystyle=H(\Delta_{\Theta}(\hat{J}_{\mathcal{N}}))-\log(\abs{X})

\displaystyle\textrm{($\Delta_{\Theta}$ is unital)}\rightarrow

\displaystyle\geq H(\hat{J}_{\mathcal{N}})-\log(\abs{X})=f(\mathcal{N}).

However, this function is not antitone with respect to the completely-uniformity preserving.

For Shannon entropy, we know that its entropy extension to the domain of classical channel is unique and is a minimum entropy output. However, this purposed formulation of entropy doesn’t suggest that it would coincide with the minimum entropy output, even when restrict the domain to classical channels. It turns out that this function is not a quantum channel entropy. To see this, we consider a classical channel counter example. Suppose

N=\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}\quad M=\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}

Since
\mathcal{N}\sim\mathcal{M}
, any classical channel entropy
H
must have the two channel agree on
H(\mathcal{N})=H(\mathcal{M})
. The entropy of a Choi matrix does not satisfy this property. The entropy of both channel is
0
as measure by any channel entropies. For the channel
\mathcal{M}
, its Choi is

\displaystyle\hat{J}_{\mathcal{M}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\outerproduct{1}{1}^{X}\otimes\outerproduct{1}{1}^{Y}+\frac{1}{2}\outerproduct{2}{2}^{X}\otimes\frac{1}{2}(\outerproduct{1}{1}^{Y}+\outerproduct{2}{2}^{Y})=\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}.

A von Neumann entropy for this state is

H(\hat{J}_{\mathcal{M}})=-\frac{1}{2}\log\pqty{1/2}-\frac{1}{2}\log\pqty{1/4}=\frac{3}{2}\log(2).

This gives the function
f
on
\mathcal{N}
to be

f(\mathcal{N})=\frac{3}{2}\log(2)-\log(2)=\frac{1}{2}\log(2)=\frac{1}{2}\neq 0.

To see why we have this missed matching of the value of two equivalent channels, consider the proof that
\Delta_{\Theta}
is unital channel if
\Theta
is a random-unitary channel.

Lemma 4.1
.

If
\Theta\in\mathrm{SUPER}((A\to B))
is random-unitary, then the induced map on Choi matrix
\Delta_{\Theta}\in\mathrm{CPTP}(AB)
is unital.

Proof.

For the maximally randomizing channel
\mathcal{R}\in\mathrm{CPTP}(A\to B)
, we have that

\Theta[\mathcal{R}^{A\to B}]=\mathcal{R}^{A\to B}.

The Choi matrix is

\displaystyle J[\Theta[\mathcal{R}]]

\displaystyle=J[\mathcal{R}]=\mathbf{u}^{AB}

\displaystyle J[\Theta[\mathcal{R}]]=\Delta_{\Theta}(J[\mathcal{R}])

\displaystyle=\Delta_{\Theta}(\mathbf{u}^{AB})=\mathbf{u}^{AB},

showing that
\Delta_{\Theta}
is unital.
∎

This leads to the answer of why we see missed match in entropy value between channel-majorization equivalent channels. The induced map
\Delta_{\Theta}
is not necessary unital if
\Theta
is completely uniformity-preserving.
From the counter example, the induced map of a superchannel transforming
\mathcal{N}
to
\mathcal{M}
cannot be unital, since it is a mapping between two different dimensions of physics systems.

Chapter 5
Conclusion and outlook

Conclusion

In this thesis, we give a definite notion of classical channel uncertainty. We propose that the exact notion of uncertainty inherent in the channel can be characterized from three perspectives. From the construction perspective, uncertainties of two classical channels are compared by an ability to reconstruct the more uncertain one with random permutations of the outcomes. In the axiomatic approach, the uncertainty of a classical channel is proposed to be preserved or increased under transformations by a specific type of superchannel, namely completely uniformity-preserving superchannel. The operational task we proposed to characterize uncertainty is a family of
\mathbf{t}
-games, where a player strategically sends an input through a classical channel to best predict the outcome of the channel. These three conceptually distinct propositions of uncertainty of classical channels are proven, in this thesis, to induce the same ordering between classical channels, establishing a unique and conceptually as well as operationally meaningful preorder reflecting uncertainty inherent in a classical channel.

With the firm foundation of majorization, we approach defining the most general class of functions evaluating uncertainty inherent in classical channels, namely monotones. We require that a monotone must behave monotonically with the majorization relation. With the additional requirement of additivity under the tensor product of channels, we derive the definition of classical channel
entropy
. We give examples of such functions by extending the existing classical state entropies into the domain of classical channels. Specifically, we show that the extensions of min-entropy and max-entropy to the classical channel domain are entropies. Moreover, with Shannon entropy, the extension that results in an entropy is uniquely identified with the maximal extension, namely the min-entropy output. In addition, the axiomatic definition of classical channel entropy disqualifies the entropy of Choi of a channel from being a valid classical channel entropy.

In complement to majorization of classical channels, we prescribe the standard form as a unique representative of classical channels in each equivalence class. We characterized the majorization relation in terms of majorization of the outputs of channels, convex set containment, predictability function, optimization problems, and, most importantly, conditional majorization. These characterizations offer perspectives and insights into the uncertainty of classical channels.

Outlook

One of the natural questions to ask is the extension to the quantum domain. Starting with the notion of majorization itself, would this notion of majorization and entropy of classical channel extend to the quantum domain? The answer is presented in
[
1
]
and the answer was partially affirmative; the axiomatic and constructive extend naturally, while the game of chance does not have a direct analogue in the quantum regime. This leaves the door open for further study of the operational approach to characterize quantum channel uncertainty. On entropy, quantum channel entropy can be defined as an additive monotone on quantum channel majorization. It is interesting to see if the extensions of entropy or classical channel entropy to the domain of quantum channel are unique.

Even on the classical channel domain, this thesis answered many questions and opened many new ones. To start with, what information-theoretic task that the channel majorization play a role in? We see in this thesis that the family of
t
-games is directly tied to the majorization of classical channels. Specifying the game could enable to simulate an informational theoretic protocol such as in cryptographic and communication tasks.

The foundation of classical channel majorization seems to suggest a path to
conditional channel entropy
. Suppose there is a classical bipartite conditionally uniform channel
\mathcal{P}^{XX^{\prime}\to YY^{\prime}}(\mathbf{p}^{XX^{\prime}})=\mathbf{u}^{Y}\otimes\mathbf{q}^{Y^{\prime}}
for any
\mathbf{p}\in\mathrm{Prob}(\absolutevalue{XX'})
. A completely uniform-preserving superchannel ensures that, even when accessing information on the output of the channel
Y^{\prime}
, the
conditional
probability distribution on
Y
remains maximally mixed. The further analysis in this venue could yield foundational, insightful results.

For the measure of channel uncertainty, it is shown in the thesis that the extension of min-entropy and max-entropy is unique and results in classical channel entropies. The questions remain in the operational interpretation of which one could extend from the work of
[
69
]
. The thesis also showed that any classical entropy that extends Shannon entropy can be nothing other than the maximal extension. This is a strong statement, but it still leaves room to wonder if there is a non-additive monotone that extends Shannon entropy but is not the maximal extension. For other entropies, would the extension of them be unique as well?

Chapter 6
Miscellaneous

Appendix 6.A
Probability theory and Shannon entropy

We present necessary terminologies and theorems from probability theory. The weak law will be stated. The detailed study of these can be found on the standard probability theory textbooks, e.g. Durrett’s
Probability: Theory and Examples

[
70
]
and Billingsley’s
Probability and Measure

[
71
]
.

A framework of probability theory begins with probability space, which is a measure space.

Definition 6.A.1
.

A probability space is a triplet
\pqty{\Omega, \cal{F}, P}
of a set sample space
\Omega
with its
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{F}
and the probability measure
P:\mathcal{F}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

A
\sigma
-algebra is a collection of sets that close under complement and countable unions. Elements of the
\sigma
-algebra are called
events
. A probability measure
P:\mathcal{F}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
is a measure, which satisfies

1.

for any
A\in\mathcal{F}
, we have
P(A)>P(\emptyset)=0
, and

2.

for any countable collections of disjoint sets
\quantity{A_i : i \in\bb{N}}\subseteq\mathcal{F}
, we have that

P\pqty{\bigcup_{i\in\bb{N} A_i}}=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}P(A_{i})

Central to probability theory is a notion of random variable, which actually is a function rather than a variable.

Definition 6.A.2
.

Given a probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},P)
. A function
X:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
is a
random variable
if
X^{-1}(\mathcal{B}(A))\in\mathcal{F}
, where
A\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathbb{R})
. The measure-theoretic term is
X
is
P
-measurable.

Remark
.

\mathfrak{B}(\mathbb{R})
, Borel
\sigma
-algebra, is the smallest
\sigma
-algebra containing all open sets.

A random variable is commonly denoted with
X
,
Y
, and
Z
. In this thesis,
X
,
Y
,
Z
, and
W
denote random variables, labels of classical systems, and Hilbert spaces corresponding to the physical system of the same letter.

1
Law of large number and Shannon entropy

Laws of large number are main tools and central to the study of probability theory. The theorem lies on the notion of independence and the convergence in probability.

Definition 6.A.3

(Independence)
.

Events
A,B\in\mathcal{F}
are independent if
P(A\cap B)=P(A)P(B)
. Random variables
X,Y
are independent if
P(X\in C,Y\in D)=P(X\in C)P(Y\in D)
for any
C,D\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R})
.

Remark
.

P(X\in C)
is defined as the probability of an event
\quantity{\omega: X(\omega) \in C}
.

Definition 6.A.4

(Independent and identically distributed)
.

A sequence of random variables
\mathbf{X}=X_{1},X_{2},X_{3},\ldots
is independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.) if for all
X_{i}
are independent and they have the same distribution.

Definition 6.A.5
.

We say
X_{n}
converges to
X

in probability
and write
X_{n}\xrightarrow{P}X
if for any
\varepsilon>0
we have that

\lim_{n\to\infty}P(\absolutevalue{X_n - X}>\varepsilon)=0.

Remark
.

In measure theoretic terms, convergence in probability is a convergence in measure, where the measure is a probability measure.

Theorem 6.A.6

(The weak law of large number)
.

Suppose
X_{i}
—i.i.d. with
\mathbb{E}[X_{i}]=\mu
and
\mathbb{E}[\absolutevalue{X_i}]<\infty
. Then,
S_{n}/n\to\mu
in probability.

2
Typicality

We will present the Shannon’s theorem. The theorem concern an i.i.d. source
\mathbf{p}
which
n
random alphabets
{X_{i}:i\in[m]}
are drawn from. That is, for any
k\in[m]
,
X_{i}=k
with probability
p_{k}
. The theorem says that the probability of drawing the drawn sequence is equally likely, with a probability given by
2^{-H(\mathbf{p})}
.

A probability of an observed event of a random vector
\mathbf{X}(\omega)=\pqty{X_1 (\omega), X_2 (\omega), \ldots, X_n (\omega)}
, is

\pi(\omega)=\prod_{i\in[n]}p\pqty{X_i}

where
p\pqty{X_i}=p_{X_{i}(\omega)}
to simplify our expression. Note that a function
p\pqty{X_i}:\Omega\to[0,1]
and
\pi:\Omega\to[0,1]
are random variables.

Theorem 6.A.7

(Asymtotic equipartition property)
.

Suppose
X_{1},X_{2},\ldots
is a sequence of independently and identically distributed with distribution
\mathbf{p}=\pqty{p_1, p_2, \ldots, p_m}^{T}\in\mathrm{Prob}(m)
, then

\frac{1}{n}\log\pqty{\pi(\omega)}\xrightarrow{P}-\sum_{x\in[m]}p_{x}\log(p_x)

Proof.

Since
X_{i}
are independent, we have that

\displaystyle\log(\pi(\omega))

\displaystyle=\log\quantity(\prod_{i\in[n]} p(X_i))=\sum_{i\in[n]}\log(p(X_i)).

By the weak law of large number,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in[n]}\log(p(X_i))

\displaystyle=-\mathbb{E}\bqty{\log(p(X_1))}\quad\mathrm{in}~P

\displaystyle=-\sum_{x\in[m]}p_{x}\log(p_x)\quad\mathrm{in}~P.

∎

Later, the quantity
-\sum_{x\in[m]}p_{x}\log(p_x)
will be referred to as a Shannon entropy,

H(\mathbf{p})=-\sum_{x\in[n]}p_{x}\log(p_x).

That is with a long enough sequence, any sequences that occur, occur with probability

\pi(\omega)\approx 2^{-H(\mathbf{p})}.

We define an
\varepsilon
-
typical sequence
to be sequence such that its probability of occuring is
\varepsilon
-close to
2^{-H(\mathbf{p})}
.

Definition 6.A.8

(Typical sequences)
.

A sequence
\mathbf{x}\in[m]^{n}
of length
n
is
\varepsilon
-typical if

2^{-(H(\mathbf{p})+\varepsilon)}\leq P\pqty{\*{X} = \*{x}}\leq 2^{-(H(\mathbf{p})-\varepsilon)}.

A set of all
\varepsilon
-typical sequences of length
n
is denoted with
A^{(n)}_{\varepsilon}
.

One consequence of the asymptotic equipartition theorem is that as
n
get larger,
\mathbf{X}\in A^{(n)}_{\varepsilon}
with probability approaching
1
.

Theorem 6.A.9
.

Suppose
\mathbf{X}=X_{1},X_{2},\ldots
is a sequence of i.i.d. random variables. For any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
n>m
,

P(\mathbf{X}\in A^{(n)}_{\varepsilon})>1-\varepsilon
.

That is given we observe a long enough sequence of i.i.d. random variables, the sequence is typical with probability close to
1
.

Proof.

Recall that, a sequence
\mathbf{X}(\omega)
is in
A^{(n)}_{\zeta}
if
2^{-H(\mathbf{p})-\zeta}<\pi(\omega)<2^{-H(\mathbf{p})+\zeta}
. The inequality can be put equivalently as

2^{-H(\mathbf{p})}(2^{-\zeta}-1)\leq\pi(\omega)-2^{-H(\mathbf{p})}\leq 2^{-H(\mathbf{p})}(2^{\zeta}-1).

The set of elements
\omega\in\Omega
satisfying above expression is a superset of the set of
\omega
satisfying

\absolutevalue{\pi(\omega) - 2^{-H(\p)}}\leq 2^{-H(\mathbf{p})}(1-2^{-\zeta})=:\delta_{0}(\zeta),

because
0<1-2^{-\zeta}<2^{\zeta}-1
for all
\zeta>0
. By theorem
6.A.7
, a random variable
\pi(\omega)
converges to
2^{-H(\mathbf{p})}
in probability. That is, for any
\delta>0
and
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, there exists a natural number
m
such that for all
n>m
,

P\pqty{\abs{\pi(\omega) - 2^{-H(\p)}} < \delta}>1-\varepsilon.

Since
\delta_{0}(\varepsilon)>0
, we have that

P\quantity(\*{X}(\omega)\in A^{n} _\varepsilon)\geq P\pqty{\abs{\pi(\omega) - 2^{-H(\p)}} < \delta_0 (\varepsilon)}>1-\varepsilon.

∎

Appendix 6.B
Linear programming

One of the main techniques we exploit multiple times in this thesis is Linear programming. Here,we provide a succinct introduction to linear programming, its dual, weak duality, the Farkas lemma, and the strong duality.

Definition 6.B.1
.

A linear program is an optimization problem of the form

max

\displaystyle\mathbf{c}\cdot\mathbf{x}

subject to

\displaystyle A\mathbf{x}\leq\mathbf{b}

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}\geq 0

where
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},

Definition 6.B.2
.

Given a linear program, its feasibility set
\mathsf{F}
is a set of all
\mathbf{x}
that satisfy the constraints. A linear program with non-empty feasibility set is said to be
feasible
.

Definition 6.B.3
.

A dual of a linear program is a minimization problem of the form

min

\displaystyle\mathbf{b}\cdot\mathbf{y}

subject to

\displaystyle\mathbf{y}^{T}A\geq\mathbf{c}^{T}

\displaystyle\mathbf{y}\geq 0

The dual of a linear program is simply referred to as the dual program while its original program is called the primal program.

Theorem 6.B.4

(Weak duality)
.

The optimal value for the primal program is no greater than the optimal value of the dual program.

Proof.

Suppose that the feasibility set of the primal (
\mathsf{F}_{P}
) and of the dual
\mathsf{F}_{D}
are not empty. Suppose that
\mathbf{x}\in\mathsf{F}_{P}
and
\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{F}_{D}
. We have that
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\geq 0
,
\mathbf{b}\geq A\mathbf{x}
, and
\mathbf{y}^{T}A\geq\mathbf{c}
. We have that

\displaystyle\mathbf{y}\cdot\mathbf{b}\geq\mathbf{y}\cdot A\mathbf{x}\geq\mathbf{c}\cdot\mathbf{x},

showing the desired inequality.
∎

Theorem 6.B.5

(Hyperplane separating theorem for a conic hull and a point)
.

Suppose that
K\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
is a conic hull of
\quantity{\*{a}_1, \*{a}_2, \ldots, \*{a}_n: \*{a}_i \in\bb{R}^m}
and
\mathbf{b}
is a point. The point
\mathbf{b}
is not in the cone
K
if and only if there exists a plane described by
h(\mathbf{x})=\mathbf{n}\cdot\mathbf{x}=0
separating
K
and
\mathbf{b}
, i.e

\mathbf{n}\cdot\mathbf{b}<0\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\mathbf{n}\cdot\mathbf{a}_{i}\geq 0~\forall i\in[n].

Proof.

Suppose
\mathbf{b}
is not in the cone
K
. We define
d
to be a distance between the cone and the point, that is

d=\inf\quantity{ \norm{\*{b}-\*{k}} ~\vert~ \*{k} \in K}.

Consider a closed ball
{B}
of radius
\norm{\*{b}}
centered at
\mathbf{b}
. This ball has non-empty intersection with
K
, at least it contains the origin. Any points
k
outside this ball is further away from
\mathbf{b}
than the points in the intersection. That is
d=\min\quantity{ \norm{\*{b}-\*{k}} ~\vert~ \*{k} \in K\cap{B}}
the replacement of infimum with a minimum is due to existence of
\mathbf{k}
yielding infimum value in
K\cap B\subset K
.

Suppose
\mathbf{k}_{0}
is a point in
K
such that
d=\norm{\*{b} - \knot}
. Define
\mathbf{n}^{\prime}=\mathbf{b}-\mathbf{k}_{0}
. We will show first that any
\mathbf{k}\in K
satisfies
(\mathbf{b}-\mathbf{k}_{0})\cdot\mathbf{k}\geq 0
. Since
\mathbf{b}-\mathbf{k}_{0}
is perpendicular to
\mathbf{k}_{0}
, we have that
(\mathbf{b}-\mathbf{k}_{0})\cdot\mathbf{k}=(\mathbf{b}-\mathbf{k}_{0})\cdot\pqty{\*{k} - \knot}
. This gives us a nice geometric visualization (see fig
1
).

\mathbf{k}
\mathbf{b}
\mathbf{k}_{0}

\theta

Figure 1
:
A geometric visualization of
\mathbf{b}-\mathbf{k}_{0}
and
\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}_{0}
.

Suppose on contrary that
(\mathbf{b}-\mathbf{k}_{0})\cdot\pqty{\*{k} - \knot}>0
. This leads to
\norm{\*{b} - \knot}\times\norm{\*{k} - \knot}\times\cos\theta>0
, where
\theta
is an angle between the two vectors. That is the point
\mathbf{k}
is strictly closer to
\mathbf{b}
than
\mathbf{k}_{0}
. Therefore,
(\mathbf{b}-\mathbf{k}_{0})\cdot\pqty{\*{k} - \knot}\leq 0
.

For the inequality with the point
\mathbf{b}
, consider
(\mathbf{b}-\mathbf{k}_{0})\cdot\mathbf{b}=(\mathbf{b}-\mathbf{k})\cdot(\mathbf{b}-\mathbf{k}_{0})=\norm{\*{b} - \knot}^{2}>0
, due to
\mathbf{b}\neq\mathbf{k}_{0}
.
To get the direction of inequalities in the theorem statement, we define
\mathbf{n}=-\mathbf{n}^{\prime}
.
∎

Corollary 6.B.6

(Geometric Farkas lemma)
.

Suppose
\mathbf{a}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{n},\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
. Exactly one of the following is true:

1.

A vector
\mathbf{b}
is in a conic hull of
\quantity{\*{a}_1, \ldots, \*{a}_n}
.

2.

There is
\mathbf{n}
such that
\mathbf{n}\cdot\mathbf{a}_{i}\geq 0
and
\mathbf{n}\cdot\mathbf{b}<0
for all
i\in[n]
.

Proof.

Notice that
\mathbf{b}
is either inside or outside the cone. It is enough to show that
2
is equivalent with
\mathbf{b}
being outside the cone. Denote the cone by
K
. Suppose that
\mathbf{b}
is outside
K
, then there exists a hyperplane separating
\mathbf{b}
and the cone
K
. The normal vector
\mathbf{n}
is then given by the plane. Conversely, if there is a plane separating between the point and the cone, then they are disjoint.
∎

Theorem 6.B.7

(Farkas lemma)
.

Suppose
A\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
,
\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
, and
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. The feasibility set
\quantity{ \*{x} ~\vert~ A\*{x} = \*{b}, \*{x}\geq 0}
is non-empty if and only if for all
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
, we have that
\mathbf{y}^{T}A\geq 0^{T}
implies
\mathbf{y}^{T}\mathbf{b}\geq 0
.

Proof.

We denote columns of
A
by
A=(\mathbf{a}_{1},\mathbf{a}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{n})
. The non-emptiness of the feasibility set is equivalent to

\sum_{i\in[n]}x_{i}\mathbf{a}_{i}=\mathbf{b}\quad\mathrm{and}\quad x_{i}\geq 0\quad\textrm{for any }i\in[n].

This show that the feasibility set is not empty if and only if
\mathbf{b}
is in the conic hull of
\quantity{\*{a}_1,\ldots, \*{a}_n}
.

The point
\mathbf{b}
is outside the cone if and only if there exists
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
such that
\mathbf{y}\cdot\mathbf{a}_{i}\geq 0
for any
i\in[n]
and
\mathbf{y}\cdot\mathbf{b}<0
. The point
\mathbf{b}
being inside the cone is equivalent with the statement:

for any
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
, the statement
\mathbf{y}\cdot\mathbf{a}_{i}\geq 0
for all
i\in[n]
is not true or
\mathbf{y}\cdot\mathbf{b}\geq 0
.

We turn this or-statement into a conditional, we have the statement:

for any
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
,
\mathbf{y}\cdot\mathbf{a}_{i}\geq 0
for all
i\in[n]
implies
\mathbf{y}\cdot\mathbf{b}\geq 0
.

Compactly, for all
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
, we have that
\mathbf{y}^{T}A\geq 0^{T}
implies
\mathbf{y}^{T}\mathbf{b}\geq 0
.
∎

This leads to the variation of Farkas lemma which we use throughout the thesis.

Farkas lemma with inequality

Corollary 6.B.8
.

Using a notation from previous theorem, we have that a feasibility set

\quantity{\*{x} ~\vert~ A\*{x} \geq\*{b} ~\text{and}~ \*{x} \geq 0}

is non-empty if and only if for all
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}
, we have
\mathbf{y}^{T}A\geq 0^{T}
implies
\mathbf{y}^{T}\mathbf{b}\geq 0
.

Proof.

Define
A^{\prime}=\pqty{A ~\vert~ I_m}
. There is a non-negative solution of
\mathbf{x}
to
A\mathbf{x}\leq\mathbf{b}
if and only if there is a non-negative solution
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
to
A^{\prime}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}=\mathbf{b}
. From the Farkas lemma, this is equivalent to
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}
, we have
\mathbf{y}^{T}A^{\prime}\geq 0^{T}
implies
\mathbf{y}^{T}\mathbf{b}\geq 0
. The expression
\mathbf{y}^{T}A^{\prime}\geq 0
can be stated with
\mathbf{y}^{T}A\geq 0
and
\mathbf{y}\geq 0
.
∎

Now, we can prove the strong duality result for linear programming.

Strong duality

Theorem 6.B.9
.

If a primal linear program is feasible, the optimal values for the primal and the dual programs are equal.

Proof.

Suppose the primal linear program and the optimal value are
p^{\star}=\min\quantity{\*{x}~\vert~A\*{x} \leq\*{b}, \*{x} \geq 0}
. The dual of this program is

min

\displaystyle\mathbf{y}\cdot\mathbf{b}

subject to

\displaystyle\mathbf{y}^{T}A\geq\mathbf{c}

\displaystyle\mathbf{y}\geq 0.

We define an augmented matrix, and a constraint vector

A^{\prime}=\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}\quad\mathbf{b}_{\varepsilon}=\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}.

Define a feasibility set by
A^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{b}_{\varepsilon}
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{F}_{\varepsilon}=\quantity{\*{x} ~\vert~ A' \*{x} \leq\*{b}, ~\*{x} \geq 0}.

The condition
A^{\prime}\mathbf{x}\leq\mathbf{b}
is equivalent with
A\mathbf{x}\leq\mathbf{b}
and
\mathbf{c}\cdot\mathbf{x}\geq p^{\star}+\varepsilon
. Since
p^{\star}
is the optimal value, the feasibility set
\mathsf{F}_{\varepsilon}
is empty unless
\varepsilon=0
.

Consider the case where
\varepsilon>0
. By Farkas lemma, the feasibility set
\mathsf{F}_{\varepsilon}
is empty if and only if there exists
\mathbf{y}=\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}
where
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}
and
z\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that
\mathbf{y}^{T}A^{\prime}\geq\mathbf{0}^{T}
and
\mathbf{y}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{\varepsilon}<0
. Notice that the condition
\mathbf{y}^{T}A^{\prime}\geq\mathbf{0}^{T}
is independent of the value of
\varepsilon
. Equivalently,

\mathbf{y}^{T}A^{\prime}\geq\mathbf{0}^{T}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{b}<z(p^{\star}+\varepsilon).

This give non-emptiness of a feasibility set

\mathsf{F}^{\prime}_{\varepsilon,z}=\quantity{\*{u} ~\vert~ \*{u}^T A \geq z\*{c}^T ,~ \*{u}\cdot\*{b} \leq\pstar+ \varepsilon, ~\*{u} \geq 0}\neq\emptyset

For
\varepsilon=0
, the feasibility set is not empty. In the case of
\varepsilon>0
, there is such
\mathbf{y}
that make
\mathbf{y}^{T}A^{\prime}\geq\mathbf{0}^{T}
, then the vector
\mathbf{y}
must satisfy

\displaystyle\mathbf{y}\cdot\mathbf{b}=\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{b}-z(p^{\star}+\varepsilon)\geq 0.

Equivalently,
\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{b}\geq z(p^{\star}+\varepsilon)
. If
z=0
,
\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{b}\geq 0
, but from the case of
\varepsilon>0
,
\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{b}<0
. Therefore, the element
{z}>0
must be strictly positive. Since the set
\mathsf{F}^{\prime}_{\varepsilon,z}
is non-empty for all
\varepsilon>0
and
z>0
, a feasibility set

\mathsf{G}_{\varepsilon}=\quantity{\*{v} ~\vert~ \*{v}^T A \geq\*{c},~ \*{v}\cdot\*{b} \leq\pstar+ \varepsilon, ~\*{v} \geq 0}

is non-empty for any
\varepsilon>0
. Because of
\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{b}\leq p^{\star}+\varepsilon
for all
\varepsilon>0
, we have that
\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{b}\leq p^{\star}
and consequently that the dual problem is feasible with optimal value at most
p^{\star}
, that is
d^{\star}\leq p^{\star}
. From the weak duality, we have
d^{\star}\geq p^{\star}
. Therefore,
d^{\star}=p^{\star}
showing the strong duality.
∎
